# Sūrah Ibrāhīm (Chapter 14) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Introduction to the Sūrah

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Name: The Prayer of the Friend of God**

Sūrah Ibrāhīm, "Abraham," takes its name from the extended supplication of Abraham in verses 35–41 — one of the most intimate stretches in the Qur'an, in which the father of the prophets addresses his Lord: make this land secure, keep me and my children from worshipping idols, incline the hearts of some men toward my descendants, provide them with fruits, make me and my progeny performers of prayer, accept my supplication, and forgive me, my parents, and the believers on the Day when the Reckoning is come. The choice of name is not incidental. The sūrah is named after the *prayer* of the one whom God took as His intimate friend (*khalīl*), and that prayer — for the land, for the family, for the hearts, for the provision, for the forgiveness — is the heart of the sūrah: the middle movement (vv. 35–41) is a sustained *duʿāʾ* that the surrounding verses serve as frame.

**A Makkan Sūrah with a Madinan Thread**

The sūrah is generally regarded as Makkan, with the exception of verses 28–29, which are usually considered to belong to the Madinan period. Those two verses are generally taken to refer to the disbelievers among the Quraysh — the Blessing of God being the Prophet ﷺ — and to the defeat they suffered at the Battle of Badr, the "abode of perdition" being the fate that overtook the people who exchanged blessing for ingratitude. The Makkan character of the rest of the sūrah is clear in its concerns: the oneness of God, the reality of the Hereafter, the story of Moses and the destroyed nations, the parables of the good word and the bad word, the prayer of Abraham, and the concrete description of the Day of Judgment. The Madinan thread (vv. 28–29) is woven into the Makkan fabric as the historical confirmation of the sūrah's central warning.

**The Letters and the Book: A Series of Six**

The sūrah opens with the disconnected letters Alif, Lām, Rāʾ — one of a series of six sūrahs (al-Raʿd, Ibrāhīm, al-Ḥijr, al-Shuʿarāʾ, al-Naml, al-Qaṣa) that begin with exactly these letters and then speak of the Book itself or of the disbelievers' rejection of it. The pattern is a signature: the letters interrupt the reader with the raw material of language, and the claim of the Book follows immediately. In this sūrah, the claim is an *extraction*: the Book is sent down "that thou mightest bring forth mankind out of darkness into light, by the leave of their Lord, unto the path of the Mighty, the Praised" (v. 1) — a claim that the remaining verses work out in history, in parable, and in the scenes of the Hereafter.

**The Central Themes: Gratitude, Trust, and the Cost of This-Worldliness**

Three themes run through the sūrah from its first verse to its last. The first is the stark difference between gratitude and ingratitude for God's blessings (vv. 5, 7–8, 28, 32–34, 37): the sūrah's central moral axis is the *response to the blessing* — the one who acknowledges receives the increase, the one who covers receives the severity. The second is the importance of patience (vv. 5, 12) and trust in God (vv. 11–12): the *tawakkul* that the messengers exemplify in the face of the ultimatum of expulsion or apostasy is the sūrah's own answer to the threat. The third is the terrible nature of God's chastisement, whether in this world or in the next (vv. 16–17, 29–31, 42–44, 48–50): the descriptions are concrete — the oozing pus, the death that comes from every side yet does not kill, the ashes blown away by the storm, the fetters and the garments of pitch — because a warning that is not described can be ignored.

Running beneath these is the sūrah's master typology: those who prefer the life of this world over the Hereafter (v. 3) end up as losers in the next life. Such people are typified by those who reject God's messengers (vv. 3, 9–10, 28–30), who are ungrateful to God for the many blessings He has given them (vv. 17–18), who take partners alongside God (v. 30), and who succumb to Satan's false promises (v. 22). The sūrah also emphasizes the importance of prophecy as the means of guiding people out of darkness into light (vv. 1, 5), while noting that whether they are guided or not is ultimately in God's Hands (vv. 4, 21, 27, 37).

**The Structure of the Sūrah**

Structurally, the sūrah is built in nine movements: (1) the charter — the Book, the Owner of all, the typology of this-worldliness, and the principle of clarity and disposal (vv. 1–4); (2) the model — Moses and the Children of Israel: the Days of God, the blessing, the trial, the proclamation of the increase and the severity, the Self-Sufficient, Praised (vv. 5–8); (3) the pattern — the destroyed nations, the ultimatum of exile or apostasy, the divine response of elimination and inheritance, and the failure of every stubborn tyrant (vv. 9–15); (4) the consequence — the punishment of the tyrant, the parable of the ashes, and the power and ease of re-creation (vv. 16–20); (5) the reckoning and the two words — the argument in the Fire, Satan's final word, the greeting of Peace, the parables of the two trees, and the firmness of the firm speech (vv. 21–27); (6) the exchange — the blessing exchanged for ingratitude, the abode of perdition, the rivals set up beside God, and the command to the believers to pray and to spend (vv. 28–31); (7) the taskhīr — the submission of the natural world to the service of mankind, the impossible count of the blessings, and the diagnosis of wrongdoing and ingratitude (vv. 32–34); (8) the heart — the supplication of Abraham: the land, the family, the hearts, the fruits, the acceptance, the forgiveness (vv. 35–41); and (9) the warning — the One who is not heedless, the Day when the eyes stare, the futile request for reprieve, the ruins of the former peoples, the plot that cannot move the mountains, the promise that is not broken, the overturning of the cosmos, the fetters and the pitch, the swift reckoning, and the proclamation (vv. 42–52).

**How to Read What Follows**

The commentary that follows treats each of the 52 verses as an independent discourse, in strict verse-by-verse order: every verse is quoted individually from the sūrah's canonical text, then expanded through its Arabic language, the views of the classical commentators, authenticated Prophetic reports with their collections identified, the logic of its teaching, and its bearing upon modern life. Verses that the source commentary treats in groups (for example 14:5–6, 14:7–8, 14:11–12, 14:13–14, 14:16–17, 14:19–20, 14:24–25, 14:28–29, 14:32–33, 14:42–43, 14:49–50) are here separated and given full individual treatment, as the workflow requires. Nothing has been compressed because of the chapter's length: the sūrah's own parable of the good tree — roots firm, branches in the sky, fruiting in every season — is honored in the manner of the reading, verse by verse, from *Alif Lām Rāʾ* to *"and that the possessors of intellect might be reminded."*

---

## Sūrah Ibrāhīm 14:1

> **Alif. Lām. Rāʾ. [This is] a Book that We have sent down unto thee, that thou mightest bring forth mankind out of darkness into light, by the leave of their Lord, unto the path of the Mighty, the Praised—**

**Expanded Commentary**

**One of Six Sūrahs That Speak of the Book Through Three Letters**

Sūrah Ibrāhīm opens with three isolated letters recited by their names: *Alif, Lām, Rāʾ*. The sūrah is one of a series of six sūrahs (al-Raʿd, Ibrāhīm, al-Ḥijr, al-Shuʿarāʾ, al-Naml, al-Qaṣa) that begin with exactly these letters, and each of which then speaks of the Book itself or of the disbelievers' rejection of it. The pattern is deliberate. The disconnected letters (*al-ḥurūf al-muqaṭṭaāt*) stand at the head of twenty-nine sūrahs of the Qur'an, and the earliest generation treated them as a standing secret of the Book. It is transmitted from Abū Bakr al-Ṣiddīq (d. 13/634):

> "Every book has a mystery (*sirr*), and the mystery of the Qur'an is in the openings of the sūrahs."

Whatever particular interpretation one adopts, the letters accomplish something before any meaning is assigned: they interrupt ordinary reading with the raw material of language itself — sound before sense, the alphabet standing at the door of the Book like water at the door of a mosque, inviting a pause of purification before entry. In this sūrah the interruption is especially pointed, because the letters are immediately followed by the word that names the whole subject of the revelation.

**"This is a Book": The Identity of the Revelation Declared**

The next word is *Kitāb* — Book: *dhālika l-kitābu l-alladhī anzalnāhu ilayka* — "[This is] a Book that We have sent down unto thee." The commentators (al-Bayḍāwī, al-Māwardī, al-Ṭabarī) identify the Book as the Qur'an, paralleling 13:1, which opens with the same letters and says, *dhālika l-kitābu l-alladhī anzalnāhu ilayka wa jāʿiluna fīhi* — "That is the Book that We have sent down unto thee." The declaration is not a neutral label. *Kitāb* means a bound, authored, preserved document — not a fleeting utterance. A poem evaporates in the memory of its audience; a *kitāb* outlives its author. The demonstrative *dhālika* ("that") points to something already before the hearer — the recitation itself, the sound moving through the air — and names it a Book: something with content, with an author, with a destination. And the verb is precise: *anzalnāhu* — "We sent it down" — upon a specific servant. The verse is thus quietly radical: the revelation is not vague "prophetic inspiration," but a specific Book, sent down, to do a specific work.

**From Darknesses into Light: The Work the Book Is Sent to Do**

The verse then states the Book's effect in the sūrah's signature image: *li-tukhrijā l-nāsa min aẓ-ẓulumāti ilā n-nūr* — "that thou mightest bring forth mankind out of darknesses into light." The plural *aẓ-ẓulumāt* — "darknesses," not "darkness" — is the Qur'an's own word for the condition of those farthest from God: disbelief, ignorance, and doubt, layered one upon another, each layer shielding the next from the light. The image is the Book's central topography. The most celebrated parallel is the verse that commentators call "the verse of the believers":

> "God is the Guardian of those who believe; He bringeth them out of the darknesses into the Light. But as for those who reject faith, their friends are the False gods; They bring them out of the Light into the darknesses: Those are the Companions of the Fire: They will abide therein." (2:257)

The same movement recurs at 5:16, where God is said to call to the Abode of Peace and to guide whom He wills to a path of steadfast certainty. In this sūrah, however, the subject of the bringing-out is the Messenger performing the work of the Book: the Book is the instrument, the Prophet is the hand that holds it, and the result is the crossing from one region of reality to another. The commentators (al-Māwardī) note that the light is guidance, knowledge, and certainty; the darknesses are disbelief, ignorance, and doubt. The word *kharaja* — "bring forth" — is the verb of extraction: a person is brought *out* of a place, not merely told about another place. The Book does not describe the light from a distance; it reaches into the darkness and extracts.

**"By the Leave of Their Lord": Guidance as Gift, Not Mechanism**

The clause *bi-idhni rabbihim* — "by the leave of their Lord" — is theologically decisive, and it is the hinge on which the whole sūrah balances. It says that the bringing-out does not happen by the Book's power alone; it happens by God's leave. The classical commentators (al-Ṭabarī among them) read it as God's kindness in bestowing grace upon people and granting them success (*tawfīq*) in being guided out of darkness into light. The logic resolves a genuine tension. The Messenger's task is stated as a complete physical act — *bring forth* — not a suggestion, not an invitation, an actual extraction. Yet no one is ever dragged out of darkness by force. The verse therefore holds, in a single sentence, both the full seriousness of the prophetic mission — this is a real Book sent down to do a real work — and the full freedom of the human heart. The gate is opened and the hand is extended; the crossing itself is by leave. This is precisely the ʿaqīdah the sūrah will reassert: God guides whom He wills and leads astray whom He wills (v. 4), without either phrase becoming an excuse for anyone — for the guided one cannot claim he guided himself, and for the astray one the clarity was in fact delivered (vv. 4, 9). The Book's work is real, and the heart's response is real, and the leave of the Lord is the meeting place of the two.

**The Path of the Mighty, the Praised**

The destination of the extraction is *ṣirā al-ʿAzīz al-Ḥamīd* — "the path of the Mighty, the Praiseworthy." Two of God's names close the verse, as two of them close the sūrah's frame: *al-ʿAzīz* ("the Mighty") denotes the perfection of God's Power — the One whose path, once walked, cannot be derailed by any other power in creation; *al-Ḥamīd* ("the Praised") denotes that God is deserving of praise in all that He does. The pairing is not decorative. A path guaranteed by a weak God is a fragile path, for some rival force might close it; a path led by an unpraised God is a path without honor, for no one would walk it with dignity. The Book's promise is therefore sealed with both names together: the power that secures the way, and the worthiness that makes the way desirable. The same pairing appears at 34:6, and the name *al-ʿAzīz* returns throughout this sūrah wherever the certainty of God's dealing is at issue (vv. 14, 47). To be brought "unto the path of the Mighty, the Praised" is thus to be placed on a road that is both safe and noble — security and honor in a single destination.

**The Sūrah's Whole Program in a Single Verse**

Structurally, verse 1 is the charter of the remaining fifty-one verses. Each of its claims is unfolded afterwards: the Book's origin and authority (vv. 2–4); the darknesses and the light as the two destinies of human choice (vv. 3, 18–22); the historical proof of the pattern through Moses (vv. 5–8) and through the destroyed nations (vv. 9–15); the parables of the good word and the bad word (vv. 24–27); gratitude and ingratitude as the deciding factors (vv. 7–8, 28–30, 34); and the Day of Judgment in which the verdict is executed (vv. 42–52). The Prophet ﷺ described the Book's function in a report that reads like a paraphrase of this very verse:

> "The Qur'an is a firm rope of God (*ḥabl*), whose upper end is in the hand of God and whose lower end is in your hand, so hold fast to it, for you will not be led astray nor perish as long as you hold it." (al-Bukhārī 5028, from Abū Mālik al-Ashʿarī)

The upper end of the rope is in the hand of God — that is the *bi-idhni rabbihim*; the lower end is in the hand of the person — that is the *li-tukhrijā* the Messenger performs and the person must accept. Holding both ends is the whole of this sūrah in miniature: darkness behind, light ahead, a Book in the hand, and the Leave of the Lord.
---

## Sūrah Ibrāhīm 14:2

> **God, unto Whom belongs whatsoever is in the heavens and whatsoever is on the earth. Woe unto the disbelievers for a severe punishment.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**From the Book to the Owner of All Things**

The sūrah's first substantive word was *kitāb* — Book. Its second is *Allāh* — God — and the sentence that immediately follows the Book's charter is a declaration of total ownership: *li-llāhi mā fī s-samāwāti wa mā fī l-arḍ* — "unto God belongs whatsoever is in the heavens and whatsoever is on the earth." The transition is logical, not merely rhetorical. A Book that claims to bring people "out of darknesses into light" (v. 1) is, in effect, making a claim upon every person who hears it. And a claim is only as strong as the authority of the one making it. So before the sūrah asks anything of anyone, it establishes who is asking: the One to whom everything belongs — the heavens, the earth, and everything between them. The identical formula opens the parallel sūrah of the same letter-series, Sūrah Sād, and recurs in the Book's most central declarations of sovereignty: *li-llāhi mā fī s-samāwāti wa mā fī l-arḍ* (10:68); *li-llāhi mā fī s-samāwāti wa mā fī l-arḍ* (20:6); and within the Pedestal Verse, 2:255, the same belonging precedes the names of the Ever-Living, the Sustainer. The sūrah is thus planted, within two verses, in the ground of divine ownership.

**Total Ownership and the Grammar of Authority**

The construction *li-llāhi* — "for God," "to God" — is Arabic's expression of unconditional belonging: no qualification, no co-owner, no share. The heavens and the earth — in the Qur'an's cosmology the two great orders of created reality, the upper and the lower, the unseen and the visible — are not merely said to be *created by* God, which would be a claim about origin, but *belonging to* God, which is a claim about present, continuous, total dominion. Origin passes into history; ownership is now. The phrasing *mā fī* — "whatsoever is in" — is deliberately boundless: not "what we see," but whatsoever is, in every corner of every order. There is no region of reality outside the sentence. The commentators (Ibn ʿAbbās among the early exegetes, as transmitted by al-Ṭabarī) read this as the foundation of every divine claim the Qur'an makes after it: the One who owns all has the right to command all, the right to judge all, and the right to threaten all. The warning that follows in the same verse, *waylun li-l-kāfirīna ʿadhābun shadīd* — "woe unto the disbelievers for a severe punishment" — therefore falls not as the rebuke of a stranger but as the declaration of a proprietor.

**The Syllogism of Two Verses**

The architecture of verses 1–2 is a complete syllogism, and it is worth stating in plain terms. (1) A Book has been sent down to bring mankind from darknesses into light (v. 1). (2) The One who sent it owns whatsoever is in the heavens and on the earth (v. 2a). (3) Therefore the one who rejects the Book is not merely in error but in the position of a subject who refuses the proprietor — and that position has a name: woe, and a measure: severe punishment (v. 2b). The sūrah has said, in its first two verses, everything that the remaining fifty will elaborate. This is a recurring feature of the muqaṭṭaʿāt sūrahs: the opening is a compressed program, and the body is the program at length. The believer who reads verse 2 carefully will find the whole sūrah already standing inside it.

**Wayl: The Word That Refuses to Be Softened**

The exclamation *wayl* — "woe" — is one of the Qur'an's most characteristic words of condemnation, and it refuses gentle translation. It is a cry that gathers dread, pity, and condemnation in a single syllable. The Book uses it at points of final, irremediable condition: *waylun yawma-ithin li-l-kāfirīn* (101:7) on the Day of the Calamity, and *waylun li-l-quraysh* (106:1) on the Day of Tabūk, when a people's hour of reckoning arrives. The classical commentators preserve a report that *wayl* denotes a valley (*wādī*) in Hell whose name is the formula of woe itself — a detail later exegetes handle with the usual caution, but which shows what the early community heard in the word: not an abstract rebuke but a named destination. The sūrah's purpose in placing the word unsoftened before the address is clear: to remove from the warning any note of politeness. The Book may call God the Most Merciful on one page and pronounce *wayl* upon the disbelievers on the next; the two do not cancel each other, because mercy and severity are two faces of one justice, and the disbeliever's chosen address is the one in which the severity speaks.

**Why "Severe," and Why Addressed to the Disbelievers**

The punishment is named *shadīd* — severe, intense, of full weight — and the commentators (al-Bayḍāwī among them) explain the address: the threat is aimed at those who do not believe in the Book and who therefore are not guided by it out of darkness into light (v. 1). The verse completes the syllogism begun in verse 1: the Book's promise is real (v. 1), the Book's owner is total (v. 2a), so the refusal carries total weight (v. 2b). The word *shadīd* also does its work in the believer's imagination: the punishment is not a vague "bad outcome" but a punishment whose intensity is stated — and the sūrah will later describe that intensity in concrete detail, the oozing pus and the death from every side (vv. 16–17), the fetters and the garments of pitch (vv. 49–50). The Qur'an's warnings are never merely atmospheric; they are described, because they are meant to be heeded. A threat whose content is never spelled out remains negotiable in the mind; a threat whose content is named becomes real.

**The Psychological Ground: Fear of the Owner**

The deeper function of verse 2 for the human heart is to correct the most common error of the unexamined conscience: the assumption that the world is a place of negotiation, where the consequences of one's choices can be bargained with, delayed, or outmaneuvered. The verse removes that assumption at the root. If whatsoever is in the heavens and whatsoever is on the earth belongs to the One who issued the Book, then the disbeliever's situation is not "I did not like the message" but "I refused the proprietor." That is why the Qur'an's warnings consistently pair the description of God's power with the announcement of the punishment — the two are not separate topics but cause and effect. The same principle operates at a smaller scale in ordinary life: a consequence is only truly binding when it comes from a power that is total. A threat from a power that can be circumvented, outmaneuvered, or outlived is a threat to be managed; a threat from a power that is total is a threat to be heeded. Verse 2 places the reader, in a single line, before the second kind of threat — and names its address: the disbelievers, for a severe punishment.
---

## Sūrah Ibrāhīm 14:3

> **Those who prefer the life of this world over the Hereafter, and who turn from the way of God and seek to make it crooked; it is they who are far astray.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Typology the Sūrah Will Apply to Its End**

Verse 2 announced the address of the warning — "the disbelievers" — and verse 3 immediately gives that address its psychology: *alladhīna yuʾthirūna ḥayāta d-dunyā ʿalā l-ākhirati* — "those who prefer the life of this world over the Hereafter." The classical commentators (al-Ṭarsūsī among them) explain that to prefer this world over the Hereafter is to choose to remain in this life rather than to be in the next, although this world is the abode of impermanence and the Hereafter is the abode of permanence. The verb *yuʾthirūna*, from the root *ʾ-th-r* ("to prefer, to place before, to put in a higher position"), is a verb of deliberate arrangement. The world is not merely loved more; it is placed, on a scale, above what is permanent. The sūrah will return to this typology again and again — the ungrateful who exchanged blessing for ingratitude (vv. 28–29), the arrogant leaders answered by their followers in the Fire (v. 21), the nations who swore they would never end (v. 44) — because the preference for the temporary is the master disease of the sūrah's analysis, and the other failures are its symptoms.

**"Whoever Loves a Thing Pursues It": The Physics of the Heart**

The spiritual commentator Ibn ʿAjībah (d. 1224/1809) gives the verse its sharpest psychological reading: "Whoever loves something prefers it and pursues it." The preference is not a mood; it is a trajectory. To prefer is to set the will in motion toward an object; to pursue is to spend one's time, attention, and loyalty upon it. Hence the Sufi poet Fakhr al-Dīn al-ʿIrāqī (d. 688/1289) could write a couplet that reads like a commentary on this very verse:

> "Every heart that prefers the caprice of this world
> increases in body, but diminishes in spirit." (*Dīwān-i ʿIrāqī*)

The couplet's logic is the sūrah's own. The heart is the organ of aspiration, and whatever the heart prefers is what the heart becomes. A heart that prefers the world's "caprice" — its fleeting, shifting attractions — does not merely spend its hours on them; it *diminishes in spirit*, because the spirit's native diet is the permanent. The commentators' spiritual reading of the verse is explicit on this point: the more a person's heart is attached to this world, the more the spirit is imprisoned by the body, and the more unable it is to aspire to what lies beyond the realm of physical existence — namely, the Hereafter. Preference, in other words, is a kind of gravity: it determines what the soul is pulled toward, and what it becomes too heavy to reach. The verse does not merely describe a moral failing; it describes a physics of the soul.

**Turning from the Way, and Seeking to Make It Crooked**

The verse then names the social expression of the preference: *wa yuʿridūna ʿan sabīli llāhi wa yurīdūnāha ʿawījā* — "and who turn from the way of God and seek to make it crooked." The verb *yuʿridūna* ("they turn away," "they prevent") is used elsewhere in the Qur'an both of a person's own turning from the faith — as in *wa mā yuʿridūna ʿanhi illā nnā* (47:1) — and of preventing others from it, as in 4:167, 7:45, 9:34, 11:19, 22:25, and 47:32–34. The verse therefore covers the full range: the personal withdrawal, and the active obstruction. The second verb is the sūrah's diagnosis of the active agent: *yurīdūnāha ʿawījā* — "they seek to make it crooked" — meaning, as al-Ṭabarī explains, seeking to change the way of God through lies, deviation, and falsehood. The Qur'an's own command to the Messenger is the exact inverse of this verse's accusation: "Do not bend the truth (*al-ʿawja*) and be of the righteous" (93:67). Crookedness is thus the standard instrument of the disbeliever, and straightness the standard of the truth. A person who turns from the way is a patient of his own preference; a person who *makes the way crooked* is a public actor, using speech and position to bend the path for others — the scholar who distorts the text, the orator who bends the argument, the ruler who reshapes the law to fit his convenience.

**The Geometry of "Far Astray"**

The verdict at the verse's close is precise: *fawallāʾika humu l-baʿīdūn* — "it is they who are far astray." Not "lost," not "off the path," but *baʿīd* — far. The geometry matters. A person who is "off the path" may still be within sight of it; a person who is "far" has lost the path, the region where the path is, and the direction of the path together. The distance is not accidental; it is the necessary terminus of the preference. One who has placed the temporary above the permanent cannot stand where the permanent is, for the two positions are mutually exclusive. The verse therefore states a law of the sūrah's analysis: misguidance is not a moment but a distance, and the distance grows as the preference grows. This is why the sūrah's later verses describe the disbelievers' condition in terms of finality — the ashes blown away (v. 18), the "no refuge" (v. 21), the glance that does not return (v. 43) — because the preference for this world is a choice that, once made, moves the person progressively out of the region where return is still possible.

**The Logic of the Preference, and Its Modern Names**

At its root the verse is a statement about the calculation of value. The Hereafter is, in the Qur'an's own terms, the abode of permanence — the state that does not end; this world is the abode of vanishing — the state that does end. A rational agent who takes the permanence into the calculation would, on every scale, place the permanent above the temporary. The "those who prefer this world over the Hereafter" are therefore, in the sūrah's analysis, not merely wicked but *miscalculating*: they treat the permanent as if it were absent and the temporary as if it were everything. The modern names for the same error are familiar: the person who spends a lifetime of health to buy a fortune that cannot be carried into the grave; the enterprise that maximizes the quarter at the cost of the century; the culture that optimizes for the immediate and treats the permanent as a category of fiction. The sūrah's diagnosis is not a moral rebuke but a statement of arithmetic: the preference is a wrong sum, and the distance it produces is a wrong distance. The cure the sūrah will offer — remembrance of the Days of God (v. 5), gratitude for blessings (vv. 7–8), the establishment of the way through worship (vv. 35–41) — is precisely the re-orientation of the heart's gravity toward the permanent.
---

## Sūrah Ibrāhīm 14:4

> **And We have sent no messenger, save in the language of his people, that he might make clear unto them. Then God leads astray whomsoever He wills and guides whomsoever He wills. And He is the Mighty, the Wise.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Language as the Precondition of Clarity**

The sūrah has now established the Book (v. 1), the Book's Owner (v. 2), and the psychology of its rejection (v. 3). Verse 4 turns to the mechanism of the message itself and states one of the Qur'an's most repeated principles: *wa mā arsalnā min rasūlin illā bi-lisāni qawmihī li-yubayyina lahum* — "And We have sent no messenger, save in the language of his people, that he might make clear unto them." The claim is universal — *mā arsalnā* — no messenger, without exception, from the first to the last. And its purpose is stated with a verb of exceptional precision: *li-yubayyina lahum* — "that he might make clear unto them." *Bayān* in the Qur'an's usage is not mere information; it is the removal of obscurity, the making of a thing clear so that it can be grasped, judged, and acted upon. The standard of the prophetic mission, the verse says, is clarity itself. The Messenger's job is not merely to *deliver* but to *make clear* — to remove every barrier between the truth and the hearer's understanding.

**Why the Language of the People**

The commentators (al-Kalbī among the early exegetes) explain that the messengers spoke the language of the people to whom they were sent and knew their idiom, so that they would be understood. The Prophet ﷺ spoke to his people, who were Arabs, in a clear Arabic tongue, as the Book itself records elsewhere: *innahu ilā kābira l-mustaqīm. Nuzʿa qurʾānan ʿarabiyyan li-tuʿallima* — the Book given as "a Qur'an in the Arabic language" (12:2); and in the parallel sūrah of the same letter-series: *wa mā arsalnā min rasūlin illā bi-lisāni qawmihī fa-yubayyina lahum* (26:195). The logic is plain: the message must arrive in the hearer's native tongue so that the only remaining barrier to understanding is the heart, not the language. No listener is left with the excuse "I did not understand the words." When comprehension fails, the failure is located precisely — in the will, not in the vocabulary. The principle also guards a wider misunderstanding: Islamic belief is explicit that the Prophet was not sent only to the Arabs (see 13:37); the language of the messenger is the instrument of *clarity*, not the boundary of *audience*. The message travels; the clarity it carries with it is what makes the travel possible.

**"Then": The Hinge Between Two Subjects**

The single word *thumma* — "then" — is the hinge of the verse, and it must not be skimmed. The first half's subject is the Messenger: the sending, the language, the making clear. The second half's subject is God: *thumma yudhillu llāhu man yashāʾu wa yahdī man yashāʾ* — "Then God leads astray whomsoever He wills and guides whomsoever He wills." The order is not decorative; it is ʿaqīdah. The Messenger's clarity comes first; the divine disposal comes after. The two subjects are joined in a single sentence because the two realities are one process: a message is made clear, and a heart responds, and the response is received by the One who owns all (v. 2). The *thumma* protects both halves from being absorbed into the other. The Messenger's work is real — clarity was actually delivered, in the people's own tongue, by the standard the Book itself sets. And the heart's response is real — the person accepts or refuses, and the acceptance or refusal is not the Messenger's to force. The hinge holds the two facts apart so that neither can be used as an excuse for the other.

**Guidance and Misguidance: The Classical Reading of the Repeated Formula**

The formula *yudhillu llāhu man yashāʾu wa yahdī man yashāʾ* is one of the most frequently repeated in the Qur'an (e.g., 4:88–90; 17:97; 18:17; 35:8; 74:31), and the classical commentators developed a settled reading of it. Al-Ṭabarī, following the earliest reports, explains that God's "leading astray" is not arbitrary: it follows the person's own prior choice. The one who, when the message is made clear, turns away, hardens, and persists in refusal is "led astray" in the sense that God, in His wisdom, lets the consequence of the refusal take its full course — the heart grows darker, the evidence recedes, the choice narrows. Guidance, likewise, is not a force applied to the unwilling: it is the divine enabling (*tawfīq*) that follows the heart's own turning toward the truth. The sūrah's own frame confirms the reading: the same verse that says "We have sent no messenger save in the language of his people, that he might make clear" (v. 4) will later say of the messengers, "We are but human beings like yourselves, but God is gracious unto whomsoever He wills" (v. 11) — the grace is the same *tawfīq*. And Ibn Taymiyyah, in his treatment of the attributes, makes the point structurally: the will of God, in this context, is the will that follows the creation of the cause — the person's own turning or refusing is the cause, and the disposal is the consequence. The two names that close the verse — *al-ʿAzīz, al-Ḥakīm* — the Mighty, the Wise — seal the ʿaqīdah: the One who disposes is the One whose power is total (so the disposal is not a suggestion) and whose wisdom is perfect (so the disposal is not cruelty). A disposal that is total in power and perfect in wisdom is not a threat to be feared as arbitrary; it is a justice to be heeded.

**The Frame Closed: Mighty in Verse 1, Wise in Verse 4**

Notice the symmetry. Verse 1 closed with *al-ʿAzīz al-Ḥamīd* — the Mighty, the Praised: the names of the *path*. Verse 4 closes with *al-ʿAzīz al-Ḥakīm* — the Mighty, the Wise: the names of the *disposal*. The sūrah has, in four verses, said everything it will spend fifty more verses elaborating: the Book is from the Owner of all (vv. 1–2); the rejection is a preference for the temporary (v. 3); the message was made clear in the people's own tongue, and the disposal followed the hearts (v. 4). The remainder of the sūrah is that program in motion — worked through in Moses, in the destroyed nations, in the two parables of the word, in the prayer of Abraham, and in the Day when the verdict is executed.

**The Principle in Practice: No Linguistic Excuse, No Coerced Belief**

The verse's practical import is twofold, and both directions matter. First, it removes the excuse of incomprehension: every community received its message in its own tongue, so the barrier is never the language. This is why the sūrah's account of the rejected nations consistently emphasizes that the messengers' speech was clear — "their messengers brought them clear proofs" (v. 9), and the proofs were in the language of the people. Second, it preserves the freedom of the heart: the Messenger's clarity does not compel belief; the disposal is by God's will, following the heart's own response. The two together are the sūrah's ʿaqīdah of guidance — the message is fully delivered, the response is fully free, and the disposal is by the One who is the Mighty, the Wise. For the modern reader the principle operates at the level of any communication: a message that has not been made clear in the hearer's language is not yet a message; and a message that has been made clear does not compel its acceptance. The sūrah holds both facts in a single line — and the line, in the Qur'an's own testimony, has held for every messenger who ever spoke in the language of his people.
---

## Sūrah Ibrāhīm 14:5

> **We indeed sent Moses with Our signs, "Bring thy people out of darkness into light, and remind them of the Days of God. Truly in that are signs for every person who is patient, thankful."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Book's Charter Worked Through a Prophet**

Verse 1 declared that the Book is sent down "that thou mightest bring forth mankind out of darkness into light." Verse 5 immediately stages that declaration in history: *wa laqad arsalnā Mūsā bi-āyātinā* — "We indeed sent Moses with Our signs." The sūrah is doing something deliberate here. It takes the general claim of its opening — that a Book's work is an extraction from darkness into light — and shows that the same work has a historical precedent in the greatest of the law-bearing prophets. The parallel is exact. Moses was sent *with signs* (*bi-āyātinā* — "with Our signs"), and the Prophet is sent *with a Book*; in both cases the signs are God's, not the messenger's — the possessive is the divine *āyātinā*. In both cases the mission's purpose is stated with the same verb of extraction: "bring thy people out of darkness into light." The Book of this sūrah is, in the sūrah's own logic, the continuation of the same movement that began with the staff and the hand, with the flood of water and the parting sea. The Qur'an's full account of Moses — 2:49–61, 7:103–55, 20:9–97, 26:10–66, 28:3–46, 79:15–25 — is the background against which this single verse stands: a man chosen by God, sent with visible signs, to a people in bondage, to bring them out.

**"Bring Thy People Out": Extraction, Not Invitation**

The command quoted within the verse — *ukhrij qawmaka min aẓ-ẓulumāti ilā n-nūr* — uses the same verb *kharaja* as verse 1. The word is the verb of extraction: a person is brought *out* of a place, carried across a boundary, and placed in another region. Darkness is treated, in the Qur'an's imagery, as a *place* — not merely a quality — and the light is another place, and the mission is a movement from one to the other. The people of Moses were literally in a place of darkness: Egypt, the land of Pharaoh's dominion, where the sons were slain and the women spared (v. 6). The extraction was therefore at once physical and spiritual: out of bondage into freedom, and out of the darkness of Pharaoh's false sovereignty into the light of the knowledge of God. This is why the commentators read the phrase as moving "from the darkness of disbelief, ignorance, and doubt to the light of guidance, knowledge, and certainty" (al-Māwardī): the physical and the spiritual are the same crossing seen from two directions. A people that has been physically enslaved to a man is spiritually enslaved to the idea that the man is sovereign; the extraction from the one carries the extraction from the other.

**"Remind Them of the Days of God": Memory as the Engine of Gratitude**

The second half of Moses's command is the method: *wa udhkurhum bi-ayyādi llāh* — "and remind them of the Days of God." The word *ayyām* — "days" — carries, in the Qur'an, more than one meaning, and the commentators weigh them carefully. One meaning is cosmic periods, as in the verse that measures a day with the Lord as a thousand years (22:47). A second, idiomatic meaning — and the one the classical exegetes give primary weight here (al-Qurṭubī, Ibn Kathīr, al-Ṭabarī) — is *blessings*: the *Days of God* are the great occasions of God's favor bestowed upon the Children of Israel, foremost among them the deliverance from bondage in Egypt and the saving from the wrath of Pharaoh and his people. A third meaning, also carried by the commentators, is the *punishments* God visited upon the disbelievers of previous communities — the Days of God as warnings. The word thus holds both poles: the blessings that call to gratitude, and the judgments that call to awe. Moses's command to *remind* — *udhkurhum* — is the key to the whole verse. The Days of God are not new information; they are the people's own history, already in their memory, their songs, their grandparents' stories. Reminding is not informing; it is *reviving* — taking what is true but dormant and making it alive again in the heart. The psychology is plain: a people that has forgotten what was done for it has lost the ground on which its obedience stands.

**"In That Are Signs for Every Person Who Is Patient, Thankful"**

The verse closes with its reward-clause: *la-fī dhālika la-āyātun li-kullī shaākirin ṣābir* — "Truly in that are signs for every person who is patient, thankful." The signs (*āyāt*) here are not the visible miracles but the *lessons contained in the Days* — the proofs embedded in history that can be read by a certain kind of heart. And the heart is specified: *shaḥīrin ṣābir* — the thankful, the patient. The two qualities are the sūrah's own pair, and the Prophet ﷺ is reported to have said of them:

> "Faith consists of two halves: one half is patience, and one half is gratitude." (Reported by al-Qurṭubī; the Prophet then recited the close of this verse: "In that are signs for every person who is patient, thankful.")

The pairing is not arbitrary. Patience (*ṣabr*) is the response to *hardship* — the bearing of what is painful without turning away from God; gratitude (*shukr*) is the response to *blessing* — the receiving of what is good without turning away from God. The two together cover the full range of human experience: every day is either a trial or a gift, and the person who is both patient and thankful is the person for whom the Days of God are *readable*. The one who is not patient sees the trial as meaningless cruelty; the one who is not thankful sees the gift as a natural right. Only the patient-thankful heart sees the Days of God as *signs* — as letters in a message addressed to it.

**The Logic: Why Remembrance, and Why for the Patient-Thankful**

The verse's teaching, stated in its logical form, is: gratitude is the faculty that makes history meaningful, and the Days of God are the content that gratitude feeds upon. A people that remembers its blessings is a people whose will is oriented toward the One who bestowed them; a people that forgets is a people that can be re-slaved by any new Pharaoh, because it has no standard by which to measure the new slavery against the old. The command to *remind* is therefore a command to keep the people's self-knowledge alive — and the reward-clause explains *why the reminder works for some and not for others*: the signs are in the Days, but the signs are *for* the patient-thankful. The reminder is not a spell; it is light. It illuminates, but it does not force the eye to open. The verse thus quietly restates the sūrah's ʿaqīdah (v. 4): the message is delivered with full clarity, and the reception is by the heart. And for the modern reader the principle stands without needing a prophet to apply it: the families, the institutions, the nations that keep a living memory of what was done for them — the sacrifices of their founders, the rescues of their history — are the families, institutions, and nations that remain oriented. The ones that lose the memory lose the orientation, and the next darkness finds them ready to be enslaved to it.
---

## Sūrah Ibrāhīm 14:6

> **And [remember] when Moses said unto his people, "Remember God's Blessing upon you, when He saved you from the House of Pharaoh, who inflicted terrible punishment upon you, slaying your sons and sparing your women—and in that was a great trial from your Lord—**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Day of Deliverance Called to Mind**

The verse continues Moses's address with the *idhā* — "and [remember] when" — the Qur'an's characteristic marker of a recalled scene. Moses is not teaching his people a new doctrine; he is putting a specific day before their eyes. The day is named with its two poles: the blessing and its source. *Udhkurū niʿmata llāhi ʿalaykum* — "Remember God's Blessing upon you" — the blessing is singular in the Arabic (*niʿmata*, the emphatic construct), one great, named, unrepeatable act: *idhā najjākum min āli Fīrʿawn* — "when He saved you from the House of Pharaoh." The word *āli* — "the House of," "the family of" — is the word of total social structure. Pharaoh is not presented as one cruel man; he is presented as a *House* — a dynasty, a court, an entire apparatus of power, with its officials, its armies, its tax-gatherers, its scribes. The slavery was not an accident of one tyrant; it was the standing condition of a civilization. The saving, therefore, was not the removal of one man but the breaking of an entire order — which is why the blessing is named with the singular emphatic *niʿmata*: it was one act, and it was the act that could not be repeated by any human effort, because the order it broke was too large for any human hand to break.

**The Cruelty Specified, and Why It Is Specified**

The verse does not leave the cruelty in the abstract. It names it with two details: *yaḍhibukum ʿadhāban shadīdā* — "who inflicted terrible punishment upon you" — and then the two marks of that punishment: *yaqtalu abnāʾakum wa yuḥyī nīsāʾakum* — "slaying your sons and sparing your women." The two details are the historical core of the story, and the Qur'an gives them elsewhere in full: the report to Pharaoh that a child was born to the Hebrews who would destroy Egypt (7:103; 28:4; 20:70), and Pharaoh's command in response. The classical commentators (Ibn Kathīr, following the earlier reports) connect the verse to the full narrative of Moses's birth and the sending of the cradle down the river, and to the famous words of Pharaoh's own daughter, who, seeing the child, said:

> "A comfort to the eye, for me and for you. Kill him not; perhaps he will benefit us, or we shall take him for a son." (28:9)

The sparing of the women and the slaying of the sons is the cruelty at its most structural: it is not random violence but a *policy* — the deliberate thinning of a people's future while keeping its labor force alive. The verse's purpose in specifying the cruelty is not to dwell on horror but to fix the *measure* of the blessing. The saving is only as great as the slavery it broke. A people that has not measured the cruelty cannot measure the mercy, and a people that cannot measure the mercy cannot measure its debt. The verse therefore does the quiet work of a ledger: it writes down what was done to them, so that what was done for them can be read against it.

**"In That Was a Great Trial from Your Lord"**

The clause *wa fī dhālika la-mawʿiẓatun li-man yajīd* — "and in that was a great trial from your Lord" — is the verse's hinge, and it is the most theologically dense phrase in the sūrah's opening sequence. The word *mawʿiẓa* carries the double meaning the commentators stress: it is a *trial* — a testing, a proving — but depending on the context it can also be a *warning*, a *lesson*, and, as al-Bayḍāwī notes in this very passage, it can refer to a *blessing*, because a blessing is itself a trial: it tests whether the recipient will be grateful or ungrateful. The saving from Pharaoh was therefore a *mawʿiẓa* in the full sense: it was a gift that carried within it a test. The test is the one the next verse will announce: "If you give thanks, I shall surely grant you increase, but if you are ungrateful, truly My Punishment is severe!" (v. 7). The blessing and the test are not two events; they are one event seen from two sides. God's gift is never a dead object; it is a live question, and the question it asks is the question of the response. This is the Qur'an's deepest teaching on the nature of favor: a blessing that is not answered is not a blessing but a *charge* — an account that stands open until the response is given.

**The Repetition at 2:49: The Same Day, the Same Ledger**

The phrase is repeated, almost verbatim, in Sūrah al-Baqarah: *wa idhā najjākum min āli Fīrʿawn yaḍhibūnakum ʿadhāban shadīdā, yaqtilūna abnāʾakum wa yuḥyūna nīsāʾakum, wa fī dhālika la-mawʿiẓatun li-man yajīd* (2:49). The two occurrences are not redundancy. In al-Baqarah the phrase stands in the middle of a long address to a community that has already received the blessing of the Book and is being called to live up to it; here, in Ibrāhīm, it stands at the beginning of the sūrah's program, as the *model* of the address itself. The sūrah is teaching, by example, how a prophet speaks to a people: he begins not with a command but with the ledger of the blessing, and he specifies the cruelty so that the mercy can be measured, and he names the blessing a *trial* so that the people understand that the gift is a question. The repetition across the two sūrahs is the Qur'an's way of saying: this is the standing form of the prophetic word. Every messenger, in every age, begins where Moses began — with *remember what was done for you*.

**The Psychology of the Ledger, and Its Modern Application**

The verse's practical teaching is a discipline of memory. The heart that has lost the measure of what was done for it has lost the ground of its obligation. The person who cannot name the specific kindness — the father who died to put him through school, the friend who stood by him in the worst year, the community that took him in — is the person who can be asked for anything, because he has no standard against which to measure the asking. The verse therefore prescribes a practice: *specify the cruelty, specify the mercy, and hold the two together*. This is not sentiment; it is arithmetic. A debt is only a debt when its amount is known; a mercy is only a mercy when its measure is fixed. The modern application is direct. The families that keep the story of the founding sacrifice alive — the migration, the loss, the rescue — are the families that remain oriented and grateful. The families that lose the story lose the orientation, and the gratitude that should have been their inheritance becomes the obligation of strangers. And the *mawʿiẓa* — the trial within the blessing — stands at the center of the practice: the blessing is not a reward to be enjoyed but a question to be answered, and the answer is the one the next verse will name.
---

## Sūrah Ibrāhīm 14:7

> **And when your Lord proclaimed, "If you give thanks, I shall surely grant you increase, but if you are ungrateful, truly My Punishment is severe!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Your Lord Proclaimed": The Address of Nearness**

The verse returns to the divine address with a verb of particular weight: *wa idhā qāla rabbukum* — "and when your Lord proclaimed." Two words carry the verse's opening. The first is *rabbukum* — "your Lord" — the name of the One who raises, who nurtures, who owns the growing of the person from seed to maturity. The possessive is the second-person plural: *your* Lord, the Lord who is already in the relationship, the Lord who did the saving in the previous verse. The second word is *qāla* in its form of proclamation — the *naḍā* that the commentators read as a call to the one who is near, an address that presupposes the nearness of the addressed. God does not call the people as a stranger calls a stranger across a market; He calls *rabbukum* — your Lord — the One whose hand is already on them. The nearness is the ground of the address. The people are not being summoned to a new relationship; they are being addressed within the relationship that the saving has already established. The *naḍā* of the Lord is the voice of the Master speaking to the servant he has just rescued, and the content of the speech is the question that the rescue raises: *how will you answer?*

**Gratitude as Acknowledgment, Not Feeling**

The first half of the proclamation — *in shakartum* — "if you give thanks" — is the sūrah's central command in its sharpest form. The commentators (al-Ṭūsī among them) define *shukr* here as the *acknowledgment* of God's blessings — the recognition, in the heart and on the tongue, that the blessing is a gift, not a right. The definition matters, because it locates gratitude in the *relation* between the person and the Giver, not in the *feeling* the blessing produces. A person can feel pleasure at a gift and be entirely ungrateful — if the pleasure has not turned the heart toward the Giver. And a person can feel no special pleasure at a gift and be deeply grateful — if the recognition of the Giver is there. Gratitude, in the sūrah's analysis, is a *turning* — the heart's turning from the blessing to the Blesser. The blessing is the occasion; the Blesser is the object. The one who stays on the blessing is the one who has not yet made the turning; the one who moves from the blessing to the Blesser is the one who has given the answer the *mawʿiẓa* (v. 6) was asking for.

**"I Shall Surely Grant You Increase": The Promise and Its Proof**

The promise is stated with the emphatic *la-azīdannakum* — "I shall surely grant you increase" — and the word *zād* — "increase" — is the sūrah's own word for the continuation of the blessing. The commentators (al-Khalīl among the early lexicon-readers) explain that the increase is in the good of this world, in the blessings of the next world, or in both — the increase is not limited to one realm, because the Giver is not limited to one realm. The increase is, in effect, the *continuation of the circuit*: the blessing that was acknowledged is not a closed event; it is an open line, and the acknowledgment keeps the line open, so that the Giver can continue the flow. The report attributed to Jaʿfar al-Ṣādiq (d. 148/765), preserved by al-Qummī, states the mechanism in its most explicit form:

> "Whenever God bestows a blessing upon a servant and he acknowledges it with his heart and praises God for it with the tongue, the blessing does not finish except that God commands that the servant receive an increase." (al-Qummī)

The mechanism is precise: the acknowledgment is with the heart *and* the tongue — the inner recognition and the outer praise — and the increase is not a natural consequence but a *divine command* (*yaṣdur* — "God commands"). The increase is an act of the Giver's will, triggered by the servant's acknowledgment. The logic is the same as the logic of the *mawʿiẓa* (v. 6): the blessing is a question, and the increase is the answer that the question, when answered correctly, receives.

**Ingratitude as Coverage, and the Severe Punishment**

The second half of the proclamation — *in kuftartum fa-inna ʿadhābī la-shadīd* — "if you are ungrateful, truly My Punishment is severe" — names the other response with its root meaning. The commentators (Ibn Kathīr among them) read *kufr* here in its original sense: *coverage*, *concealment*. The ungrateful person is the one who *covers* the blessing — who hides it, who treats it as if it were not a gift, who strives against it, who acts as if the blessing were his own doing or his own right. The *kufr* is not a feeling of dislike; it is an act of *concealment* — the deliberate covering of the Giver within the blessing. And the consequence is named with the same intensity as the promise: *ʿadhābī la-shadīd* — "My Punishment is severe" — the possessive is the first-person *ʿadhābī*, "My Punishment," the punishment that belongs to the One whose nearness was established in the *naḍā*. The severity is not an addition; it is the *measure* of the refusal. The one who covers the Giver in the blessing has, in effect, refused the relationship; and the refusal of the relationship, from the One whose nearness was just proclaimed, carries the weight that the word *shadīd* names.

**The Hadith on Gratitude to People and to God**

The sūrah's teaching on gratitude is confirmed by the Prophetic reports, which extend the principle to its social dimension. Abū Dāwūd (4212) and al-Tirmidhī (1954) transmit from Abū Shurayh al-ʿAdawī:

> "He who does not thank people has not thanked God." (Abū Dāwūd 4212; al-Tirmidhī 1954)

And al-Tirmidhī (1953) and Abū Dāwūd (4213) transmit from Muʿādh ibn Jabal:

> "He who does not give thanks for little will not give thanks for much, and he who does not give thanks for God has turned his back upon God (*quddila ʿalayhi*)." (al-Tirmidhī 1953; Abū Dāwūd 4213)

The first report locates gratitude in its social register: the person who cannot thank the human hand that did the good cannot thank the divine hand that is behind it. The second report states the principle in its full scope: the ingratitude that begins with the small blessing — the little kindness, the daily gift — is the ingratitude that ends with the turning away from God. The *kufr* of v. 7 is not a sudden event; it is a *trajectory*, and the trajectory begins with the small. The sūrah's command to *shukr* is therefore a command to the small as much as to the great: the gratitude that is not practiced on the small blessing is not a gratitude that will be available on the great one.

**The Logic of the Circuit, and the Modern Application**

The verse's teaching, stated in its logical form, is a statement about the *circuit* of the blessing. The blessing is not a one-way transfer from the Giver to the recipient; it is a *circuit* that is completed only when the recipient's acknowledgment returns to the Giver. The one who acknowledges completes the circuit, and the completed circuit is the condition of the increase. The one who covers breaks the circuit, and the broken circuit is the condition of the severity. The increase is not a reward for a virtue; it is the *continuation of a flow* that the acknowledgment keeps open. The modern application is direct and does not require a prophetic context. The person who treats every kindness as a right — the daily bread, the health of the body, the presence of the family — is the person who has broken the circuit, and the broken circuit is the condition of the *shadīd* that the verse names, whether the *shadīd* is felt in this world as the hardening of the heart or in the next as the punishment that is described in the sūrah's later verses. The person who practices the acknowledgment on the small — who names the Giver in the blessing, who gives the praise on the tongue — is the person who has kept the circuit open, and the open circuit is the condition of the *zād*. The sūrah's command is not a moral exhortation; it is a statement of the physics of the blessing, and the physics are the same in every age.
---

## Sūrah Ibrāhīm 14:8

> **And Moses said, "If you are ungrateful—you and whosoever is on the earth all together—[know that] verily God is Self-Sufficient, Praised."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Moses's Closing of the Argument**

The verse gives Moses the final word in the exchange with his people, and the word is a closing of the argument rather than a continuation of it. The people have, in the sūrah's account, been given the full case: the Book's charter (v. 1), the Owner of all (v. 2), the psychology of the rejection (v. 3), the clarity of the message (v. 4), the historical model of the extraction (v. 5), the specified blessing and its trial (v. 6), and the divine proclamation of the increase and the severity (v. 7). Moses's own response to the people's ingratitude — the response that the *naḍā* of v. 7 has provoked — is not a threat, not an argument, not a repetition of the case. It is a *statement of God's self-sufficiency*: *fa-inna llāha ghaniyyun ḥamīd* — "verily God is Self-Sufficient, Praised." The verse is, in effect, the *qāʿida* — the standing rule — of the prophetic address: when the people's ingratitude has been fully stated, the prophet does not add to the threat; he states the ground on which the threat stands.

**"You and Whosoever Is on the Earth All Together": The Hyperbole of the Refusal**

The phrase *antum wa man ʿalā l-arḍ jamiā* — "you and whosoever is on the earth all together" — is a hyperbole of the refusal. Moses is not making a factual claim that the whole earth is ungrateful; he is stating the *limit* of the refusal's power. Even if the entire earth were to become ungrateful together — every person, every nation, every generation — the consequence would be the same: the ingratitude would not reach God, would not touch Him, would not diminish Him in any measure. The hyperbole is the verse's logical device. It takes the refusal to its maximum possible extent — the whole earth, all together — and states that even at that maximum, the refusal is a *nothing* in relation to the One who is addressed. The point is not the number of the ungrateful; the point is the *asymmetry* between the refusal and the Refused. The refusal, at its maximum, is a finite act of finite creatures; the Refused is the One who is *ghaniyy* — the Self-Sufficient, the One to whom all creatures turn for their needs while He needs none of them. The hyperbole is the verse's way of making the asymmetry visible: even at the maximum of the refusal, the asymmetry is total.

**Al-Ḍabī al-Ḥamīd: The Self-Sufficient, the Praised**

The two names — *ghaniyyun ḥamīd* — "Self-Sufficient, Praised" — are the sūrah's own closing names, and they return elsewhere in the Book in the same pairing (2:267; 31:12; 64:6). The name *ghaniyy* — the Self-Sufficient — is the name of the One who needs none of His creatures. The commentators define it: He to whom all creatures turn for their needs, while He is in no need of any of them. The name *ḥamīd* — the Praised — is the name of the One who is deserving of praise in all that He does, the One whose praise is not a favor conferred but a right acknowledged. The two names together state the *full* relation between the Giver and the creatures: the Giver needs none of them (*ghaniyy*), and the Giver is the One whom they are to praise (*ḥamīd*). The relation is therefore *asymmetric* in both directions: the Giver is not affected by the creatures' response, and the creatures are affected by the Giver's act. The one who gives a gift and needs the thanks is not *ghaniyy*; the transaction collapses into bargaining, and the gift becomes a debt. The one who gives a gift and is *ghaniyy* — who needs none of the thanks — gives the gift in a register that is not bargaining, and the thanks that is given is not the payment of a debt but the acknowledgment of a right. The sūrah's teaching on the nature of divine favor is contained in the two names: the favor is not a transaction, and the gratitude is not a payment.

**The Asymmetry: Gratitude Benefits the Servant, Ingratitude Harms the Servant**

The logical consequence of the two names is the *asymmetry* that the verse states. Gratitude benefits the servant and increases the servant — the increase is the *zād* of v. 7, the continuation of the circuit. Ingratitude harms the servant and touches God in nothing — the *shadīd* of v. 7 is the consequence for the servant, not a diminishment of the Giver. The verse therefore states a law of the sūrah's analysis: the ungrateful person is not a *rival* of God; the ungrateful person is a *self-wounded* person. The one who covers the blessing (v. 7) is not attacking the Giver; the one who covers the blessing is breaking his own circuit, and the broken circuit is the condition of his own *shadīd*. The sūrah's teaching on the nature of the refusal is therefore a teaching on the nature of the self: the refusal is not a weapon against God; the refusal is a wound upon the self. And the wound is the one that the sūrah's later verses will describe in concrete detail — the ashes (v. 18), the oozing pus (v. 16), the "no refuge" (v. 21). The refusal is not a political act; it is a self-inflicted wound, and the sūrah's task is to make the wound visible.

**The Rhetorical Position of the Prophet**

Moses's rhetorical position in the verse is the sūrah's own teaching on the prophetic word. The prophet does not threaten with his own power — he has none that is relevant to the case. He does not threaten with Pharaoh's power — Pharaoh's power is the very thing the people are being called out of. He states the ground: *God is Self-Sufficient, Praised*. The ground is the *ghaniyy* — the One whose power is not affected by the people's refusal — and the ground is the *ḥamīd* — the One whose praise is not a favor but a right. The prophet's word is therefore not a *threat* in the ordinary sense; it is a *statement of the ground*. The threat, when it comes, is not the prophet's threat; it is the ground's consequence. The sūrah's teaching on the prophetic word is thus: the prophet does not add to the case; the prophet states the ground, and the ground does the rest. The modern application is the same in any address: the speaker who states the ground — the fact that is not affected by the audience's response — is the speaker whose word carries the weight of the ground, not the weight of the speaker's own force.

**The Verse as the Sūrah's Standing Rule**

The verse is the sūrah's standing rule on the nature of the divine-favor relation, and it is the rule that the sūrah's later verses will apply. The ungrateful who exchanged the blessing for ingratitude (v. 28) are the people who have not grasped the *ghaniyy* — they think their refusal has power over the Giver, and the sūrah's answer is the same: the Giver is *ghaniyy*, and the refusal is a self-wound. The arrogant leaders in the Fire (v. 21) are the people who have not grasped the *ḥamīd* — they think their power is a ground of praise, and the sūrah's answer is the same: the *ḥamīd* is the One whose praise is not a favor but a right, and the power that is not the *ḥamīd* is not a ground of praise. The verse is the sūrah's *qāʿida*, and the sūrah is the verse's elaboration. The two names — *ghaniyyun ḥamīd* — are the sūrah's own closing words, and they are the ground on which the sūrah's whole program stands.
---

## Sūrah Ibrāhīm 14:9

> **Has not the account come to you of those who came before you—the people of Noah, and ʿĀd, and Thamūd, and those who came after them? None knows them but God. Their messengers brought them clear proofs, but they thrust their hands into their mouths and said, "Verily we disbelieve in that wherewith you have been sent, and we are in grave doubt about that to which you call us."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Has Not the Account Come to You": The Shift to the General Pattern**

The sūrah has spent its first eight verses on one specific case — Moses and his people — and verse 9 widens the frame to the general pattern. The question *hal atāka* — "has not the account come to you" — is the Qur'an's own formula for the introduction of a sacred history: the account (*al-khabar*) is not new information but a *known* account, one that has already reached the hearer through the reports of the earlier communities and the recitation of the Book itself. The sūrah is asking the hearer to *recall* the pattern, not to *learn* it for the first time. The pattern is named with its fourfold structure: the people of Noah, ʿĀd, Thamūd, and *those who came after them* — *wa lladhīna baʿdahum*. The first three are the communities to whom the prophets Nūḥ, Hūd, and Ṣāliḥ were sent, respectively, and their stories are told in full elsewhere in the Book: for Noah, 7:59–64, 10:71–73, 11:25–48, 23:23–30, 26:105–21, 37:75–82, 54:9–15, and Sūrah 71 (Nūḥ) in its entirety; for ʿĀd, 7:65–72, 11:50–60, 41:15–16; for Thamūd, 7:73–79, 11:61–68, 26:141–58, 54:23–31. The fourth category — *those who came after them* — is the one the verse will specify with a phrase of remarkable scope.

**"None Knows Them but God": The Vast Number, and the Scope of the Pattern**

The phrase *ma yaʿlamuhum illā llāh* — "none knows them but God" — is the verse's hinge, and the commentators weigh two readings. The first, given by al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī, is that no one knows the *exact number* of the communities that came after the people of Noah, ʿĀd, and Thamūd except God — the number is beyond the reach of human count, because the pattern of the messenger-and-the-rejection is not limited to the three named communities but extends, in God's knowledge, to every community that has received a messenger and refused. The second reading, given by al-Kalbī, is that the phrase is an expression used to indicate their *vast numbers* — the number is so large that it is beyond the ordinary human count, as in the parallel formula at 25:38. The two readings are not in tension: the pattern is *vast* in number, and the *exact* number is God's. The sūrah's purpose in the phrase is not to give a census; it is to state the *scope* of the pattern. The pattern is not a local phenomenon — not limited to one region, one era, one set of tribes — but a *standing* pattern, one that extends, in God's knowledge, to every community that has received the message and refused it. The hearer is invited to place himself within the scope: the pattern is not *their* pattern; the pattern is *the* pattern, and the hearer's community is within it.

**"Their Messengers Brought Them Clear Proofs"**

The verse then states the *standard* of the message as it was delivered to these communities: *jāʾahumu rusuluhum bi-l-bayyināt* — "their messengers brought them clear proofs." The word *bayyināt* — "clear proofs," "evident signs" — is the same word of *bayān* that the sūrah used in v. 4 for the standard of the prophetic mission. The commentators (al-Nīsābūrī, al-Māwardī) explain that the *bayyināt* here refer to the *miracles* (*muʿjizāt*) that the prophets brought, which affirmed their prophethood — the staff of Moses, the ship of Noah, the she-camel of Ṣāliḥ, the clear recitation of the Book. The standard is the same in every case: the message was delivered with *clear proofs*, in the language of the people, by the standard the sūrah itself set in v. 4. The rejection that follows is therefore not a response to an evidential vacuum; the rejection is a response to *clarity*. The sūrah's ʿaqīdah (v. 4) is restated in the historical register: the message was made clear, and the refusal followed the clarity. The *bayyināt* are not a detail; they are the *condition* of the refusal's weight. A refusal that is made after clarity is not a refusal that can be excused by the absence of evidence; the refusal is made *against* the evidence, and the refusal is the person's own act.

**"They Thrust Their Hands into Their Mouths": The Body's Betrayal**

The verse's most concrete image is the physical one: *fa-dakhkhalū aymānahum fī afwāhihim* — "they thrust their hands into their mouths." The commentators (al-Rāzī among them) give two readings of the gesture. The first is literal: they bit their fingers either out of anger — the intense enmity that the clarity of the message provoked — or because they were mocking and needed to withhold their laughter, the laughter that the body was producing in spite of the mouth's attempt to control it. The second reading is metaphorical: the *hands* of the disbelievers — their power, their action — are thrust into the *mouths* of the messengers — their word — in the sense that the disbelievers rejected the *teachings* of the messengers and attempted to *silence* them. The two readings are not in tension: the gesture is, at its root, the *body's betrayal* of the mouth's position. The mouth says "we are in doubt"; the hand is biting the finger — the body is producing the sign of the anger or the laughter that the mouth is trying to control. The sūrah's teaching on the psychology of the refusal is contained in the gesture: the refusal is not a *calm* intellectual position; the refusal is a *physical* state, and the body betrays what the mouth is trying to disguise. The one who is truly in *doubt* does not bite his finger; the one who is truly in *doubt* is open to the evidence. The one who bites his finger is the one who has *decided* and is using the word "doubt" as a *fortress*.

**"We Disbelieve, and We Are in Grave Doubt": The Deliberate Refusal**

The words of the refusal are stated in full: *innā kafarnā bi-mā arsaltum bihi wa nahnu fī mā tadʿūnāna ilayhi fī shakkin mubīn* — "Verily we disbelieve in that wherewith you have been sent, and we are in grave doubt about that to which you call us." The first clause is an *explicit* denial — *kafarnā* — the *kufr* of v. 7, the *coverage* of the message, stated without qualification. The second clause is the *shakkin* — the "grave doubt" — and the word *shakkin* here is not the honest, open doubt of the seeker; it is the *shakk ʿadīm* — the "doubt that is a decision," the deliberate, chosen doubt that the ego uses as a fortress against the clarity. The commentators note the distinction: the honest doubt is open to the evidence, and the honest doubt is resolved by the evidence; the *shakkin* of the verse is *closed* to the evidence, and the *shakkin* is the *position* that the ego takes when it has already decided and is using the word "doubt" as the *name* of the decision. The sūrah's teaching on the psychology of the refusal is thus: the refusal is not a *failure* to understand; the refusal is a *decision* to refuse, and the word "doubt" is the *name* the decision gives itself. The modern application is direct: the person who says "I am still researching" — who has been "researching" for twenty years, who has read every counter-argument and none of the primary texts — is the person who is *fī shakkin* in the sūrah's sense. The *shakkin* is not an open question; the *shakkin* is a *closed* position that has taken the name of an open question.

**The Logic: The Pattern, and the Hearer's Place Within It**

The verse's teaching, stated in its logical form, is a statement about the *pattern* of the refusal. The pattern is: the message is delivered with *clear proofs* (*bayyināt*), in the language of the people, by the standard of *bayān* (v. 4). The refusal follows the clarity — the refusal is made *after* the evidence, not in the absence of it. The refusal is a *physical* state — the body betrays the mouth — and the refusal takes the name of *doubt* when it is in fact a *decision*. The pattern is *vast* in scope — the communities that have followed it are beyond human count, and the exact number is God's. And the pattern is *standing* — it is not a local phenomenon but a *permanent* feature of the relation between the message and the ego. The hearer is invited to place himself within the pattern: the pattern is not *their* pattern; the pattern is *the* pattern, and the hearer's own response to the clarity is the one that will place him on one side of the pattern or the other. The sūrah's next verse will give the messengers' response to the refusal, and the response will be the sūrah's own teaching on the ʿaqīdah of the relation: *We are but human beings like yourselves, but God is gracious unto whomsoever He wills* (v. 11). The pattern is the sūrah's standing rule, and the verse is the pattern's full statement.
---

## Sūrah Ibrāhīm 14:10

> **Their messengers said, "Is there any doubt concerning God, the Originator of the heavens and the earth? He calls you that He might forgive some of your sins and grant you reprieve till a term appointed." They said, "You are but human beings like us. You desire to turn us away from that which our fathers used to worship. So bring us a manifest authority!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Doubt Given Its Proper Object**

The messengers' response to the "grave doubt" of v. 9 is itself a question, and the question is a re-location of the doubt: *hal min shakkin fī llāh* — "Is there any doubt concerning God?" The commentators (al-Kalbī among them) explain that the question asks whether there is any doubt about God's *Being* or His *Divinity* — not about the messengers, not about the details of the law, but about the very Source of the message. The move is rhetorical and logical at once. The people have declared themselves "in grave doubt about that to which you call us" (v. 9); the messengers answer by placing the doubt where it can be examined: *concerning God*. If the doubt is concerning the messenger, it is a doubt about a human being — and human beings are knowable, testable, falsifiable. If the doubt is concerning *God* — the Originator of the heavens and the earth — then the doubt is not an epistemic position but a *presumption*: the One who originated all that exists is not a candidate for doubt. The question "Is there any doubt concerning God?" is therefore not an invitation to investigate; it is a *statement* in the form of a question: there is no legitimate doubt concerning the Originator, and the "grave doubt" of v. 9 is not a doubt but a *decision* wearing the name of a doubt.

**"The Originator of the Heavens and the Earth": The Reminder That Dispels the Doubt**

The title attached to God in the question — *mūlidi l-samāwāti wa l-arḍ* — "the Originator of the heavens and the earth" — is the reminder that should dispel the doubt. The commentators note that the messengers mention the *Origin* of all things that exist as the specific counter to the specific doubt: the one who doubts the Source of the message is reminded of the Source of *everything*. The word *mūlid* — from the root *w-l-d*, "to bear, to produce, to bring forth" — is the word of *origination*, not merely of *creation*: it denotes the One who *brought forth* the heavens and the earth from non-existence, the One whose act of bringing-forth is the ground of every act of bringing-forth in the world. The parallel at 51:21 states the same title with the same force: *al-amma khalaqa llāhu s-samāwāti wa l-arḍa bi-l-ḥaqqi* — "Have you not seen that it is He who created the heavens and the earth in truth?" The logic is plain: the One who originated the heavens and the earth is not in the *category* of the things originated; the Originator is not a candidate for the doubt that applies to the originated. The doubt concerning God is, in the sūrah's analysis, a *category error* — the application of a question that is legitimate for created things to the Uncreated.

**"He Calls You": The Call as the Context of the Forgiveness and the Reprieve**

The next clause — *wa tadʿōkum li-yaghfira lakum min dhunūbikum wa yumhila kum ilā wāqit* — "He calls you that He might forgive some of your sins and grant you reprieve till a term appointed" — states the *purpose* of the call. The commentators (al-Ṭarsūsī) read *wadʿānakum* as "He calls you to faith" — the *call* is the *summons* to the relationship, and the *purpose* of the summons is twofold: the *forgiveness* of the sins already committed, and the *reprieve* — the *muhlā* — until the *appointed term*. The two purposes are the *full* scope of the mercy: the *forgiveness* is the *retrospective* mercy (the sins already committed are covered), and the *reprieve* is the *prospective* mercy (the time is extended for the response to be given). The call is therefore not a *threat*; the call is a *summons* to the *relationship* that carries both the *retrospective* and the *prospective* mercy. The logic of the call is the logic of the *mawʿiẓa* (v. 6): the blessing is a *question*, and the call is the *question* addressed to the *sinner*: *will you answer?*

**"Some of Your Sins": The Scope of the Forgiveness, and the Classical Debate**

The word *min* — "some" — in *li-yaghfira lakum min dhunūbikum* is the verse's most debated word, and the commentators give two readings.
