The first, given by al-Ṭarsūsī and others, is that God forgives *some* sins, not *all*, because there are sins He will not forgive — foremost among them the sin of ascribing partners unto Him (*shirk*), and the violation of the rights of God's servants, whose forgiveness is entirely up to the one who has been wronged. The parallel at 4:48 states the principle explicitly:

> "Truly God forgives not that any partner be ascribed unto Him, but He forgives what is less than that for whomsoever He wills." (4:48)

The second reading, given by al-Ṭabarī, is that *min*, used in this instance as a stylistic feature of classical Arabic rhetoric, actually means "all" — the *min* is the *min al-taʿdīd* (the "min of enumeration"), which in Arabic can denote the *whole* when the context indicates totality. The two readings are not in tension at the level of the *principle*: the *shirk* is the *one* sin that is not forgiven, and all *other* sins are forgiven for whom God wills. The sūrah's teaching on the *scope* of the forgiveness is thus: the forgiveness is *total* except for the *one* — the *shirk* — and the *one* is the *shirk* because the *shirk* is the *refusal* of the *relationship* itself. The *forgiveness* is the *mercy* of the *relationship*; the *shirk* is the *refusal* of the *relationship*; and the *refusal* of the *relationship* is not *forgiven* because the *forgiveness* is the *mercy* of the *relationship*.

**"A Term Appointed": The Reprieve and Its Limit**

The word *wāqit* — "a term appointed" — is, according to al-Qurṭubī, *death* — the *appointed term* is the *term* of the *life*, and the *reprieve* is the *extension* of the *life* until the *appointed term*. The *reprieve* is therefore not an *indefinite* extension; the *reprieve* is the *extension* until the *appointed term*, and the *appointed term* is the *death*. The *logic* of the *reprieve* is the *logic* of the *mawʿiẓa*: the *blessing* is a *question*, and the *reprieve* is the *time* in which the *question* is to be *answered*. The *reprieve* is not a *gift* of *time*; the *reprieve* is a *gift* of the *opportunity* to *answer*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *reprieve* is thus: the *reprieve* is the *time* in which the *response* is to be *given*, and the *response* is the *one* that the *mawʿiẓa* (v. 6) and the *naḍā* (v. 7) have *asked* for.

**"You Are but Human Beings Like Us": The Objection, and Its Logic**

The people's response — *qālū in-antum bashasharun amthālunā* — "You are but human beings like us" — is the *standard* objection of the *rejected* messengers, and it recurs throughout the *Book*: 11:27, 17:94, 21:3, 23:24, 33, 64:6. The *logic* of the *objection* is the *logic* of the *category*: the *messengers* are *human*, and the *human* is *not* in the *category* of the *divine*, therefore the *message* is *not* *divine*. The *objection* is the *application* of the *category* to the *messenger* in a way that *excludes* the *possibility* of the *divine* choosing *from* among the *human*. The *sūrah's* response (v. 11) will *accept* the *category* — *yes, we are human like you* — and *turn* the *objection* into the *proof*: the *very fact* of the *human* being *chosen* is the *demonstration* of the *divine* freedom. The *objection* is the *refusal* of the *category*; the *proof* is the *acceptance* of the *category* and the *demonstration* of the *divine* freedom within it.

**"That Which Our Fathers Used to Worship": The Authority of Ancestral Custom**

The second clause of the people's response — *turdūnā ʿammā kāna yaʿbudū ābāʾunā* — "You desire to turn us away from that which our fathers used to worship" — is the *social* register of the *objection*. The *worship* of the *fathers* is not *examined*; the *worship* of the *fathers* is *inherited*. The *logic* of the *clause* is the *logic* of the *taqlīd* (the *following* of the *ancestors*): the *authority* of the *fathers* is *presumed*, and the *examination* of the *fathers'* *worship* is *refused*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *psychology* of the *refusal* is thus: the *refusal* is not a *refusal* of the *evidence*; the *refusal* is a *refusal* of the *examination* of the *inherited*. The *fathers'* *worship* is the *ground* of the *identity*; the *examination* of the *fathers'* *worship* is a *threat* to the *identity*; and the *refusal* of the *examination* is the *defense* of the *identity*. The *modern application* is *direct*: the *person* who *defends* the *inherited* *practice* without *examining* it is the *person* who is *fī* the *sūrah's* *sense* of the *refusal*.

**"So Bring Us a Manifest Authority!": The Demand on Their Own Terms**

The final clause — *fa-tiʾūnā bi-sultānin mubīn* — "So bring us a manifest authority!" — is the *demand* for a *sultān* — a *clear*, *visible*, *decisive* *proof*. The *sultān* is the *authority* that is *visible* and *material*, the *proof* that is on the *terms* of the *demanders* — not on the *terms* of *reason*, but on the *terms* of *sight*. The *logic* of the *demand* is the *logic* of the *closed* *position* (the *shakkin* of v. 9): the *demand* for a *sultān* is the *demand* for a *proof* that will *not* be *refused*, a *proof* that is *so* *visible* that *refusal* is *impossible*. The *sūrah's* response (v. 11) will state the *principle*: *it is not for us to bring you an authority, save by God's Leave*. The *sultān* is not the *messengers'* to *give*; the *sultān* is *God's* to *give* by *His* *Leave*. The *demand* is *answered* by the *principle*: the *source* of the *proof* is the *same* as the *source* of the *message* — *God*.
---

## Sūrah Ibrāhīm 14:11

> **Their messengers said unto them, "We are but human beings like yourselves, but God is gracious unto whomsoever He wills among His servants. And it is not for us to bring you an authority, save by God's Leave; so in God let the believers trust.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"We Are but Human Beings Like Yourselves": The Objection Accepted**

The messengers' response to the objection "You are but human beings like us" (v. 10) is to *accept* the objection: *qālū innā bashasharun amthālukum* — "We are but human beings like yourselves." The acceptance is the sūrah's own logic: the messengers do not deny their humanity; they *affirm* it. The parallel at 18:110 and 41:6 states the same affirmation: *qūlū innā bashasharun amthālukum* — "Say: We are but human beings like you." The logic of the acceptance is the logic of the *proof*: the very fact that the messenger is human is the *demonstration* of the possibility for humans. If the messenger were not human — if the messenger were an angel, or a being of a different order — then the message would be a *demonstration* of the *impossibility* for humans. The fact that the messenger *is* human, and that the messenger has been *chosen* by God, is the *demonstration* that the *choice* is *God's* — that the *choice* is not a *human* achievement but a *divine* gift. The acceptance of the humanity is therefore the *acceptance* of the *proof*: the *human* is *chosen*, and the *choice* is *God's*.

**"But God Is Gracious Unto Whomsoever He Wills Among His Servants"**

The word *lākinn* — "but" — is the hinge of the verse, and it introduces the *proof* that the acceptance of the humanity carries: *wa lākinnā llāha yumanni ʿalā man yashāʾu min ʿibādihi* — "but God is gracious unto whomsoever He wills among His servants." The commentators (al-Ṭarsūsī, al-Zamakhsharī) explain that the *mann* — the *grace*, the *gift* — here refers to God's bestowal of *prophethood*, and to His granting of the *ability* to perform *miracles* to the prophets. The word *yumanni* — "He is gracious" — is the verb of the *gift* that is not *earned*: the *mann* is the *gift* that is *given*, not the *reward* that is *earned*. The *logic* of the *clause* is the *logic* of the *divine* freedom: the *choice* is not distributed according to *human* achievement; the *choice* is distributed according to *God's* will. The *one* whom God *chooses* is the *one* whom God *chooses* — not the *one* who has *earned* the *choice*, but the *one* whom God *has graciously* *chosen*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of *prophethood* is thus: *prophethood* is not a *human* achievement; *prophethood* is a *divine* gift, and the *gift* is *given* to *whomsoever* God *wills*.

**The Logic: Why the Humanity Is the Proof, Not the Objection**

The *logic* of the *verse*, stated in its *full* form, is a *statement* about the *nature* of the *choice*. The *objection* (v. 10) is: *you are human like us, therefore the message is not divine*. The *response* (v. 11) is: *yes, we are human like you — but God is gracious unto whomsoever He wills among His servants*. The *logic* of the *response* is the *logic* of the *divine* freedom: the *choice* is *God's*, and the *choice* is *not* limited by the *category* of the *chosen*. The *one* whom God *chooses* is the *one* whom God *chooses* — and the *category* of the *chosen* is *human* because *God* has *chosen* the *human*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *choice* is thus: the *choice* is *God's* freedom, and the *freedom* is *demonstrated* by the *choice* of the *human*. The *human* is *chosen*, and the *choice* is *God's* — the *two* together are the *proof*.

**"It Is Not for Us to Bring You an Authority, Save by God's Leave"**

The next clause — *wa mā yakūnu lanā an najiʾakum bi-sultānin illā bi-idhni llāh* — "And it is not for us to bring you an authority, save by God's Leave" — is the *response* to the *demand* for a *sultān* (v. 10). The *messengers* do not *refuse* the *demand*; the *messengers* state the *principle*: the *sultān* is not *theirs* to *give*; the *sultān* is *God's* to *give* by *His* *Leave*. The *word* *idhn* — "Leave" — is the *same* word as in v. 1 (*bi-idhni rabbihim* — "by the leave of their Lord"). The *Leave* is the *divine* will, the *divine* permission, the *divine* *enabling*. The *logic* of the *clause* is the *logic* of the *source*: the *source* of the *proof* is the *same* as the *source* of the *message* — *God*. The *messengers* are the *hands* that *deliver* the *message*; the *sultān* is *God's* to *give*, by *His* *Leave*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *proof* is thus: the *proof* is not the *messengers'* *achievement*; the *proof* is *God's* *gift*, given by *His* *Leave*.

**"So in God Let the Believers Trust": The First Tawakkul of the Sūrah**

The verse closes with the *first* occurrence of *tawakkul* in the sūrah: *fa-ʿalā llāhi fawakkala l-muʾminūn* — "so in God let the believers trust." The *verb* *fawakkala* — "to entrust one's affairs to God" — is the *verb* of the *entrustment*: the *one* who *trusts* in God *entrusts* his *affairs* to *God*, and the *entrustment* is the *response* to the *principle* that the *messengers* have *stated*. The *address* shifts from *we* (the *messengers*) to *the believers* — the *messengers* include *their own followers* in the *address*. The *tawakkul* is the *response* to the *principle*: the *sultān* is *God's* to *give*, by *His* *Leave*; the *choice* is *God's* to *make*, by *His* *grace*; and the *response* to the *two* is the *entrustment* of the *affairs* to *God*. The *Prophet* ﷺ is *reported* to have *described* the *tawakkul* in a *report* *preserved* by *al-Bayhaqī*:

> "If you were to rely upon God with the reliance due to Him, He would have provided for you as He provided for the birds: they go out in the morning hungry and return in the evening full." (Recorded by al-Bayhaqī)

The *tawakkul* is not *passivity*; the *tawakkul* is the *entrustment* of the *outcome* after the *performance* of the *duty*. The *birds* *go out* in the *morning* — the *duty* is *performed*; the *birds* *return* in the *evening* *full* — the *outcome* is *entrusted* to *God*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *tawakkul* is thus: the *tawakkul* is the *entrustment* of the *outcome* to *God*, and the *entrustment* is the *response* to the *principle* that the *source* of the *outcome* is *God*, not the *effort* of the *efforter*.

**The Cross-References: The Tawakkul as a Standing Principle**

The *formula* *fa-ʿalā llāhi fawakkala l-muʾminūn* and its *variants* recur throughout the *Book* as a *standing principle*: 3:122, 160; 5:11; 9:51; 12:67; 39:38; 58:10; 64:13. The *principle* is the *response* to the *threat*: the *one* who is *threatened* either *fears* (and *therefore negotiates*) or *trusts* (and *therefore endures*). The *tawakkul* is the *response* of the *one* who *trusts*: the *one* who *trusts* does not *negotiate* with the *threat*; the *one* who *trusts* *endures* the *threat*, because the *outcome* is *entrusted* to *God*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *psychology* of the *tawakkul* is thus: the *tawakkul* is the *solution* to the *threat*, and the *solution* is the *entrustment* of the *outcome* to *God*.
---

## Sūrah Ibrāhīm 14:12

> **And why should we not trust in God, when He has guided us in our ways? And we shall surely endure patiently, however you may torment us. And let those who trust, trust in God.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Why Should We Not Trust in God?": The Rhetorical Question as the Statement of Impossibility**

The verse opens with a rhetorical question that is, in the sūrah's logic, a *statement* of impossibility: *fa-kayfa naʿkufu ʿan llāh* — "And why should we not trust in God?" The commentators (al-Ṭarsūsī) explain the question in two registers. The first: "What is it that we have, if we do not trust in God and entrust our affairs to Him?" — the question states that the *one* who does *not trust* in God has *nothing* to *rely* upon, and the *one* who has *nothing* to *rely* upon is the *one* who is *without* ground. The second: "We have no excuse if we do not trust in God" — the question states that the *one* who has *experienced* the *guidance* of God is *without* excuse if he does *not trust* in God. The *form* of the *question* — *kayfa* — is the *form* of the *impossibility*: *how* can we *do* otherwise? The *answer* is *implied* in the *form* of the *question*: we *cannot* do *otherwise*, because the *ground* of the *trust* is the *experience* of the *guidance*, and the *experience* of the *guidance* is the *evidence* that makes the *trust* *rational*.

**"When He Has Guided Us in Our Ways": The Ground of the Trust**

The clause *idhā hadānā aṣ-ṣirāṭa* — "when He has guided us in our ways" — is the *ground* of the *trust*. The *plural* *aṣ-ṣirāṭa* — "our ways" — denotes the *full* scope of the *guidance*: the *guidance* of the *mission* (the *path* of the *message*) and the *guidance* of the *inner life* (the *path* of the *heart*). The *logic* of the *clause* is the *logic* of the *experience*: the *trust* is not a *leap* into the *unknown*; the *trust* is the *conclusion* drawn from the *experience*. God has *already* shown *Himself* to be the *Guide* — the *guidance* is the *evidence* that has *already* been *given*; the *trust* is the *response* to the *evidence* that has *already* been *received*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *trust* is thus: the *trust* is not a *leap*; the *trust* is a *conclusion*, and the *conclusion* is drawn from the *experience* of the *guidance*. The *one* who has *experienced* the *guidance* is *obligated* to *trust* — the *trust* is the *rational* response to the *experience*, not the *irrational* *leap* into the *unknown*.

**"We Shall Surely Endure Patiently, However You May Torment Us": The Patience as the Expression of the Trust**

The next clause — *wa la-naṣbiranna ʿalā ʿadhābikum* — "And we shall surely endure patiently, however you may torment us" — is the *consequence* of the *trust*. The *emphatic* *lā-n* — "we shall surely" — is the *emphatic* of the *certainty*: the *patience* is not a *hope*; the *patience* is a *certainty*. The *phrase* *however you may torment us* — *ʿalā ʿadhābikum* — states the *threat* without *flinching*: the *torment* is *real*, the *torment* is *theirs* (the *possessive* *ʿadhābikum* — "your torment"), and the *response* to the *torment* is the *patience*. The *logic* of the *clause* is the *logic* of the *subordination*: the *fear* of the *torment* is *subordinated* to the *certainty* of the *guidance*. The *one* who *trusts* does not *deny* the *torment*; the *one* who *trusts* *places* the *torment* in its *proper* *position*: the *torment* is *real*, but the *torment* is *not* the *last* word. The *patience* is the *expression* of the *trust*: the *one* who *trusts* *endures*, because the *outcome* is *entrusted* to *God*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *psychology* of the *patience* is thus: the *patience* is not the *absence* of *fear*; the *patience* is the *subordination* of the *fear* to the *certainty* of *God*.

**"And Let Those Who Trust, Trust in God": The Second Tawakkul, and the Maintenance of the Trust**

The verse closes with the *second* occurrence of *tawakkul* in the sūrah: *wa-ʿalā llāhi yatawakkal l-muʾminūn* — "And let those who trust, trust in God." The commentators (al-Rāzī, al-Zamakhsharī) distinguish the two occurrences: v. 11's *fa-ʿalā llāhi fawakkala* is the *virtue* of *trust* — the *entrustment* of the *affairs* to *God*; v. 12's *wa-ʿalā llāhi yatawakkal* is the *need to strive to maintain* this virtue — the *ongoing* *effort* to *keep* the *trust* *alive*. The *distinction* is the *sūrah's* own teaching on the *dynamic* of the *tawakkul*: the *tawakkul* is not a *one-time* act; the *tawakkul* is an *ongoing* *effort*. The *one* who *trusts* must *keep* trusting — the *trust* is not a *state* that is *achieved* and *maintained* passively; the *trust* is a *practice* that is *performed* and *maintained* *actively*. The *two* verses together state the *full* dynamic of the *tawakkul*: the *entrustment* (v. 11) and the *maintenance* (v. 12). The *entrustment* is the *act*; the *maintenance* is the *practice*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *tawakkul* is thus: the *tawakkul* is an *ongoing* *practice*, not a *one-time* *act*.

**The Logic: The Trust as the Rational Response to the Experience**

The *logic* of the *verse*, stated in its *full* form, is a *statement* about the *nature* of the *trust*. The *trust* is not a *leap*; the *trust* is a *conclusion*. The *conclusion* is drawn from the *experience* of the *guidance* (the *ground*), and the *conclusion* is *expressed* in the *patience* (the *consequence*). The *one* who has *experienced* the *guidance* is *obligated* to *trust*; the *one* who *trusts* *endures* the *torment*; and the *one* who *endures* must *keep* enduring — the *trust* is an *ongoing* *practice*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *psychology* of the *trust* is thus: the *trust* is the *rational* response to the *experience* of the *guidance*, and the *rational* response is *expressed* in the *patience*, and the *patience* is *maintained* by the *ongoing* *effort* to *trust*.

**The Modern Application: The Trust as the Discipline of the Outcome**

The *verse's* teaching on the *trust* is *directly* applicable to the *modern* reader. The *person* who *entrusts* the *outcome* to *God* does not *abandon* the *effort*; the *person* who *entrusts* the *outcome* to *God* *performs* the *duty* and *entrusts* the *outcome*. The *trust* is the *discipline* of the *outcome*: the *one* who *trusts* does not *anxiously* *monitor* the *outcome*; the *one* who *trusts* *performs* the *duty* and *entrusts* the *outcome*. The *patience* is the *expression* of the *trust*: the *one* who *trusts* *endures* the *torment* — the *torment* of the *delay*, the *torment* of the *rejection*, the *torment* of the *uncertainty* — because the *outcome* is *entrusted* to *God*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *psychology* of the *trust* is thus: the *trust* is the *solution* to the *anxiety* of the *outcome*, and the *solution* is the *entrustment* of the *outcome* to *God* and the *performance* of the *duty*.
---

## Sūrah Ibrāhīm 14:13

> **But those who disbelieved said to their messengers, "We shall surely expel you from our land, or you shall revert to our creed." So their Lord revealed unto them, "We shall surely destroy the wrongdoers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Ultimatum: Exile or Apostasy**

The verse gives the disbelievers' final word in the exchange with their messengers, and the word is an *ultimatum*: *la-nukhrijannakum min balādinā aw tarjaʿū fī dīninā* — "We shall surely expel you from our land, or you shall revert to our creed." The two options are the full scope of the disbelievers' power: *physical expulsion* from the land, or *theological capitulation* to their creed. The logic of the ultimatum is the logic of the *closed position*: the disbelievers offer a choice between two things, and neither of the two things is the *truth*. The choice is between *exile* and *apostasy* — between *leaving* the land and *abandoning* the message. The sūrah's teaching on the psychology of the ultimatum is thus: the one who issues the ultimatum has *exhausted* his arguments; the one who issues the ultimatum has *moved* from the *evidence* to the *threat*. The ultimatum is the *sign* of the *closed position*: the one who is *truly* open to the evidence does not issue an ultimatum; the one who issues an ultimatum has *already decided* and is using the *threat* as the *enforcement* of the decision.

**"Or You Shall Revert to Our Creed": The Conversion Demanded as a Reversion**

The second option — *tarjaʿū fī dīninā* — "you shall revert to our creed" — is a phrase that the commentators (al-Bayḍāwī, al-Kalbī, al-Zamakhsharī) handle with care. The word *tarjaʿū* — "you shall revert" — does *not* imply that the messengers had *previously* followed the false creed and are now being given the option to *return* to it. The messengers did not follow the false creed; the messengers were *sent* from among the people with the *truth*. The word *tarjaʿū*, in keeping with common Arabic usage, means that the messengers would *start* to follow that creed — the "reversion" is in fact a *conversion*. The demand is that the messengers *adopt* the creed of the people, not that they *return* to a creed they had previously held. The sūrah's teaching on the logic of the demand is thus: the demand for "reversion" is the demand for *conversion* — the one who has never been in the creed is demanded to *enter* it, and the *entry* is called a *reversion* because, from the perspective of the people, the *exit* (the messengers' departure from the people's creed) is the *aberration*, and the *re-entry* is the *return* to the *norm*. The parallel at 7:88–89 states the same ultimatum in the context of Shuʿayb:

> "They said: 'O Shuʿayb! Shall your prayer compel us that we should leave what our fathers used to worship, or leave in our hands our property? Verily, we are in grave doubt concerning that wherewith you call us, and surely we, [on our side], are in truth resolute in [our purpose].' He said: 'O my people! If my certainty is clear to me of what my Lord has revealed to me...'" (7:88–89, paraphrased)

**"So Their Lord Revealed Unto Them": The Divine Response to the Ultimatum**

The shift in the verse — *fa-ūḥī rabbuhum ilayhim* — "So their Lord revealed unto them" — is the sūrah's own statement of the *divine response* to the ultimatum. The *word* *ūḥī* — "revealed" — is the verb of the *divine communication* to the messengers: the *revelation* is the *communication* of the *divine command* or the *divine promise* to the *messenger*. The *commentators* note that, although it is the *messengers* who are *mentioned* explicitly in this verse, it is *possible* that *both* the *messengers* and their *followers* are *intended* (al-Bayḵāwī): the *revelation* is to the *messengers*, and the *promise* is for the *messengers* and their *followers* together. The *logic* of the *shift* is the *logic* of the *response*: the *ultimatum* is *answered* by *God*; the *choice* between *exile* and *apostasy* is *replaced* by the *certainty* of the *destruction* of the *wrongdoers*. The *messengers* do not *choose* between the *two* options; the *wrongdoers* are *eliminated*, and the *messengers* — and their *followers* — *remain*.

**"We Shall Surely Destroy the Wrongdoers": The Promise of the Elimination**

The content of the revelation — *innā muḥliqūna l-ẓālimīn* — "We shall surely destroy the wrongdoers" — is the *promise* of the *elimination*. The *verb* *aḥlaqa* — "to destroy, to eliminate" — is the verb of the *total removal*: the *wrongdoers* are not merely *punished*; the *wrongdoers* are *eliminated*, *removed* from the *land*, *removed* from the *history* of the *land*. The *emphatic* *inna* — "We shall surely" — is the *emphatic* of the *certainty*: the *elimination* is not a *threat*; the *elimination* is a *certainty*. The *promise* is addressed to the *messengers*: *God* will *assist* them against their *enemies*. The *commentators* (al-Qurṭubī) explain that the *promise* is the *divine* *guarantee* of the *victory* of the *messengers* over their *enemies*. The *parallel* at 37:171–73 — the *story* of the *destruction* of the *nations* that *rejected* their *messengers* — is the *historical* *confirmation* of the *promise*: the *nations* that *rejected* their *messengers* were *eliminated*, and the *messengers* — and their *followers* — *remained*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *promise* is thus: the *promise* is the *divine* *guarantee* of the *victory* of the *truth* over the *falsehood*, and the *guarantee* is *certain* because the *One* who *guarantees* is the *One* whose *power* is *total* (v. 2).

**The Logic: The Ultimatum Answered by the Certainty**

The *logic* of the *verse*, stated in its *full* form, is a *statement* about the *response* to the *ultimatum*. The *ultimatum* offers a *choice* between *exile* and *apostasy*; the *divine response* *replaces* the *choice* with the *certainty* of the *elimination* of the *wrongdoers*. The *messengers* do not *choose*; the *wrongdoers* are *eliminated*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *psychology* of the *ultimatum* is thus: the *ultimatum* is the *sign* of the *closed position*, and the *response* to the *ultimatum* is the *certainty* of the *elimination* of the *wrongdoers*. The *one* who *issues* the *ultimatum* has *exhausted* his *arguments*; the *one* who *answers* the *ultimatum* is *God*, and the *answer* is the *certainty*.

**The Modern Application: The Ultimatum and the Closed Position**

The *verse's* teaching on the *ultimatum* is *directly* applicable to the *modern* reader. The *person* who *issues* the *ultimatum* — the *person* who *says* "either you agree with me or you leave" — is the *person* who has *exhausted* his *arguments* and has *moved* from the *evidence* to the *threat*. The *response* to the *ultimatum* is not the *acceptance* of the *two* options; the *response* to the *ultimatum* is the *recognition* of the *closed position* and the *trust* in the *certainty* that the *One* who *owns* all (v. 2) will *answer* the *ultimatum*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *psychology* of the *ultimatum* is thus: the *ultimatum* is the *sign* of the *closed position*, and the *response* to the *ultimatum* is the *trust* in the *certainty* of the *divine* *answer*.
---

## Sūrah Ibrāhīm 14:14

> **And We shall surely make you to dwell in the land after them. This is for those who fear My Station and fear My Threat.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"We Shall Surely Make You to Dwell in the Land After Them": The Inheritance**

The verse continues the divine promise begun in v. 13, and the continuation is the *inheritance*: *la-nusknunnakum min baʿdihim fī l-arḍ* — "And We shall surely make you to dwell in the land after them." The commentators (al-Bayḍāwī, al-Nīsābūrī, al-Qurṭubī) explain that the *inheritance* is the *consequence* of the *elimination*: after God has destroyed the wrongdoers and they have perished, He will cause the messengers and their followers to *inherit* their land and homes. The verb *sakan* — "to dwell" — is the verb of the *establishment*: the *messengers* and their *followers* are not merely *allowed* to *enter* the *land*; the *messengers* and their *followers* are *established* in the *land*, *settled* in the *land*, *made* to *dwell* in the *land*. The *inheritance* is the *result* of the *elimination* of the *wrongdoers*: the *wrongdoers* are *removed*, and the *messengers* — and their *followers* — *take* their *place*. The parallel at 7:137 states the same principle in the context of the Children of Israel:

> "And We gave victory to Moses and Aaron and caused them and their people to be delivered from the severe punishment." (7:137)

The *inheritance* is the *divine* *guarantee* of the *victory* of the *truth* over the *falsehood*: the *falsehood* is *eliminated*, and the *truth* *inherits* the *land*.

**The Logic: The Inheritance as the Consequence of the Elimination**

The *logic* of the *inheritance*, stated in its *full* form, is a *statement* about the *consequence* of the *elimination*. The *elimination* (v. 13) is the *removal* of the *wrongdoers*; the *inheritance* (v. 14) is the *establishment* of the *messengers* and their *followers* in the *place* of the *wrongdoers*. The *two* are *cause* and *effect*: the *elimination* is the *cause*, and the *inheritance* is the *effect*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *inheritance* is thus: the *inheritance* is not a *reward* for *virtue*; the *inheritance* is the *result* of the *elimination* of the *wrongdoers*. The *messengers* and their *followers* do not *earn* the *inheritance*; the *messengers* and their *followers* *receive* the *inheritance* as the *consequence* of the *elimination* of the *wrongdoers*.

**"This Is for Those Who Fear My Station": The First Fear**

The next clause — *dhālika li-man khāsha shaʿṭī* — "This is for those who fear My Station" — is the *condition* of the *inheritance*. The *inheritance* is not *unconditional*; the *inheritance* is *for* a *specific* *group*: those who *fear* *My Station*. The *word* *shaʿṭī* — "My Station" — is the *first* of the *two* *fears*, and the *commentators* (al-Ajfārī, al-Rāzī) give it three *readings*. The *first* reading: *My Station* refers to *human beings' standing before God on the Day of Judgment* — the *ʿAqd* — the *standing* in which the *person* is *before* *God*, *accounted* for, *judged*. The *parallel* at 55:46 and 79:40 states the same *standing*: the *Day* in which the *people* *stand* before *God*, *awaiting* the *judgment*. The *second* reading: *My Station* refers to *God's Awareness of all that human beings do, whether in secret or openly* — the *ʿAqd* is the *awareness* of *God*, the *knowledge* of *God* of *every* *act*, *every* *word*, *every* *thought*. The *third* reading: *My Station* refers to *the greatness of God's Majesty and Essence* — the *ʿAqd* is the *Majesty* of *God*, the *Essence* of *God*, the *One* whose *Majesty* is *beyond* the *comprehension* of the *creature*. The *three* readings are not in *tension*: the *standing* before *God* (the *first*), the *awareness* of *God* (the *second*), and the *Majesty* of *God* (the *third*) are the *three* *dimensions* of the *same* *reality*: the *person* is *before* *God*, and *God* is *aware* of the *person*, and *God* is *Majestic* beyond the *comprehension* of the *person*.

**"And Fear My Threat": The Second Fear**

The second clause — *wa khashiya waʿdī* — "and fear My Threat" — is the *second* of the *two* *fears*. The *word* *waʿdī* — "My Threat" — is, according to al-Bayḍāwī, *God's Punishment in the Hereafter* — the *threat* is the *punishment* that *God* has *threatened* in the *Hereafter*, the *punishment* that is the *consequence* of the *refusal* of the *message*. The *two* *fears* together state the *full* scope of the *fear*: the *fear of the Station* (the *standing* before *God*, the *awareness* of *God*, the *Majesty* of *God*) and the *fear of the Threat* (the *punishment* in the *Hereafter*). The *logic* of the *two* *fears* is the *logic* of the *condition*: the *inheritance* is *for* those who *fear* the *two* — the *fear of the Station* and the *fear of the Threat*. The *one* who *fears* the *two* is the *one* who *takes* the *standing* before *God* *seriously*, and the *one* who *takes* the *standing* before *God* *seriously* is the *one* who *lives* in the *light* of the *Hereafter*.

**The Logic: The Inheritance is Conditional, and the Condition is the Fear**

The *logic* of the *verse*, stated in its *full* form, is a *statement* about the *condition* of the *inheritance*. The *inheritance* is not *unconditional*; the *inheritance* is *for* those who *fear* the *two* — the *fear of the Station* and the *fear of the Threat*. The *one* who *fears* the *two* is the *one* who *lives* in the *light* of the *Hereafter*, and the *one* who *lives* in the *light* of the *Hereafter* is the *one* who *inherits* the *land*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *inheritance* is thus: the *inheritance* is *conditional*, and the *condition* is the *fear* — the *fear of the Station* and the *fear of the Threat*. The *one* who *fears* is the *one* who *inherits*; the *one* who *does not fear* is the *one* who *does not inherit*.

**The Modern Application: The Fear as the Ground of the Responsibility**

The *verse's* teaching on the *fear* is *directly* applicable to the *modern* reader. The *fear of the Station* is the *fear of the accounting* — the *fear* that the *person* will *stand* before *God* and be *accounted* for *every* *act*, *every* *word*, *every* *thought*. The *fear of the Threat* is the *fear of the punishment* — the *fear* that the *refusal* of the *message* will *result* in the *punishment* in the *Hereafter*. The *two* *fears* together are the *ground* of the *responsibility*: the *one* who *fears* the *two* is the *one* who *lives* in the *light* of the *accounting*, and the *one* who *lives* in the *light* of the *accounting* is the *one* who *takes* his *acts* *seriously*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *psychology* of the *fear* is thus: the *fear* is not the *absence* of *peace*; the *fear* is the *ground* of the *responsibility*, and the *responsibility* is the *ground* of the *dignity* of the *person*.
---

## Sūrah Ibrāhīm 14:15

> **And they sought victory, and every stubborn tyrant fails.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"They Sought Victory": The Messengers' Supplication**

The verse turns from the people's ultimatum (v. 13) and the divine response (vv. 13–14) to the *messenger's own act* in the middle of the conflict: *wa-staʿāna* — "and they sought victory." The commentators (al-Bayḍāwī, al-Kalbī, al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī) explain that the *messengers* sought *God's Assistance* against their enemies — the word *staʿāna* is the verb of the *seeking of help*, the *turning to God* for the *aid* that is *not* in the *power* of the *messenger* alone. The parallel at 7:89 states the same *supplication* in the *context* of *Shuʿayb*:

> "Our Lord! Decide between us and our people in truth, and Thou art the best of deciders." (7:89)

The *logic* of the *supplication* is the *logic* of the *tawakkul* (vv. 11–12): the *messenger* does *not* *fight* with his *own* *power*; the *messenger* *seeks* *God's* *victory*. The *victory* is not the *messenger's* *achievement*; the *victory* is *God's* *gift*, *sought* by the *messenger* and *given* by *God*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *victory* is thus: the *victory* is *God's* to *give*, and the *messenger's* *role* is the *seeking* of the *victory*.

**"Every Stubborn Tyrant Fails": The Compound of the Vice**

The second half of the verse — *wa-kullu ʿaūdin jaḥūd* — "and every stubborn tyrant fails" — is the *sūrah's* *diagnosis* of the *one* who *opposes* the *messenger*. The *compound* *ʿaṭūdin jaḥūdin* is the *key* to the *diagnosis*. The *word* *ʿaṭūd* — from the *root* *ʿ-ṭ-w-d*, "to be tough, to be stubborn" — is the *word* of the *toughness*: the *one* whose *nature* is *tough*, whose *heart* is *hard*, whose *will* is *set*. The *word* *jaḥūd* — from the *root* *j-h-d*, "to deny, to be ungrateful" — is the *word* of the *denial*: the *one* who *denies* the *evidence*, who is *ungrateful* for the *blessing*. The *two* words together state the *full* scope of the *vice*: the *heart* is *tough* (*ʿaūd* — the *arrogance* that *prevents* *submission*), and the *tongue* *denies* (*jaḥūd* — the *denial* that *prevents* *acknowledgment*). The *compound* is the *sūrah's* own *definition* of the *stubborn tyrant*: the *one* whose *heart* is *tough* and whose *tongue* *denies*, the *one* who *cannot* *submit* because his *heart* is *hard*, and who *will not* *acknowledge* because his *tongue* is *set* in *denial*.

**"Fails": The End of the Tyrant, in the Language of the Withered Plant**

The *verb* *yabīsū* — "fails" — is the *verb* of the *withering*: the *plant* that *cannot* *bear* *fruit*, the *branch* that *dries* up, the *tree* that *loses* its *leaves*. The *commentators* (al-Bayḵāwī) explain that the *phrase* *every stubborn tyrant fails* signifies that *God aided and granted success to the messengers*, whereas *their people who acted haughtily before God and stubbornly rejected the truth were not granted success* — the *messengers'* *request* for *Divine Help* was *answered*, and *their people were destroyed* (al-Qurṭubī). The *logic* of the *verb* is the *logic* of the *plant*: the *tyrant's* *arrogance* was *supposed* to be a *source* of *strength*; the *tyrant's* *arrogance* is in *fact* a *source* of *withering*. The *one* whose *heart* is *tough* is the *one* who *cannot* *bend*; the *one* who *cannot* *bend* is the *one* who *breaks*; and the *one* who *breaks* is the *one* who *fails*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *psychology* of the *tyrant* is thus: the *tyrant's* *strength* is his *weakness*, and his *weakness* is his *strength* — the *two* are the *same* thing, seen from the *two* *directions*.

**The Logic: The Supplication Answered, and the Tyrant Failed**

The *logic* of the *verse*, stated in its *full* form, is a *statement* about the *response* to the *supplication*. The *messengers* *sought* *victory* (the *supplication*); *God* *answered* the *supplication* (the *divine* *response* of vv. 13–14); and the *tyrant* *failed* (the *consequence*). The *three* are *cause*, *response*, and *effect*: the *supplication* is the *cause*, the *divine* *response* is the *answer*, and the *failure* of the *tyrant* is the *effect*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *victory* is thus: the *victory* is *God's* to *give*, the *messenger's* *role* is the *seeking*, and the *tyrant's* *end* is the *failure*.

**The Modern Application: The Arrogance as the Source of the Withering**

The *verse's* teaching on the *tyrant* is *directly* applicable to the *modern* reader. The *person* whose *heart* is *tough* — the *person* who *cannot* *bend*, who *cannot* *submit*, who *cannot* *acknowledge* — is the *person* who is *fī* the *sūrah's* *sense* of the *stubborn tyrant*. The *arrogance* is *supposed* to be a *source* of *strength*; the *arrogance* is in *fact* a *source* of *withering*. The *one* who *cannot* *bend* *breaks*; the *one* who *breaks* *fails*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *psychology* of the *arrogance* is thus: the *arrogance* is the *one* *vice* that *contains* its *own* *destruction*, and the *destruction* is the *consequence* of the *vice*.
---

## Sūrah Ibrāhīm 14:16

> **Beyond him lies Hell; and he shall be given to drink of oozing pus,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Subject: The Stubborn Tyrant of the Previous Verse**

The verse continues the description begun in v. 15, and the subject is the *stubborn tyrant* — *kullu ʿaṭūdin jaḥūd* — the one whose heart is tough and whose tongue denies. The commentators (al-Ṭabarī) note that the subject of these verses is the *every stubborn tyrant* mentioned in v. 15: the singular "he" (*huwa*) is the representative of the class, and the description that follows is the *description of the class* in the *form of the individual*. The sūrah is doing something specific here: it is taking the *abstract diagnosis* of v. 15 (the tyranny, the denial, the failure) and giving it a *concrete destination* — the description of the *punishment* that the *failure* leads to. The abstract and the concrete are the *same* thing seen from the *two* directions: the *failure* of v. 15 is the *entry* into the *punishment* described in vv. 16–17.

**"Beyond Him Lies Hell": The Direction of the Punishment**

The first clause — *wa-mā warāʾahu jahannam* — "Beyond him lies Hell" — states the *direction* of the *punishment*. The commentators (al-Qurṭubī, al-Zamakhsharī) give two readings of the word *warāʾahu*. The first: *after his death he will go to Hell* — the *warāʾ* is the *temporal* *beyond*: the *Hell* is *beyond* the *life*, the *Hell* is the *destination* that comes *after* the *death*. The second: *before him lies Hell* — the *warāʾ* is the *spatial* *beyond*: the *Hell* is *in front of him*, the *Hell* is *right ahead*, the *one* is *on the edge* of the *Hell*, *about to fall* into it. The two readings are not in tension: the *temporal* *beyond* (the *Hell* after the *death*) and the *spatial* *beyond* (the *Hell* in front of him) are the *two* *dimensions* of the *same* *reality*: the *Hell* is *ahead* of the *tyrant*, both *temporally* (it comes *after* the *death*) and *spatially* (it is *in front of him*, at the *edge* of his *path*). The *logic* of the *direction* is the *logic* of the *inevitability*: the *Hell* is not an *alternative*; the *Hell* is the *direction*, the *destination*, the *end* of the *path* that the *tyrant* has *chosen*.

**"He Shall Be Given to Drink of Oozing Pus": The Specificity of the Punishment**

The second clause — *wa-yusqā māʾan ṣadīdā* — "and he shall be given to drink of oozing pus" — is the *first* *specific* of the *punishment*. The *word* *ṣadīd* — "oozing pus" — is, according to al-Bayḍāwī and al-Ṭabarī, a *mixture of blood and pus*, which *oozes from the bodies of the people in Hell* (al-Bayḍāwī, al-Zamakhsharī). The *specificity* of the *description* is the *sūrah's* own *teaching* on the *nature* of the *warning*: the *warning* is not *atmospheric*; the *warning* is *described*, because the *warning* is *meant to be heeded*. The *one* who *warns* with *vague* *threats* is the *one* whose *warning* can be *ignored*; the *one* who *warns* with *specific* *descriptions* is the *one* whose *warning* *cannot* be *ignored*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *psychology* of the *warning* is thus: the *specificity* of the *description* is the *condition* of the *attention* of the *hearer*.

**The Logic: The Abstract Diagnosis and the Concrete Destination**

The *logic* of the *verse*, stated in its *full* form, is a *statement* about the *transition* from the *abstract* to the *concrete*. The *abstract* *diagnosis* of v. 15 (the *tyranny*, the *denial*, the *failure*) is *given* a *concrete* *destination* in v. 16: the *Hell* (the *direction*) and the *oozing pus* (the *specific*). The *two* are the *same* thing seen from the *two* directions: the *abstract* *diagnosis* is the *cause*, and the *concrete* *destination* is the *effect*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *punishment* is thus: the *punishment* is not an *addition*; the *punishment* is the *consequence* of the *diagnosis*. The *one* who is *diagnosed* as a *stubborn tyrant* is the *one* whose *destination* is the *Hell*, and whose *drink* is the *oozing pus*.

**The Modern Application: The Specificity of the Warning**

The *verse's* teaching on the *specificity* of the *warning* is *directly* applicable to the *modern* reader. The *person* who *warns* with *vague* *threats* is the *person* whose *warning* can be *ignored*; the *person* who *warns* with *specific* *descriptions* is the *person* whose *warning* *cannot* be *ignored*. The *specificity* of the *description* is the *condition* of the *attention* of the *hearer*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *psychology* of the *warning* is thus: the *warning* is *effective* only when it is *specific*, and the *specificity* is the *condition* of the *attention*.
---

## Sūrah Ibrāhīm 14:17

> **which he will gulp down, but can scarcely swallow. Death shall come upon him from every side; yet he will not die, and before him lies a grave punishment.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"He Will Gulp Down, but Can Scarcely Swallow": The Mechanism of the Torment**

The verse continues the description of the punishment with its *mechanism*: *yataskkawnahu fa-lā yākdūna yutaḥassawnahu* — "which he will gulp down, but can scarcely swallow." The commentators (al-Qurṭubī) explain that he *gulps* the liquid *because of the liquid's bitterness and heat* — the *ṣadīd* (the oozing pus of v. 16) is *bitter* and *hot*, and the *one* who *drinks* it *gulps* it, *tries* to *get* it *down*, *struggles* with it. And the *one* who *gulps* it *can scarcely swallow* it — *fa-lā yākdūna yutaḥassawnahu* — because he *chokes* on it (al-Bayḵāwī). The *logic* of the *mechanism* is the *logic* of the *double* *torment*: the *liquid* is *too* *bitter* to *swallow* *gently*, and *too* *hot* to *hold* in the *mouth*. The *one* who *drinks* it is *caught* between the *two*: the *one* who *swallows* it *gently* *cannot* (the *bitterness* and the *heat* *force* the *gulp*), and the *one* who *gulps* it *cannot* *swallow* it *easily* (the *choking* is the *consequence* of the *gulp*). The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *torment* is thus: the *torment* is not a *single* *act*; the *torment* is a *mechanism* — a *double* *constraint* that *catches* the *one* *tormented* between the *two*.

**"Death Shall Come Upon Him from Every Side; Yet He Will Not Die": The Torment as the Experience of Death Without the Release of Death**

The next clause — *wa-yaʾtīhi l-mawtu min kulli makānin wa-mā huwa bi-mayyitin* — "Death shall come upon him from every side; yet he will not die" — is the *most precise* *expression* in the *sūrah* of the *nature* of the *torment*. The *commentators* (al-Rāzī) explain that the *phrase* refers to a *state of torment in which each part of his body will be affected by the pains and afflictions that bring about death, but they will not lead to death* — the *death* *comes* *from every side* (*min kulli makānin* — "from every place, from every direction"), and the *death* *does not kill*. And al-Bayḵāwī adds the *point* that makes the *precision* *complete*: *death would be a state of rest from the torment* — the *one* *tormented* *wants* to *die*, because the *death* is the *rest*; and the *one* *tormented* *cannot* *die*, because the *death* is *denied*. The *logic* of the *clause* is the *logic* of the *double* *denial*: the *body* *experiences* *death* (each *part* as *if* it is *dying*), and the *body* *does not die* (the *release* is *denied*). The *torment* is thus *defined* in its *most precise* *form*: the *experience of death without the release of death*.

The *parallel* at 47:15 states the *same* *principle* in the *context* of the *disbelievers'* *drink*:

> "Their drink shall be boiling water that will scorch their bowels." (47:15)

The *two* *descriptions* together state the *full* scope of the *torment*: the *drink* that *scorches* (the *internal* *torment*) and the *death* that *comes from every side* (the *external* *torment*), and the *two* are *united* in the *one* *state*: the *one* who is *tormented* is the *one* who *experiences* *death* *without* the *release* of *death*.

**"And Before Him Lies a Grave Punishment": The Continuation of the Torment**

The final clause — *wa-mā warāʾahu ʿadhābun ʿaẓīm* — "and before him lies a grave punishment" — is the *continuation* of the *torment*. The *word* *warāʾahu* — "before him" — is the *same* *word* as in v. 16, and the *same* *two* *readings* apply: the *punishment* is *ahead* of him, *temporally* (it comes *after* the *current* *torment*) and *spatially* (it is *in front of him*, at the *next* *stage* of the *path*). The *word* *ʿaẓīm* — "grave, great" — is the *word* of the *magnitude*: the *punishment* is not merely *severe*; the *punishment* is *great*, *magnificent* in its *scale*, *beyond* the *measure* of the *current* *torment*. The *logic* of the *clause* is the *logic* of the *continuation*: the *torment* is not a *single* *event*; the *torment* is a *sequence*, and the *sequence* *continues* — the *one* who is *tormented* in the *current* *stage* is the *one* who will be *tormented* in the *next* *stage*.

**The Logic: The Torment as a Sequence, Not a Single Event**

The *logic* of the *verse*, stated in its *full* form, is a *statement* about the *nature* of the *torment*. The *torment* is not a *single* *event*; the *torment* is a *mechanism* (the *double* *constraint* of the *gulp* and the *choking*), a *state* (the *experience of death without the release of death*), and a *sequence* (the *continuation* of the *torment* in the *next* *stage*). The *three* are the *three* *dimensions* of the *same* *reality*: the *torment* is *mechanistic* (it *works* by a *mechanism*), the *torment* is *stative* (it *is* a *state*), and the *torment* is *sequential* (it *continues*). The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *torment* is thus: the *torment* is *precise*, *specific*, and *continuing* — the *three* *qualities* that make the *warning* *effective*.

**The Modern Application: The Precision of the Warning**

The *verse's* teaching on the *precision* of the *warning* is *directly* applicable to the *modern* reader. The *person* who *warns* with *vague* *threats* is the *person* whose *warning* can be *ignored*; the *person* who *warns* with *precise* *descriptions* — the *mechanism*, the *state*, the *sequence* — is the *person* whose *warning* *cannot* be *ignored*. The *precision* of the *description* is the *condition* of the *attention* of the *hearer*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *psychology* of the *warning* is thus: the *warning* is *effective* only when it is *precise*, and the *precision* is the *condition* of the *attention*.
---

## Sūrah Ibrāhīm 14:18

> **The parable of those who disbelieve in their Lord: their deeds are as ashes that the wind blows hard on a stormy day. They have no power over aught they have earned. That is extreme error.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"The Parable of Those Who Disbelieve in Their Lord": The Frame of the Image**

The verse introduces one of the sūrah's central parables with a frame that specifies the *subject* of the image: *mathalu alladhīna kafarū bi-rabbihim* — "The parable of those who disbelieve in their Lord." The word *mathal* — "parable" — is the Qur'an's own word for the *image that carries a lesson*: the parable is not a *decoration*; the parable is a *vehicle* for the *meaning*. And the *frame* specifies the *subject* with precision: the parable is not of *all* people; the parable is of *those who disbelieve in their Lord* — the *disbelief* is *specific*: the *disbelief in the Lord*, the *refusal of the relationship*, the *covering* of the *Giver* in the *blessing* (the *kufr* of v. 7). The sūrah's teaching on the *nature* of the *parable* is thus: the *parable* is a *vehicle* for the *meaning*, and the *meaning* is *specific* to the *subject* — the *disbelievers*, the *ones* who *refuse* the *relationship*.

**"Their Deeds Are as Ashes": The First Stage of the Nullification**

The first clause of the parable — *aʿmāluhum ka-ʿanabin tanāḥāhā rīḥun ʿatīd* — "their deeds are as ashes" — is the *first stage* of the *nullification*. The *word* *ʿanab* — "ashes" — is the *word* of the *remains*: the *ash* is what is *left* after the *fire* has *consumed* the *fuel*. The *deeds* of the *disbelievers* are *ashes* because the *deeds* have *consumed* their *fuel* — the *deeds* have *burned out*, and what is *left* is the *ash*. The *commentators* (al-Rāzī) explain that, *because of their disbelief in God, their deeds become nullified and no trace of them remains* — the *deeds* are *voided* by the *disbelief*, and the *voiding* is expressed in the *image* of the *ash*: the *deed* that has *lost* its *purpose* is the *deed* that has *become* *ash*. The *logic* of the *image* is the *logic* of the *purpose*: the *deed* has a *purpose* (the *relationship* with *God*), and the *deed* that has *lost* its *purpose* is the *deed* that has *become* *ash*.

**"That the Wind Blows Hard on a Stormy Day": The Second Stage of the Nullification**

The second clause — *tanāḥāhā rīḥun ʿatīd* — "that the wind blows hard on a stormy day" — is the *second stage* of the *nullification*. The *word* *ʿatīd* — "stormy, violent" — is the *word* of the *wind* that *blows* *hard*, the *wind* that *scatters*, the *wind* that *leaves* *no trace*. The *ash* is not merely *ash*; the *ash* is *blown away* by the *stormy wind*. The *two stages* together state the *full scope* of the *nullification*: the *deed* does not *produce fruit* (the *ash* — the *first stage*), and *even the ash* does not *remain* (the *blowing away* — the *second stage*). The *logic* of the *two stages* is the *logic* of the *totality*: the *nullification* is *total*, *complete*, *without remainder*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *nullification* is thus: the *nullification* is not a *partial* *loss*; the *nullification* is a *total* *dissolution* — the *deed* does not *bear fruit*, and *even the remains* of the *deed* are *blown away*.

**The Scope of the "Deeds": Good, Evil, or Both**

The *commentators* (al-Rāzī) note that there are *several ways* in which the *deeds* of those who *disbelieve in their Lord* can be *understood* here. The *deeds* can refer to *their good actions* — such as *giving charity* and *feeding the hungry*; to *their evil actions* — such as *worshipping idols*; or to *a combination of both good and bad deeds*. The *logic* of the *three readings* is the *logic* of the *scope*: the *nullification* applies to *all* *deeds*, *good* and *evil* alike, when the *deeds* are *performed* in the *state* of *disbelief*. The *good deed* performed in the *state of disbelief* is *voided* because the *good deed* has *lost* its *purpose* (the *relationship* with *God*); the *evil deed* performed in the *state of disbelief* is *voided* because the *evil deed* is *added* to the *account* of the *disbelief*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *nullification* is thus: the *nullification* is *total* in its *scope* — it applies to *all* *deeds*, *good* and *evil* alike, when the *deeds* are *performed* in the *state* of *disbelief*.

**"They Have No Power Over Aught They Have Earned": The Day of the Account**

The next clause — "They have no power over aught they have earned" — is the *consequence* of the *nullification*. The commentators (al-Bayḵāwī) explain that *on the Day of Judgment, the disbelievers will not receive even a trace of heavenly reward for their actions* — the *deeds* that were *voided* in this *world* are *voided* in the *next*, and the *one* who *performed* the *deeds* has *no power* over the *deeds*, *no claim* on the *deeds*, *no right* to the *deeds*. The parallel at 18:105 states the same principle: the *deeds* of the *disbelievers* are *brought to naught*, and the *one* who *performed* them *receives* *nothing*. The *logic* of the *clause* is the *logic* of the *account*: the *deed* is an *investment*, and the *investment* is made in a *currency* that is *not valid* — the *currency* of the *relationship* with *God* — and the *one* who *invests* in the *invalid currency* *receives* *nothing*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *account* is thus: the *account* is *precise*, and the *precise account* is the *consequence* of the *nullification*.

**"That Is Extreme Error": The Farthest Distance, and the Regret**

The final clause — *dhālika huwa l-ḍallāl al-baʿīd* — "That is extreme error" — is the *verdict* on the *nullification*. The commentators give two readings. The first (al-Bayḵāwī): *extreme error refers to the farthest distance from the path of Truth* — the *error* is not merely *off the path*; the *error* is the *farthest distance* from the *path*, the *one* who is *farthest* from the *path* is the *one* who has *lost* the *path*, the *region* of the *path*, and the *direction* of the *path* together. The second reading (al-Rāzī): *extreme error also refers to the extreme grief and regret that the disbelievers will experience upon realizing that their deeds have gone to waste* — the *error* is the *regret*, the *grief* of the *one* who *realizes* that the *investment* has *been voided*. The *two readings* are not in tension: the *distance* from the *path* (the *first*) and the *regret* upon the *realization* (the *second*) are the *two* *dimensions* of the *same* *reality*: the *one* who is *far* from the *path* is the *one* who *realizes* the *distance*, and the *realization* is the *regret*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *error* is thus: the *error* is the *distance* and the *regret* together — the *one* who is *far* is the *one* who *regrets*, and the *regret* is the *consequence* of the *distance*.

**The Logic: The Parable and Its Positive Counterpart**

The *logic* of the *verse*, stated in its *full* form, is a *statement* about the *nature* of the *parable*. The *parable* of the *ash* (v. 18) is the *negative* *image*; the *parable* of the *good tree* (vv. 24–25) is the *positive* *image*. The *two* *parables* together state the *full scope* of the *sūrah's* teaching on the *deeds*: the *deed* performed in the *state of disbelief* is *ash* (voided, blown away), and the *deed* performed in the *state of faith* is the *good tree* (rooted, fruiting, stable). The *two* *parables* are the *two* *destinies* of the *deed*, and the *destiny* of the *deed* is *determined* by the *state* of the *one* who *performs* it. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *deed* is thus: the *deed* is not *neutral*; the *deed* is *colored* by the *state* of the *one* who *performs* it, and the *color* of the *deed* is the *destiny* of the *deed*.
---

## Sūrah Ibrāhīm 14:19

> **Hast thou not considered that God created the heavens and the earth in truth? If He wills, He can remove you and bring a new creation,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Hast Thou Not Considered": The Invitation to Reflection**

The verse opens with the sūrah's own formula for the invitation to reflection: *aw lam tara* — "Hast thou not considered?" The word *taʿanna* — "to consider, to observe, to look carefully" — is the verb of the *deliberate attention*: the one who *considers* is the one who *looks carefully*, who *examines*, who *does not skim*. The sūrah is inviting the hearer to *apply* the *same* *attention* to the *fact* of the *creation* that the hearer *applies* to the *facts* of *ordinary life*: the hearer *considers* the *business deal*, the *hearer considers* the *medical diagnosis*, the *hearer considers* the *legal argument* — the *hearer* is *capable* of the *consideration*; the *sūrah* is asking the *hearer* to *apply* the *capability* to the *fact* of the *creation*. The logic of the invitation is the logic of the *capability*: the *hearer* is *not* *incapable* of the *consideration*; the *hearer* has *simply not applied* the *capability* to this *object*. The *consideration* is *available*; the *consideration* is *not applied*.

**"God Created the Heavens and the Earth in Truth": The Purpose of the Creation**

The first clause — *khalaqa llāhu s-samāwāti wa l-arḍa bi-l-ḥaqq* — "God created the heavens and the earth in truth" — is the *fact* that the *consideration* is to *examine*. The *word* *bi-l-ḥaqq* — "in truth" — is the *key* to the *clause*. The commentators (al-Māwardī) explain that *bi-l-ḥaqq* means that *God did not create them falsely or in vain; rather, He created them for an exalted purpose* — the *creation* is *not* *false*; the *creation* is *not* *in vain*; the *creation* is *in truth*, and the *truth* of the *creation* is the *purpose* of the *creation*. The *parallel* at 6:73 and 64:3 states the same *principle*: the *creation* is *in truth*, and the *truth* of the *creation* is the *purpose*. The *complement* at 23:115 states the *purpose* explicitly:

> "Did you suppose, then, that We created you frivolously, and that you would not be returned unto Us?" (23:115)

The *logic* of the *clause* is the *logic* of the *purpose*: the *creation* is *in truth* because the *creation* has a *purpose*, and the *purpose* is *not* *frivolous*. The *one* who *considers* the *creation* *in truth* is the *one* who *recognizes* the *purpose* of the *creation*, and the *purpose* is the *return* to *God* (the *resurrection*, the *account*, the *judgment*). The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *creation* is thus: the *creation* is *not* *frivolous*; the *creation* is *purposeful*, and the *purpose* is the *return* to *God*.

**"If He Wills, He Can Remove You and Bring a New Creation": The Power of Re-Creation**

The second clause — *in yashāʾ yukhlijkum yā ʿibādi wa-yāʾtī bi-khalqin jadīd* — "If He wills, He can remove you and bring a new creation" — is the *consequence* of the *purpose*. The commentators (al-Bayḵāwī) explain that the *words* mean that *God can make the present creation nonexistent and bring about another creation in its place if He so wills, because He is the Creator of the heavens and the earth* — the *power* of the *Creator* is the *power* to *remove* and *replace*. The *word* *yakhlij* — "to remove, to make empty" — is the verb of the *total removal*: the *present creation* is *removed*, *made empty*, *replaced*. The *word* *jadīd* — "new" — is the word of the *replacement*: the *new creation* is *not* a *modification* of the *old*; the *new creation* is a *replacement* of the *old*. The *logic* of the *clause* is the *logic* of the *power*: the *power* of the *Creator* is the *power* to *remove* and *replace*, and the *power* is *not limited* by the *scale* of the *creation*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *power* is thus: the *power* of the *Creator* is *total*, and the *totality* of the *power* is *demonstrated* by the *power* to *remove* and *replace*.

**The Personal Address: "You" Can Be Removed**

The *clause* is addressed *personally*: *yukhlijkum* — "He can remove *you*" — the *hearer* is *within* the *scope* of the *power*. The *Creator* can *remove* *the hearer*, and *replace* *the hearer* with a *new creation*. The *logic* of the *personal address* is the *logic* of the *relevance*: the *fact* of the *creation* is not an *abstract* *cosmological* *fact*; the *fact* of the *creation* is *personal*, *addressed* to the *hearer*. The *hearer* is *within* the *scope* of the *power*, and the *power* can *remove* the *hearer*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *fact* of the *creation* is thus: the *fact* is *personal*, and the *personal* *fact* is the *condition* of the *attention*.

**The Cross-References: The Power of Re-Creation Throughout the Book**

The *principle* of the *power* to *remove* and *replace* recurs throughout the *Book* as a *standing* *declaration* of the *divine* *power*: 4:133, 6:133, 35:16, 47:38, and the *parallel* at 5:54:

> "And if they turn away, We shall appoint for thee a people other than them; and then they shall not be able to harm thee in any way." (5:54)

The *two* *dimensions* of the *power* are *stated* in the *two* *scales*: the *scale* of the *nations* (the *removal* and *replacement* of a *people*) and the *scale* of the *creation* (the *removal* and *replacement* of the *entire creation*). The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *power* is thus: the *power* is *total* in its *scope*, and the *totality* is *stated* in the *two* *scales*.

**The Logic: The Purpose and the Power Together**

The *logic* of the *verse*, stated in its *full* form, is a *statement* about the *two* *aspects* of the *creation*. The *first aspect* is the *purpose*: the *creation* is *in truth*, and the *purpose* is the *return* to *God*. The *second aspect* is the *power*: the *Creator* can *remove* and *replace* the *creation*. The *two* *aspects* together state the *full* scope of the *creation*: the *creation* is *purposeful* (the *first*), and the *creation* is *re-creatable* (the *second*). The *purpose* is the *reason* for the *creation*; the *power* is the *guarantee* of the *purpose*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *creation* is thus: the *creation* is *purposeful* and *re-creatable*, and the *two* together are the *condition* of the *attention* of the *hearer*.
---

## Sūrah Ibrāhīm 14:20

> **and that is no great matter for God.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"No Great Matter for God": The Ease of the Total Power**

The verse is the close of the thought begun in v. 19, and it states the *ease* of the *power* that v. 19 stated. The clause *wa-mā ẓālika ʿalā llāhi bi-ḥāqir* — "and that is no great matter for God" — is a *double negative* in the Arabic: *lā ... bi-ḥāqir* — "it is not a *light* thing for God" — which is the Arabic's own way of saying that the thing is *easy*, *not difficult*, *not impossible* for God. The commentators (al-Bayḵāwī, al-Māwardī) explain that the *words* mean that *it is not difficult or impossible for God to bring about a new creation* — rather, *it is easy for Him, since He is All-Powerful by nature* (al-Bayḵāwī, al-Māwardī). The *logic* of the *clause* is the *logic* of the *ease*: the *power* of v. 19 (the *power* to *remove* and *replace* the *creation*) is not a *power* that is *strained*; the *power* is a *power* that is *easy*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *power* is thus: the *power* is *total* (v. 19) and the *power* is *easy* (v. 20) — the *two* together are the *full* description of the *divine* power.

**The Parallel: "His Command Is Only to Say 'Be' and It Is"**

The *principle* of the *ease* is *stated* elsewhere in the *Book* in its *most precise* form:

> "His command, when He intends a thing, is only to say 'Be,' and it is." (36:82)

The *two* *verses* together state the *full* scope of the *ease*: the *power* to *create* (v. 19) and the *ease* of the *power* (v. 20), and the *ease* is *expressed* in the *form* of the *command* — the *command* is *only* to *say* "Be," and the *thing* *is*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *ease* is thus: the *ease* is not the *absence* of the *power*; the *ease* is the *form* of the *power* — the *power* is *easy* because the *power* is the *command*, and the *command* is *only* to *say* "Be."

**The Logic: Power and Ease Together**

The *logic* of the *verse*, stated in its *full* form, is a *statement* about the *two* *aspects* of the *divine* power. The *first aspect* is the *power* (v. 19): the *power* to *remove* and *replace* the *creation*. The *second aspect* is the *ease* (v. 20): the *ease* of the *power*, the *ease* of the *command*. The *two* *aspects* together state the *full* scope of the *power*: the *power* is *total* in its *scope* (the *removal* and *replacement* of the *entire creation*), and the *power* is *easy* in its *exercise* (the *command* is *only* to *say* "Be"). The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *power* is thus: the *power* is *total* and *easy*, and the *two* together are the *condition* of the *attention* of the *hearer*.

**The Human "Impossible" and the Divine "Easy"**

The *verse's* teaching on the *ease* is *directly* applicable to the *modern* reader. The *human* sense of the *impossible* is a *measure* of the *human* *limitation*, not a *measure* of the *divine* *limitation*. The *one* who *measures* the *divine* power by the *human* *standard* is the *one* who *mistakes* the *human* *limitation* for the *divine* *limitation*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *psychology* of the *impossible* is thus: the *impossible* is the *human* *word* for the *human* *limitation*, and the *easy* is the *divine* word for the *divine* *power*. The *one* who *recognizes* the *distinction* between the *two* is the *one* who *recognizes* the *ease* of the *divine* power.

**The Frame: The Creation is Purposeful and Re-Creatable, and the Power is Easy**

The *two* *verses* (vv. 19–20) together state the *full* scope of the *creation* and the *power*: the *creation* is *purposeful* (v. 19: *in truth*), the *creation* is *re-creatable* (v. 19: *remove and replace*), and the *power* is *easy* (v. 20: *no great matter*). The *three* *statements* together are the *condition* of the *attention* of the *hearer*: the *hearer* who *considers* the *three* is the *hearer* who *recognizes* the *purpose* of the *creation*, the *power* of the *Creator*, and the *ease* of the *power*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *consideration* is thus: the *consideration* is the *application* of the *attention* to the *three* *statements*, and the *application* is the *condition* of the *recognition*.
---

## Sūrah Ibrāhīm 14:21

> **They will appear before God all together, and the weak will say to those who waxed arrogant, "Truly we were your followers; so can you avail us in any way against the Punishment of God?" They will say, "Had God guided us, we would have guided you. It is the same for us whether we are anxious or patient; for us there is no refuge."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"They Will Appear Before God All Together": The Assembly of the Reckoning**

The verse shifts the scene from the *description* of the *punishment* (vv. 16–17) and the *parable* of the *ash* (v. 18) to the *assembly* of the *reckoning*. The clause — "They will appear before God all together" — is the *setting* of the *scene*. The commentators (al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭūsī) explain that the *assembly* is on the *Day of Judgment*, *when they come forth from their graves* — the *jamīʿ* — "all together" — is the *word* of the *totality*: *all* the *people* *appear* before *God*, *all together*, *without exception*. The *logic* of the *assembly* is the *logic* of the *account*: the *account* is *not* a *partial* *account*; the *account* is a *total* *account*, and the *total account* requires the *total* *assembly*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *account* is thus: the *account* is *total* in its *scope*, and the *total scope* is the *condition* of the *total assembly*.

**"The Weak and Those Who Wax Arrogant": The Social Structure of the Reckoning**

The *scene* is *populated* by *two* *groups*: the *weak* (*adh-ḍaʿīf*) and *those who waxed arrogant* (*al-mustakbirīn*). The commentators (al-Qurṭubī) explain that *the weak represent those who were the followers of those who waxed arrogant, that is, their leaders* — the *weak* are the *followers*, and the *arrogant* are the *leaders*. The *social structure* of the *scene* is the *social structure* of the *world*: the *followers* and the *leaders*, the *weak* and the *arrogant*. The *sūrah* is *showing* the *social structure* of the *world* *in the* *scene* of the *reckoning*: the *followers* *appeal* to the *leaders*, and the *leaders* *answer* the *followers*. The *logic* of the *structure* is the *logic* of the *responsibility*: the *followers* *entrusted* their *judgment* to the *leaders* in this *world*; the *followers* *ask* the *leaders* to *avail* them in the *next*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *social structure* is thus: the *social structure* of the *world* is *reproduced* in the *scene* of the *reckoning*, and the *reproduction* is the *condition* of the *account*.

**"Truly We Were Your Followers; So Can You Avail Us?": The Appeal of the Followers**

The *words* of the *followers* — "Truly we were your followers; so can you avail us in any way against the Punishment of God?" — are the *appeal* of the *followers* to the *leaders*. The *first* clause — *innā kunnā lakum tabiʿīn* — "Truly we were your followers" — is the *statement* of the *fact*: the *followers* *followed* the *leaders*. The *second* clause — *fa-hal tunkithūna min ʿadhābi llāhi min ḥāq* — "so can you avail us in any way against the Punishment of God?" — is the *appeal*: the *followers* *ask* the *leaders* to *avail* them, to *help* them, to *remove* the *punishment* of *God* *from* them. The *logic* of the *appeal* is the *logic* of the *entrustment*: the *followers* *entrusted* their *judgment* to the *leaders* in this *world*; the *followers* *ask* the *leaders* to *bear* the *consequence* of the *entrustment* in the *next*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *psychology* of the *appeal* is thus: the *appeal* is the *consequence* of the *entrustment*, and the *entrustment* is the *condition* of the *appeal*.

**"Had God Guided Us, We Would Have Guided You": The Answer of the Leaders**

The *answer* of the *leaders* — *qālū law hadānā llāhu la-hadānakum* — "Had God guided us, we would have guided you" — is the *refusal* of the *appeal*. The *logic* of the *answer* is the *logic* of the *symmetry*: the *leaders* *could not* *guide* the *followers* *without* being *guided* *themselves*; the *leaders* *cannot* *save* the *followers* *without* being *saved* *themselves*. The *two* are the *same* thing seen from the *two* directions: the *guidance* of the *followers* *depended* on the *guidance* of the *leaders* in this *world*; the *salvation* of the *followers* *depends* on the *salvation* of the *leaders* in the *next*. The *leaders* *answer* the *appeal* by *stating* the *symmetry*: *had God guided us, we would have guided you* — the *condition* of the *guidance* of the *followers* is the *guidance* of the *leaders*, and the *leaders* were *not guided*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *guidance* is thus: the *guidance* is *not* *transferable*; the *guidance* is *personal*, and the *personal* guidance of the *leader* is the *condition* of the *guidance* of the *follower*.

**"It Is the Same Whether We Are Anxious or Patient; For Us There Is No Refuge": The Finality of the State**

The *final* clause of the *answer* — "It is the same for us whether we are anxious or patient; for us there is no refuge" — is the *statement* of the *finality*. The *logic* of the *clause* is the *logic* of the *futility*: the *anxiety* and the *patience* are the *two* *responses* to the *state*; the *state* is *such* that the *two* *responses* are *the same* — the *anxiety* does not *change* the *state*, and the *patience* does not *change* the *state*. The *word* *mūnūn* — "the lost ones" — is the *word* of the *total loss*: the *leaders* are *lost*, and the *loss* is *total*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *finality* is thus: the *finality* is the *state* in which the *responses* are *futile*, and the *futility* of the *responses* is the *condition* of the *total loss*.

**The Wider Context: The Argument in the Fire**

The *verse* provides a *wider context* for the *parallel* at 40:47–48:

> "And when they are arguing with one another in the Fire, the weak will say to those who waxed arrogant, 'Truly we were your followers; so can you avail us against any portion of the Fire?' Those who waxed arrogant will say, 'Truly we are all in it. God has indeed judged between His servants.'" (40:47–48)

The *two* *verses* together state the *full* scope of the *argument* in the *Fire*: the *appeal* of the *followers* (v. 21, 40:47) and the *answer* of the *leaders* (v. 21, 40:48). The *answer* at 40:48 — *Truly we are all in it* — is the *confirmation* of the *symmetry*: the *leaders* are *in* the *Fire* *with* the *followers*, and the *judgment* of *God* is the *final* word. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *argument* is thus: the *argument* is the *consequence* of the *entrustment*, and the *entrustment* is the *condition* of the *argument*.

**The Modern Application: The Psychology of Following and the Responsibility of Leadership**

The *verse's* teaching on the *psychology* of *following* is *directly* applicable to the *modern* reader. The *follower* who *entrusts* his *judgment* to the *leader* is the *follower* who *appeals* to the *leader* at the *moment* of the *reckoning*. The *leader* who *leads* the *followers* into *error* is the *leader* who *answers* the *appeal* by *stating* the *symmetry*: *had God guided us, we would have guided you*. The *two* together state the *full* scope of the *responsibility*: the *responsibility* of the *leader* (the *guidance* of the *followers* depends on the *guidance* of the *leader*) and the *responsibility* of the *follower* (the *judgment* of the *follower* is *personal*, and the *personal judgment* is *not transferable*). The *sūrah's* teaching on the *psychology* of the *social structure* is thus: the *social structure* of the *world* is *reproduced* in the *scene* of the *reckoning*, and the *reproduction* is the *condition* of the *account*.
---

## Sūrah Ibrāhīm 14:22

> **And Satan will say, when the matter has been decreed, "Verily God made you the Promise of truth; and I made you a promise, but I failed you. And I had no authority over you, save that I called you, and you responded to me. So do not blame me, but blame yourselves. I cannot respond to your cries for help; nor can you respond to my cries for help. Truly I disbelieved in your ascribing me as partner aforetime. As for the wrongdoers, surely theirs shall be a painful punishment."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"When the Matter Has Been Decreed": The Setting of the Scene**

The verse shifts the scene to the *final* *moment* of the *reckoning*: *idhā tuqmila l-ʿamālu* — "when the matter has been decreed." The commentators (al-Bayḵāwī, al-Kalbī) explain that the *phrase* refers to the *Hereafter*, *after people have taken their places of residency in Heaven or Hell* — the *matter* is *decreed*, the *judgment* is *final*, the *places* are *assigned*. The *logic* of the *setting* is the *logic* of the *finality*: the *scene* is *set* in the *moment* of the *finality*, and the *finality* is the *condition* of the *words* that follow. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *scene* is thus: the *scene* is the *final* *scene*, and the *finality* is the *condition* of the *words*.

**"God Made You the Promise of Truth": The Divine Promise**

The *first* *word* of *Satan* — *inna llāha waʿadakum waʿda l-ḥaqq* — "Verily God made you the Promise of truth" — is the *statement* of the *divine* promise. The *commentators* (al-Bayḵāwī) explain that the *Promise* that *God made* here refers to the *Resurrection* and the *meting out of rewards on the Last Day* — the *promise* is the *truth*, the *promise* is the *reality*, the *promise* is the *one* that *will be fulfilled*. The *logic* of the *statement* is the *logic* of the *acknowledgment*: *Satan* *acknowledges* the *divine* promise, and the *acknowledgment* is the *condition* of the *contrast* that follows. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *acknowledgment* is thus: the *acknowledgment* of the *divine* promise is the *condition* of the *contrast* with the *diabolic* promise.

**"I Made You a Promise, but I Failed You": The Diabolic Promise and Its Failure**

The *second* *word* of *Satan* — *wa-waʿadtukumunna fa-akhlaftukum* — "and I made you a promise, but I failed you" — is the *statement* of the *diabolic* promise and its *failure*.
