The *commentators* (al-Bayḵāwī, al-Ṭūsī) explain that *Satan's promise, which failed—taken to mean that he lied—refers to his promise to God's servants that the Resurrection and accounting on the Last Day are untrue, and that if these are realities, then the idols shall act as their intercessors* — the *promise* is the *lie*, the *promise* is the *delusion*, the *promise* is the *one* that *failed*. The *parallel* at 4:120 states the *principle* explicitly:

> "He makes them promises and stirs in them desires, but Satan promises naught but delusion." (4:120)

The *logic* of the *contrast* is the *logic* of the *two* promises: the *divine* promise is the *truth* (it *will be fulfilled*), and the *diabolic* promise is the *lie* (it *failed*). The *Satan* *acknowledges* the *failure* of his *own* promise, and the *acknowledgment* is the *condition* of the *blame* that follows. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *promise* is thus: the *promise* is *either* the *truth* (the *divine* promise) or the *lie* (the *diabolic* promise), and the *truth* *will be fulfilled*, and the *lie* *will fail*.

**"I Had No Authority Over You, Save That I Called You, and You Responded to Me": The Limit of the Diabolic Power**

The *third* *word* of *Satan* — *wa-mā kāna lī ʿalaykum min sultānin illā an daʿaftum fa-stajabtum* — "And I had no authority over you, save that I called you, and you responded to me" — is the *statement* of the *limit* of the *diabolic* power. The *commentators* (al-Ṭabrisī) explain that the *phrase* means: *"I had no power to force or compel you to disbelieve and disobey—I only had the means of whispering and calling."* The *logic* of the *statement* is the *logic* of the *limit*: *Satan* *had no power* to *compel*; *Satan* *had only* the *means* of *whispering* and *calling*. The *parallel* at 4:119 and 114:4 states the *principle*: *Satan* can *only* *lead* God's *servants* astray *by inciting them to evil actions by means of whispering or suggesting evil*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *diabolic* power is thus: the *diabolic* power is *not* the *power* of *compulsion*; the *diabolic* power is the *power* of *suggestion*, and the *power* of *suggestion* is the *limit* of the *diabolic* power.

**"So Do Not Blame Me, but Blame Yourselves": The Refusal of the Scapegoat**

The *fourth* *word* of *Satan* — *fa-lā talūmūnī wa-lūmū anfusakum* — "So do not blame me, but blame yourselves" — is the *refusal* of the *scapegoat*. The *commentators* (al-Ṭūsī, Ibn Kathīr) explain that the *phrase* means that *they did not have firm proof or evidence to disobey God and follow Satan*, especially *since they had been shown the truth by God's messengers* (Ibn Kathīr). The *logic* of the *refusal* is the *logic* of the *responsibility*: *Satan* *refuses* to *bear* the *responsibility*; *Satan* *returns* the *responsibility* to the *one* who *followed* him. The *responsibility* is *personal*, and the *personal* responsibility is *not transferable*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *responsibility* is thus: the *responsibility* is *personal*, and the *personal* responsibility is the *condition* of the *account*.

**"I Cannot Respond to Your Cries; Nor Can You Respond to Mine": The Mutual Helplessness**

The *fifth* *word* of *Satan* — "I cannot respond to your cries for help; nor can you respond to my cries for help" — is the *statement* of the *mutual* helplessness. The *commentators* (Ibn Kathīr) explain that *Satan cannot benefit or save in any manner those who succumbed to his whisperings*, since *individuals will only be taken to task for their own wrong actions and their own choice to heed or not heed Satan's call* — for the *Quranic theme of personal accountability for one's actions*, see 74:38. The *parallel* at 8:48 and 59:16 states the *same* *principle*. The *logic* of the *statement* is the *logic* of the *mutuality*: *Satan* *cannot* *save* the *one* who *followed* him, and the *one* who *followed* *Satan* *cannot* *save* *Satan*. The *two* are *mutually* helpless, and the *mutual* helplessness is the *condition* of the *personal* responsibility. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *helplessness* is thus: the *helplessness* is *mutual*, and the *mutual* helplessness is the *condition* of the *personal* responsibility.

**"I Disbelieved in Your Ascribing Me as Partner Aforetime": The Rejection of the Association**

The *sixth* *word* of *Satan* — "Truly I disbelieved in your ascribing me as partner aforetime" — is the *rejection* of the *association*. The *commentators* (Āl al-Kāsī) explain that the *phrase* refers to *his rejection even before the Day of Judgment, in the life of this world, of his being taken as a partner alongside God*. The *alternate* reading (al-Ṭūsī) is that *aforetime* can refer to the *time when Satan was commanded to bow before Adam but refused to do so on account of his pride* (7:12), in which case the *verse* would be *read*: *"I disbelieved in that which you ascribed to me as a partner"* — *Satan had already rejected God*. The *logic* of the *rejection* is the *logic* of the *pride*: *Satan* *rejects* the *association* because the *association* is an *honor* that *Satan* does not *deserve*, and the *honor* is *not* the *one* that *Satan* *seeks*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *rejection* is thus: the *rejection* is the *consequence* of the *pride*, and the *pride* is the *condition* of the *rejection*.

**"As for the Wrongdoers, Surely Theirs Shall Be a Painful Punishment": The Final Word**

The *final* *word* of *Satan* — *fa-inna l-ẓālimīna la-hum ʿadhābun alīm* — "As for the wrongdoers, surely theirs shall be a painful punishment" — is the *final* word of the *scene*. The *logic* of the *word* is the *logic* of the *finality*: the *punishment* is the *final* word, and the *finality* is the *condition* of the *punishment*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *final word* is thus: the *final word* is the *punishment*, and the *punishment* is the *condition* of the *finality*.

**The Modern Application: The Psychology of Blame and the Responsibility of Influence**

The *verse's* teaching on the *psychology* of *blame* is *directly* applicable to the *modern* reader. The *one* who *follows* the *influence* of the *other* is the *one* who *appeals* to the *other* at the *moment* of the *reckoning*. The *one* who *influences* the *other* is the *one* who *refuses* the *appeal* by *stating* the *limit* of the *influence*: *I had no authority over you, save that I called you, and you responded to me*. The *two* together state the *full* scope of the *responsibility*: the *responsibility* of the *influencer* (the *influence* is the *suggestion*, not the *compulsion*) and the *responsibility* of the *one* influenced (the *response* is *personal*, and the *personal* response is *not transferable*). The *sūrah's* teaching on the *psychology* of the *blame* is thus: the *blame* is *refused* by the *influencer*, and the *refusal* is the *condition* of the *personal* responsibility.
---

## Sūrah Ibrāhīm 14:23

> **Those who believe and perform righteous deeds shall be made to enter Gardens with rivers running below, abiding therein by the leave of their Lord. Their greeting therein shall be, "Peace!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Those Who Believe and Perform Righteous Deeds": The Standard Formula**

The verse shifts the scene from the *final word* of *Satan* (v. 22) to the *destiny* of the *believers*. The *subject* of the *verse* is the *standard* formula of the *Book*: *alladhīna āmanū wa-ʿamilū ṣ-ṣāliḥāt* — "Those who believe and perform righteous deeds." The *formula* is the *combination* of the *two*: the *belief* (the *internal* state) and the *righteous deeds* (the *external* action). The *logic* of the *formula* is the *logic* of the *completeness*: the *belief* without the *deed* is *incomplete*, and the *deed* without the *belief* is *void* (the *ash* of v. 18). The *two* together are the *condition* of the *destiny*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *formula* is thus: the *formula* is the *combination* of the *belief* and the *deed*, and the *combination* is the *condition* of the *destiny*.

**"Gardens with Rivers Running Below": The Destination**

The *destination* is stated with the *standard* image of the *Book*: *jannāti ʿadna tajrī min taḥtihā l-anhār* — "Gardens with rivers running below." The *word* *jannāt* — "Gardens" — is the *word* of the *enclosure*, the *garden* that is *enclosed*, the *garden* that is *protected*, the *garden* that is *the opposite* of the *desert*. The *word* *ʿadn* — from the *root* *ʿ-d-n*, "to dwell, to remain" — is the *word* of the *abode*: the *garden* of *ʿadn* is the *garden* of *abiding*, the *garden* in which the *one* *dwells*. The *rivers* *running* *below* are the *sign* of the *abundance*: the *water* is *below* the *garden*, the *water* *rises* to the *garden*, the *water* *irrigates* the *garden*. The *logic* of the *image* is the *logic* of the *abundance*: the *garden* is *abundant* because the *water* is *below* it, and the *water* *rises* to the *garden*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *destination* is thus: the *destination* is the *abundant* garden, and the *abundance* is the *condition* of the *abiding*.

**"Abiding Therein by the Leave of Their Lord": The Frame of the Sūrah**

The *clause* *muqīmīna bi-idhni rabbihim* — "abiding therein by the leave of their Lord" — is the *frame* of the *sūrah*. The *word* *bi-idhni rabbihim* — "by the leave of their Lord" — is the *same* phrase as in v. 1 (*bi-idhni rabbihim* — "by the leave of their Lord"). The *sūrah* *opens* with the *extraction* from *darkness* into *light* *by the leave of their Lord* (v. 1), and the *sūrah* *closes* this *section* with the *abiding* in the *garden* *by the leave of their Lord* (v. 23). The *two* *together* state the *full* scope of the *leave*: the *entry* into the *light* is *by leave*, and the *abiding* in the *garden* is *by leave*. The *logic* of the *frame* is the *logic* of the *continuity*: the *leave* is not a *one-time* event; the *leave* is a *continuing* state. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *leave* is thus: the *leave* is the *condition* of the *entry* and the *abiding*, and the *two* together are the *full* scope of the *leave*.

**"Their Greeting Therein Shall Be, 'Peace!': The Language of Paradise**

The *final* clause — *wa-salāmūn* — "Their greeting therein shall be, 'Peace!'" — is the *statement* of the *language* of *Paradise*. The *word* *salām* — "Peace" — is the *word* of the *safety*, the *word* of the *completion*, the *word* of the *state* in which there is *no fear*, *no grief*, *no harm*. The *greeting* of *Paradise* is the *greeting* of *Peace*: the *one* who *enters* the *garden* is *greeted* with *Peace*, and the *greeting* is the *condition* of the *abiding*. The *parallel* at 36:58 states the *same* *greeting* in its *full* form:

> "And [their greeting will be] peace — a word from a merciful Lord." (36:58)

And the *parallel* at 56:26 states the *greeting* in its *doubled* form:

> "Peace, peace — a word from a merciful Lord." (56:26)

The *two* *parables* together state the *full* scope of the *greeting*: the *greeting* is *Peace*, and the *Peace* is the *word* of the *merciful Lord*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *language* of *Paradise* is thus: the *language* of *Paradise* is the *language* of *Peace*, and the *Peace* is the *word* of the *merciful Lord*.

**The Contrast: The Language of the Fire and the Language of the Garden**

The *verse* stands in *contrast* with the *language* of the *Fire* described in vv. 21–22. The *language* of the *Fire* is the *language* of the *regret* (v. 21: *Had God guided us, we would have guided you*) and the *language* of the *blame* (v. 22: *So do not blame me, but blame yourselves*). The *language* of the *Garden* is the *language* of *Peace* (v. 23: *Their greeting therein shall be, "Peace!"*). The *two* *languages* together state the *full* scope of the *destinies*: the *language* of the *Fire* is the *language* of the *regret* and the *blame*, and the *language* of the *Garden* is the *language* of *Peace*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *languages* is thus: the *languages* are the *two* *destinies*, and the *destinies* are the *consequences* of the *choices*.

**The Modern Application: The Language as the State**

The *verse's* teaching on the *language* of *Paradise* is *directly* applicable to the *modern* reader. The *language* of the *one* is the *state* of the *one*: the *one* whose *language* is the *language* of the *Peace* is the *one* whose *state* is the *state* of the *Peace*, and the *one* whose *language* is the *language* of the *regret* and the *blame* is the *one* whose *state* is the *state* of the *regret* and the *blame*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *psychology* of the *language* is thus: the *language* is the *expression* of the *state*, and the *state* is the *condition* of the *language*.
---

## Sūrah Ibrāhīm 14:24

> **Hast thou not considered how God sets forth a parable? A good word is as a good tree: its roots firm and its branches in the sky.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Hast Thou Not Considered How God Sets Forth a Parable?": The Invitation to the Image**

The verse opens with the *sūrah's* own formula for the *invitation* to *reflection*: *aw lam tara kaifa yamthu llāhu* — "Hast thou not considered how God sets forth a parable?" The *word* *mathal* — "parable" — is the *word* of the *image that carries a lesson*. The *sūrah* is *inviting* the *hearer* to *consider* the *image* that *God* *sets forth*. The *logic* of the *invitation* is the *logic* of the *capability*: the *hearer* is *capable* of the *consideration*, and the *consideration* is the *condition* of the *recognition*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *parable* is thus: the *parable* is the *image* that *carries* the *lesson*, and the *lesson* is the *condition* of the *recognition*.

**"A Good Word Is as a Good Tree": The Image and Its Meaning**

The *first* clause of the *parable* — *mathalu kalimatin ṭayyibatin ka-shajaratin ṭayyibah* — "A good word is as a good tree" — is the *statement* of the *image*. The *commentators* (al-Qurṭubī) understand *kalimati ṭayyibah* — "a good word" — to refer here to the *formula of the shahādah*, *"There is no god but God"* (*lā ilāha illā llāh*) — the *word* that is the *foundation* of the *faith*, the *word* that is the *root* of the *belief*. The *word* *ṭayyibah* — "good" — is the *word* of the *purity*, the *word* of the *state* in which there is *no impurity*, *no corruption*, *no falsehood*. The *good word* is the *word* that is *pure*, *true*, and *the foundation* of the *belief*. The *logic* of the *image* is the *logic* of the *correspondence*: the *good word* *corresponds* to the *good tree*, and the *correspondence* is the *condition* of the *recognition*.

**"Its Roots Firm": The Depth of the Foundation**

The *first* *attribute* of the *good tree* — *ṣadīdatu l-jidhr* — "its roots firm" — is the *statement* of the *depth* of the *foundation*. The *word* *jidhr* — "root" — is the *word* of the *depth*, the *word* of the *part* of the *tree* that is *in the earth*, the *part* that is *hidden*, the *part* that *holds* the *tree*. The *root* that is *firm* is the *root* that *holds* the *tree* *firmly*, the *root* that *does not* *move*, the *root* that *is the condition* of the *stability* of the *tree*. The *logic* of the *attribute* is the *logic* of the *depth*: the *stability* of the *tree* *depends* on the *depth* of the *root*, and the *depth* of the *root* is the *condition* of the *stability*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *foundation* is thus: the *foundation* is the *root*, and the *root* is the *condition* of the *stability*.

**"And Its Branches in the Sky": The Height of the Reach**

The *second* *attribute* of the *good tree* — *wa-shajaruhā fī s-samāʾ* — "and its branches in the sky" — is the *statement* of the *height* of the *reach*. The *word* *shajar* — "branches" — is the *word* of the *part* of the *tree* that is *in the air*, the *part* that *reaches*, the *part* that *bears* the *fruit*. The *branch* that is *in the sky* is the *branch* that *reaches* *high*, the *branch* that *is the condition* of the *fruit*. The *logic* of the *attribute* is the *logic* of the *reach*: the *fruit* of the *tree* *depends* on the *reach* of the *branch*, and the *reach* of the *branch* is the *condition* of the *fruit*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *reach* is thus: the *reach* is the *branch*, and the *branch* is the *condition* of the *fruit*.

**The Date Palm: The Prophetic Identification of the Tree**

The *tree* of the *parable* is *identified* in a *famous report* *narrated* by *ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar*, the *son* of the *second Caliph* *ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb*: the *Prophet* ﷺ *asked* his *Companions* if they *knew* what *kind* of *tree* was *being referred to* in this *verse*. *Ibn ʿUmar* *knew* the *answer*, but *was too shy to speak* in *front* of his *elders*. Then the *Prophet* *revealed* the *answer*, and *it was just as* *Ibn ʿUmar* *had thought*: *it was a date palm*. The *report* is *preserved* by *al-Bayhaqī* and *Ibn Kathīr* *quote* it in *his* *commentary* on this *verse*. The *date palm* is the *tree* of the *parable* because the *date palm* is the *tree* that is *firmly rooted* in the *earth* and *reaches* *high* in the *sky*, and the *date palm* is the *tree* that *bears* *fruit* *in every season*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *parable* is thus: the *parable* is the *date palm*, and the *date palm* is the *tree* that *corresponds* to the *good word*.

**The Spiritual Reading: The Tree of Knowledge Rooted in the Heart**

The *commentators* (al-Rāzī) give the *parable* its *spiritual* reading: *when the tree of knowledge is firmly rooted in the land of one's heart, one becomes stronger and more complete, thereby enabling "fruit" to issue forth from its "branches" in abundance*. The *tree of knowledge*, *rooted* in the *land of the *heart*, has *branches* that *are in the sky*: the *forms of knowledge* that *reach* the *Divine realm*. The *tree* *thus* *brings forth fruit* *in every season* (v. 25): the *soul* of a *person* who is *firmly rooted* in *knowledge* develops a *spiritual disposition* through which it *produces* "fruit" *perpetually* in the *form* of *beautiful words, righteous actions, a state of humility, self-effacement, weeping, and lowliness*. The *logic* of the *reading* is the *logic* of the *correspondence*: the *root* in the *heart* *corresponds* to the *firmness* of the *faith*, the *branch* in the *sky* *corresponds* to the *reach* of the *knowledge*, and the *fruit* *corresponds* to the *righteous action*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *parable* is thus: the *parable* is the *tree* of *knowledge*, and the *tree* of *knowledge* is the *condition* of the *fruit*.

**The Logic: The Good Word and the Good Tree**

The *logic* of the *verse*, stated in its *full* form, is a *statement* about the *correspondence* between the *good word* and the *good tree*. The *good word* is the *foundation* of the *belief* (the *shahādah*), and the *good tree* is the *image* of the *foundation*. The *root* of the *tree* is the *depth* of the *foundation*, and the *branch* of the *tree* is the *reach* of the *foundation*. The *two* together state the *full* scope of the *foundation*: the *foundation* is *deep* (the *root*), and the *foundation* is *high* (the *branch*). The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *foundation* is thus: the *foundation* is *deep* and *high*, and the *two* together are the *condition* of the *fruit*.
---

## Sūrah Ibrāhīm 14:25

> **It brings forth fruit in every season, by the Leave of its Lord. God sets forth parables for mankind, that haply they may remember.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"It Brings Forth Fruit in Every Season": The Continuity of the Good Word**

The verse continues the parable of the good tree begun in v. 24, and the continuation is the *statement* of the *continuity*. The clause *tusʿīku l-thamarata kulla ʿamrin* — "It brings forth fruit in every season" — is the *third* attribute of the good tree (after the *firm root* and the *high branch* of v. 24). The *word* *ʿamrin* — "season" — is the word of the *period*, the *word* of the *time* in which the *tree* bears *fruit*. The *fruit* is *not* a *one-time* event; the *fruit* is *continuous*, the *fruit* is *in every season*. The *logic* of the *continuity* is the *logic* of the *sustained* production: the *good word* does *not* produce *fruit* *once*; the *good word* *produces* fruit *continuously*, *in every season*, *without cessation*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *good word* is thus: the *good word* is *sustained* in its *production*, and the *sustained production* is the *condition* of the *continuity* of the *benefit*.

**"By the Leave of Its Lord": The Frame of the Sūrah, Restated**

The *clause* *bi-idhn rabbihā* — "by the Leave of its Lord" — is the *frame* of the *sūrah*, *restated*. The *phrase* *bi-idhn rabbih* — "by the leave of the Lord" — appears in v. 1 (the *extraction* from *darkness* into *light* *by the leave of their Lord*), in v. 23 (the *abiding* in the *garden* *by the leave of their Lord*), and now in v. 25 (the *fruiting* of the *good tree* *by the leave of its Lord*). The *three* *occurrences* together state the *full scope of the leave*: the *entry* into the *light* is *by leave*, the *abiding* in the *garden* is *by leave*, and the *fruiting* of the *good word* is *by leave*. The *logic* of the *frame* is the *logic* of the *continuity*: the *leave* is *not* a *one-time* event; the *leave* is a *continuing* state. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *leave* is thus: the *leave* is the *condition* of the *entry*, the *abiding*, and the *fruiting*, and the *three* together are the *full scope of the leave*.

**The Spiritual Reading: The Perpetual Production of the Soul**

The *commentators* (al-Rāzī) give the *verse* its *spiritual* reading: *this tree thus brings forth fruit in every season, which is to say that the soul of a person who is firmly rooted in knowledge develops a spiritual disposition through which it produces "fruit" perpetually in the form of beautiful words, righteous actions, a state of humility, self-effacement, weeping, and lowliness*. The *logic* of the *reading* is the *logic* of the *continuity*: the *soul* that is *rooted* in *knowledge* *produces* fruit *perpetually*, and the *perpetual production* is the *condition* of the *continuity* of the *benefit*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *soul* is thus: the *soul* that is *rooted* in *knowledge* is the *soul* that *produces* fruit *perpetually*, and the *perpetual production* is the *condition* of the *continuity* of the *benefit*.

**"God Sets Forth Parables for Mankind, That Haply They May Remember": The Purpose of the Parable**

The *final* clause — *yamthu llāhu l-nāsa amthālahā laʿallahum yadhkarūn* — "God sets forth parables for mankind, that haply they may remember" — is the *statement* of the *purpose* of the *parable*. The *word* *dhikr* — "remembrance" — is the *word* of the *revival*, the *word* of the *state* in which the *one* *remembers* what he had *forgotten*. The *parable* is *set forth* *so that* the *one* *may remember* — the *parable* is the *vehicle* of the *remembrance*, and the *remembrance* is the *purpose* of the *parable*. The *logic* of the *purpose* is the *logic* of the *revival*: the *parable* *revives* the *one* who *has forgotten*, and the *revival* is the *condition* of the *remembrance*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *parable* is thus: the *parable* is the *vehicle* of the *remembrance*, and the *remembrance* is the *purpose* of the *parable*.

**The Logic: The Continuity and the Remembrance**

The *logic* of the *verse*, stated in its *full* form, is a *statement* about the *two* *aspects* of the *parable*. The *first aspect* is the *continuity*: the *good word* *produces* fruit *in every season*, *by the leave of its Lord*. The *second aspect* is the *purpose*: the *parable* is *set forth* *so that* the *one* *may remember*. The *two* *aspects* together state the *full scope of the parable*: the *parable* is *continuous* in its *production* (the *first*), and the *parable* is *purposeful* in its *setting forth* (the *second*). The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *parable* is thus: the *parable* is *continuous* and *purposeful*, and the *two* together are the *condition* of the *remembrance*.

**The Modern Application: The Practice of the Good Word**

The *verse's* teaching on the *continuity* of the *good word* is *directly* applicable to the *modern* reader. The *person* who *practices* the *good word* — the *shahādah*, the *truth*, the *purity* — is the *person* whose *word* *produces* fruit *in every season*. The *fruit* is *not* a *one-time* event; the *fruit* is *continuous*. The *one* who *expects* the *fruit* of the *good word* to be a *one-time* event is the *one* who *does not understand* the *nature* of the *good word*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *psychology* of the *practice* is thus: the *practice* of the *good word* is *continuous*, and the *continuity* is the *condition* of the *fruit*.
---

## Sūrah Ibrāhīm 14:26

> **And the parable of a bad word is a bad tree: uprooted from the face of the earth; it has no stability.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"The Parable of a Bad Word": The Negative Image**

The verse gives the *negative* counterpart to the *parable* of the *good word* (vv. 24–25). The clause *wa-mathalu kalimatin sayyiʾah* — "And the parable of a bad word" — is the *statement* of the *subject*. The *commentators* (al-Kalbī) understand *kalimatin sayyiʾah* — "a bad word" — to mean a *statement of disbelief in God* — the *opposite* of the *shahādah* (*lā ilāha illā llāh*) symbolically represented as a *good tree* in v. 24. The *word* *sayyiʾah* — "bad" — is the *word* of the *corruption*, the *word* of the *state* in which there is *impurity*, *falsehood*, *and disbelief*. The *bad word* is the *word* that is *corrupt*, *false*, and *the opposite* of the *truth*. The *logic* of the *image* is the *logic* of the *contrast*: the *good word* *corresponds* to the *good tree*, and the *bad word* *corresponds* to the *bad tree*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *bad word* is thus: the *bad word* is the *opposite* of the *good word*, and the *opposite* is the *condition* of the *contrast*.

**"A Bad Tree": The Ḥanẓalah Tree, and Its Shallow Roots**

The *image* of the *bad tree* is *specified* in the *commentators'* reading. The *commentators* (al-Ajfārī) understand *shajaratin sayyiʾah* — "a bad tree" — to be a *reference to a ḥanalah tree* — the *tree* that is *easily uprooted* from the *face of the earth*, *because its roots are shallow, close to the surface of the earth*. The *ḥanẓalah* is the *tree* that *seems* to be a *tree* but is *not* a *tree* in the *sense* of the *good tree*: the *ḥanẓalah* has *shallow roots*, the *ḥanẓalah* is *easily uprooted*, the *ḥanẓalah* *provides no stability*. The *logic* of the *image* is the *logic* of the *contrast*: the *good tree* has *deep roots* (the *firm root* of v. 24) and *high branches* (the *branch in the sky* of v. 24), and the *bad tree* has *shallow roots* and *no stability*. The *two* *trees* together state the *full scope of the contrast*: the *good tree* is *stable*, and the *bad tree* is *unstable*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *bad tree* is thus: the *bad tree* is the *opposite* of the *good tree*, and the *opposite* is the *condition* of the *contrast*.

**"Uprooted from the Face of the Earth": The Shallow Root**

The *first* attribute of the *bad tree* — *maqtūʿatun min wajhi l-arḍ* — "uprooted from the face of the earth" — is the *statement* of the *shallow root*. The *word* *maqtūʿah* — "uprooted, cut off" — is the *word* of the *severance*, the *word* of the *part* that is *cut off* from the *earth*. The *bad tree* is *uprooted* because its *root* is *shallow*, the *root* is *close to the surface*, the *root* *does not* *hold* the *tree*. The *logic* of the *attribute* is the *logic* of the *shallow root*: the *stability* of the *tree* *depends* on the *depth* of the *root*, and the *shallow root* *does not* *provide* the *stability*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *shallow root* is thus: the *shallow root* is the *condition* of the *uprooting*, and the *uprooting* is the *condition* of the *instability*.

**"It Has No Stability": The Final Attribute**

The *final* clause — *ma la-hā min thabūt* — "it has no stability" — is the *statement* of the *finality* of the *instability*. The *word* *thabūt* — "stability" — is the *word* of the *firmness*, the *word* of the *state* in which the *one* *stands firm*. The *bad tree* *has no stability* because its *root* is *shallow*, and the *shallow root* *does not* *provide* the *stability*. The *logic* of the *clause* is the *logic* of the *finality*: the *instability* is *final*, the *instability* is *total*, the *instability* is the *condition* of the *uprooting*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *instability* is thus: the *instability* is *final* and *total*, and the *finality* and the *totality* are the *conditions* of the *uprooting*.

**The Logic: The Good Tree and the Bad Tree**

The *logic* of the *verse*, stated in its *full* form, is a *statement* about the *contrast* between the *good tree* and the *bad tree*. The *good tree* has *deep roots* (the *firm root* of v. 24), *high branches* (the *branch in the sky* of v. 24), and *continuous fruit* (the *fruit in every season* of v. 25). The *bad tree* has *shallow roots* (the *uprooting* of v. 26) and *no stability* (the *finality* of the *instability* of v. 26). The *two* *trees* together state the *full scope of the contrast*: the *good tree* is *stable*, *fruitful*, and *continuous*, and the *bad tree* is *unstable*, *fruitless*, and *final*. The *sūrah's teaching on the *nature* of the *contrast* is thus: the *contrast* is the *condition* of the *recognition*, and the *recognition* is the *purpose* of the *parable*.

**The Modern Application: The Word That Has No Foundation**

The *verse's* teaching on the *bad tree* is *directly* applicable to the *modern* reader. The *person* whose *word* is the *bad word* — the *word* of *disbelief*, the *word* of *corruption*, the *word* of *falsehood* — is the *person* whose *word* is *uprooted*, whose *word* *has no stability*. The *word* that *has no foundation* is the *word* that is *easily blown away*, the *word* that *does not* *hold*, the *word* that *is the condition* of the *instability*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *psychology* of the *word* is thus: the *word* is *stable* only when it is *rooted* in the *truth*, and the *rooting* in the *truth* is the *condition* of the *stability*.
---

## Sūrah Ibrāhīm 14:27

> **God makes firm those who believe with firm speech in the life of this world and in the Hereafter. And God leads the wrongdoers astray; God does whatsoever He wills.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"God Makes Firm Those Who Believe with Firm Speech": The Firm Word**

The verse shifts from the *parable* of the *two trees* (vv. 24–26) to the *principle* that the *parables* *illustrate*. The clause *yathabbitu llāhu ll-muʾminīna bi-qawlin rābiḍ* — "God makes firm those who believe with firm speech" — is the *statement* of the *principle*. The *word* *qawlin rābiḍ* — "firm speech, firm word" — is, according to al-Nīsābūrī, the *full form of the shahādah formula*, *"There is no god but God, and Muhammad is the Messenger of God"* (*lā ilāha illā llāh wa-Muḥammadun rasūlu llāh*). The *firm word* is the *word* that *makes firm*, the *word* that *is the condition* of the *firmness* of the *believer*. The *logic* of the *clause* is the *logic* of the *source*: the *firmness* of the *believer* is *not* the *believer's own achievement*; the *firmness* is *God's gift*, given *through* the *firm word*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *firmness* is thus: the *firmness* is *God's* to *give*, and the *firm word* is the *condition* of the *gift*.

**"In the Life of This World": The Firmness in the World of Trial**

The *first* realm of the *firmness* — *fī d-dunyā* — "in the life of this world" — is the *statement* of the *firmness* in the *world of trial*. The commentators (al-Bayḵāwī) explain that the *firm word* *makes the believers firm* by *protecting their faith from the trials of this world* — the *trials* of the *world* are the *trials* that *test* the *faith*, and the *firm word* is the *protection* against the *trials*. The *logic* of the *realm* is the *logic* of the *protection*: the *firm word* *protects* the *faith* from the *trials* of the *world*, and the *protection* is the *condition* of the *firmness* in the *world*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *firmness* in the *world* is thus: the *firmness* in the *world* is the *protection* of the *faith* from the *trials* of the *world*, and the *protection* is the *condition* of the *firmness*.

**"And in the Hereafter": The Firmness in the Grave and Beyond**

The *second* realm of the *firmness* — *wa l-ākhirah* — "and in the Hereafter" — is the *statement* of the *firmness* in the *Hereafter*. The commentators (al-Bayḵāwī, al-Qurṭubī) explain that the *firm word* *makes the believers firm* *in the Hereafter* *from the moment when the angels Munkar and Nakīr visit them in the grave and ask them the questions*: *"Who is your Lord?"* and *"Who is your prophet?"* The *firm word* is the *answer* to the *questions* of the *grave*, and the *answer* is the *condition* of the *firmness* in the *grave*. The *Prophet* ﷺ described the *interrogation* of the *grave* in a *report* recorded by *Muslim* (2840, from Abū Hurayrah):

> "When my servant is buried and I (his companions) go away or walk away, he hears the sound of their sandals. Then he sees two angels coming to him, who make him sit and say to him: 'What was your opinion about this man, Muhammad?' ... The believer says: 'I bear witness that he is the servant of God and His Messenger.' It is said: 'Look to your seat in the Fire.' He says: 'O Lord, return me to the people and live my life again.' They say: 'No.' That is his one sigh." (Muslim 2840)

The *firm word* is the *answer* that the *believer* *gives* in the *grave*, and the *answer* is the *condition* of the *firmness* in the *grave*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *firmness* in the *Hereafter* is thus: the *firmness* in the *Hereafter* is the *answer* to the *questions* of the *grave*, and the *answer* is the *condition* of the *firmness*.

**The Prophetic Confirmation: The Three Men and the Firm Word**

The *principle* of the *firm word* is *confirmed* by a *Prophetic report* recorded by *al-Bukhārī* (4480) and *Muslim* (152): the *story* of the *three men* who *fell* into a *cave*, and the *rock* *rolled* over the *mouth* of the *cave*. The *three* *men* *made* *supplications*, and *each* *supplication* was *based* on a *righteous deed*. The *first* *man* *supplied* his *father* with *wages* and *did not* *withhold* anything; the *second* *man* *had* a *relation* and *kept* his *chastity*; the *third* *man* *paid* his *workers* their *wages*. The *rock* *moved* for each *supplication*, and the *three* *men* *came out*. The *report* *confirms* the *principle*: the *righteous deed* — the *firm word* *acted* — is the *condition* of the *deliverance*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *firm word* is thus: the *firm word* is the *condition* of the *firmness*, and the *firmness* is the *condition* of the *deliverance*.

**"And God Leads the Wrongdoers Astray": The Contrast**

The *second* half of the *verse* — *wa-yudhillu llāhu l-ẓālimīna* — "And God leads the wrongdoers astray" — is the *contrast* to the *firmness* of the *believers*. The *wrongdoers* are *led astray*, and the *leading astray* is the *consequence* of the *wrongdoing*. The *parallel* at 4:88–90, 17:97, 18:17, and 74:31 states the *same* *principle*. The *logic* of the *contrast* is the *logic* of the *two* *paths*: the *believers* are *made firm* (the *first*), and the *wrongdoers* are *led astray* (the *second*). The *two* *paths* are the *two* *destinies*, and the *destinies* are the *consequences* of the *choices*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *contrast* is thus: the *contrast* is the *condition* of the *recognition*, and the *recognition* is the *purpose* of the *parable*.

**"God Does Whatsoever He Wills": The Final Word**

The *final* clause — *wa-yafʿalu llāhu mā yashāʾ* — "God does whatsoever He wills" — is the *final* word of the *verse*. The *logic* of the *clause* is the *logic* of the *sovereignty*: *God* *does* *whatsoever* *He wills*, and the *sovereignty* is the *condition* of the *two* *paths*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *sovereignty* is thus: the *sovereignty* is the *condition* of the *two* *paths*, and the *two* *paths* are the *consequences* of the *choices*.

**The Modern Application: The Firm Word as the Source of the Firmness**

The *verse's* teaching on the *firm word* is *directly* applicable to the *modern* reader. The *person* who *holds* the *firm word* — the *shahādah*, the *truth*, the *purity* — is the *person* who is *made firm* in the *world* and in the *Hereafter*. The *firm word* is the *protection* against the *trials* of the *world*, and the *firm word* is the *answer* to the *questions* of the *grave*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *psychology* of the *firm word* is thus: the *firm word* is the *source* of the *firmness*, and the *firmness* is the *condition* of the *deliverance*.
---

## Sūrah Ibrāhīm 14:28

> **Hast thou not considered those who exchanged the Blessing of God for ingratitude and caused their people to dwell in the abode of perdition—**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Hast Thou Not Considered": The Invitation to the Specific Case**

The verse opens with the *sūrah's* own formula for the *invitation* to *reflection*: *aw lam tara* — "Hast thou not considered?" The *invitation* is *directed* to a *specific* case: the *case* of the *one* who *exchanged* the *Blessing of God* for *ingratitude*. The *logic* of the *invitation* is the *logic* of the *attention*: the *hearer* is *invited* to *consider* the *specific case*, and the *attention* to the *specific case* is the *condition* of the *recognition*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *invitation* is thus: the *invitation* is the *condition* of the *attention*, and the *attention* is the *condition* of the *recognition*.

**"Those Who Exchanged the Blessing of God for Ingratitude": The Exchange**

The *subject* of the *verse* is the *one* who *exchanged* the *Blessing of God* for *ingratitude*: *alladhīna badalū niʿmata llāhi kufrā* — "those who exchanged the Blessing of God for ingratitude." The *word* *badalū* — "exchanged" — is the *word* of the *substitution*, the *word* of the *one* who *puts* one *thing* in *place of* another. The *one* who *exchanges* the *Blessing* for *ingratitude* is the *one* who *puts* *ingratitude* in *place of* the *Blessing*, the *one* who *replaces* the *gratitude* with the *ingratitude*. The *logic* of the *exchange* is the *logic* of the *substitution*: the *one* who *exchanges* the *Blessing* for *ingratitude* is the *one* who *chooses* the *ingratitude* *over* the *Blessing*, and the *choice* is the *condition* of the *exchange*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *exchange* is thus: the *exchange* is the *choice* of the *ingratitude* over the *Blessing*, and the *choice* is the *condition* of the *exchange*.

**The Occasion of the Revelation: The Disbelievers among the Quraysh**

The *commentators* (al-Bayḵāwī, al-Jāmiʿ) note that the *reference* here is *particularly* to the *disbelievers among the Quraysh*. The *Blessing of God* refers to the *Prophet Muhammad* ﷺ — the *Blessing* is the *Prophet*, the *Blessing* is the *guidance*, the *Blessing* is the *one* that the *Quraysh* *exchanged* for *ingratitude*. The *verse* belongs to the *Madinan period*, and the *reference* to the *Quraysh* is the *reference* to the *one* who *exchanged* the *Prophet* for *ingratitude*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *occasion* is thus: the *occasion* is the *specific case* of the *Quraysh*, and the *specific case* is the *condition* of the *recognition*.

**"And Caused Their People to Dwell in the Abode of Perdition": The Consequence**

The *second* clause — *wa-ajtarū qaumahumu ṣ-aḥfā* — "and caused their people to dwell in the abode of perdition" — is the *statement* of the *consequence*. The *word* *ajtarū* — "caused to dwell, made to enter" — is the *word* of the *causation*: the *one* who *exchanged* the *Blessing* *caused* his *people* to *dwell* in the *abode of perdition*. The *word* *ṣ-aḥfā* — "the abode of perdition" — is the *word* of the *ruin*, the *word* of the *state* in which the *one* *is lost*. The *commentators* (Ibn Kathīr) explain that the *reference* to the *abode of perdition* refers to the *defeat that the Quraysh suffered at the Battle of Badr* — the *defeat* at *Badr* is the *abode of perdition*, the *defeat* is the *ruin*, the *defeat* is the *one* that the *Quraysh* *dwelt in*. The *alternate* reading (al-Jāmiʿ) is that the *abode of perdition* refers to the *destruction they brought upon their own people in general* — the *destruction* is the *ruin*, the *destruction* is the *one* that the *Quraysh* *caused*. The *logic* of the *clause* is the *logic* of the *causation*: the *one* who *exchanged* the *Blessing* *caused* his *people* to *dwell* in the *ruin*, and the *causation* is the *condition* of the *consequence*.

**The Logic: The Exchange and the Consequence**

The *logic* of the *verse*, stated in its *full* form, is a *statement* about the *two* *aspects* of the *exchange*. The *first aspect* is the *exchange*: the *one* *exchanged* the *Blessing* for *ingratitude*. The *second aspect* is the *consequence*: the *one* *caused* his *people* to *dwell* in the *abode of perdition*. The *two* *aspects* together state the *full scope of the exchange*: the *exchange* is the *choice* of the *ingratitude* over the *Blessing* (the *first*), and the *consequence* is the *ruin* of the *people* (the *second*). The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *exchange* is thus: the *exchange* is the *condition* of the *consequence*, and the *consequence* is the *condition* of the *recognition*.

**The Social Ethics: The Leader Who Exchanges the Blessing**

The *verse's* teaching on the *social ethics* is *directly* applicable to the *modern* reader. The *leader* who *exchanges* the *Blessing* for *ingratitude* is the *leader* who *causes* his *people* to *dwell* in the *ruin*. The *leader* who *chooses* the *ingratitude* over the *Blessing* is the *leader* who *leads* his *people* into the *ruin*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *psychology* of the *leader* is thus: the *leader* is *responsible* for the *consequence* of the *exchange*, and the *consequence* is the *ruin* of the *people*.

**The Frame: The Abode of Perdition and the Abode of the Garden**

The *verse* stands in *contrast* with the *abode* of the *garden* described in v. 23. The *believer* *dwells* in the *garden* (v. 23: *muqīmīna* — "abiding"), and the *ungrateful* one *dwells* in the *abode of perdition* (v. 28: *ajtarū* — "caused to dwell"). The *two* *abodes* together state the *full scope of the dwelling*: the *dwelling* in the *garden* is the *consequence* of the *gratitude*, and the *dwelling* in the *abode of perdition* is the *consequence* of the *ingratitude*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *dwelling* is thus: the *dwelling* is the *consequence* of the *choice*, and the *choice* is the *condition* of the *dwelling*.
---

## Sūrah Ibrāhīm 14:29

> **Hell, wherein they shall burn. What an evil dwelling place!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Hell": The Specification of the Abode of Perdition**

The verse *specifies* the *abode of perdition* named in v. 28: the *abode* is *Hell*: *jahannam* — "Hell." The *specification* is the *sūrah's* own *teaching* on the *nature* of the *abode*: the *abode of perdition* is *not* an *abstract* concept; the *abode of perdition* is *Hell*, and *Hell* is the *one* that the *ungrateful* one *dwells in*. The *logic* of the *specification* is the *logic* of the *concreteness*: the *abode* is *specified*, the *abode* is *concrete*, and the *concreteness* is the *condition* of the *attention*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *abode* is thus: the *abode* is *Hell*, and *Hell* is the *condition* of the *attention*.

**"Wherein They Shall Burn": The Dwelling as the Burning**

The *clause* *yuḥraqūnā fīhā* — "wherein they shall burn" — is the *statement* of the *dwelling*. The *word* *yuḥraqūnā* — "they shall burn" — is the *word* of the *burning*, the *word* of the *state* in which the *one* *is consumed*. The *dwelling* in *Hell* is the *burning*: the *one* who *dwells* in *Hell* *burns* in *Hell*, and the *burning* is the *condition* of the *dwelling*. The *logic* of the *clause* is the *logic* of the *concreteness*: the *dwelling* is *specified*, the *dwelling* is *concrete*, and the *concreteness* is the *condition* of the *attention*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *dwelling* is thus: the *dwelling* is the *burning*, and the *burning* is the *condition* of the *dwelling*.

**"What an Evil Dwelling Place!": The Verdict**

The *final* clause — *sāʾa dhālika masakan* — "What an evil dwelling place!" — is the *verdict* on the *abode*. The *word* *masakan* — "dwelling place" — is the *word* of the *abode*, the *word* of the *state* in which the *one* *dwells*. The *verdict* is the *statement* of the *evil* of the *dwelling*: the *dwelling* is *evil*, the *dwelling* is the *one* that is *to be avoided*. The *logic* of the *verdict* is the *logic* of the *warning*: the *verdict* is the *warning*, and the *warning* is the *condition* of the *avoidance*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *verdict* is thus: the *verdict* is the *warning*, and the *warning* is the *condition* of the *avoidance*.

**The Logic: The Abode, the Dwelling, and the Verdict**

The *logic* of the *verse*, stated in its *full* form, is a *statement* about the *three* *aspects* of the *abode*. The *first aspect* is the *specification*: the *abode* is *Hell*. The *second aspect* is the *dwelling*: the *dwelling* in *Hell* is the *burning*. The *third aspect* is the *verdict*: the *dwelling* is *evil*. The *three* *aspects* together state the *full scope of the abode*: the *abode* is *Hell* (the *first*), the *dwelling* is the *burning* (the *second*), and the *verdict* is the *evil* (the *third*). The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *abode* is thus: the *abode* is *Hell*, the *dwelling* is the *burning*, and the *verdict* is the *evil*.

**The Frame: The Dwelling in Hell and the Dwelling in the Garden**

The *verse* stands in *contrast* with the *dwelling* in the *garden* described in v. 23. The *believer* *dwells* in the *garden* (v. 23: *muqīmīna* — "abiding"), and the *ungrateful* one *dwells* in *Hell* (v. 29: *yuḥraqūnā* — "they shall burn"). The *two* *dwellings* together state the *full scope of the dwelling*: the *dwelling* in the *garden* is the *consequence* of the *gratitude*, and the *dwelling* in *Hell* is the *consequence* of the *ingratitude*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *dwelling* is thus: the *dwelling* is the *consequence* of the *choice*, and the *choice* is the *condition* of the *dwelling*.

**The Modern Application: The Choice of the Abode**

The *verse's* teaching on the *choice* of the *abode* is *directly* applicable to the *modern* reader. The *person* who *chooses* the *ingratitude* over the *Blessing* is the *person* who *dwells* in *Hell*, and the *person* who *chooses* the *gratitude* over the *ingratitude* is the *person* who *dwells* in the *garden*. The *choice* is the *condition* of the *dwelling*, and the *dwelling* is the *consequence* of the *choice*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *psychology* of the *choice* is thus: the *choice* is the *condition* of the *dwelling*, and the *dwelling* is the *consequence* of the *choice*.
---

## Sūrah Ibrāhīm 14:30

> **They set up equals unto God, that they might lead astray from His Way. Say, "Enjoy yourselves! For truly your journey is unto the Fire!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"They Set Up Equals unto God": The Mechanism of Shirk**

The verse states the *act* that the *ungrateful* of vv. 28–29 *have committed*: *wa-jaʿalū li-llāhi akhnāṣā* — "They set up equals unto God." The *word* *akhnāṣā* — "equals, partners, rivals" — is the *word* of the *shirk*, the *word* of the *one* who *sets up* a *rival* beside *God*. The *shirk* is the *ultimate* form of the *ingratitude* of v. 28: the *one* who *exchanged* the *Blessing* for *ingratitude* is the *one* who *sets up* a *rival* beside *God*, and the *rival* is the *one* who *takes* the *place* of *God* in the *heart*. The *logic* of the *act* is the *logic* of the *substitution*: the *one* who *sets up* a *rival* beside *God* is the *one* who *puts* the *rival* in the *place* of *God*, and the *substitution* is the *condition* of the *shirk*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *shirk* is thus: the *shirk* is the *substitution* of the *rival* for *God*, and the *substitution* is the *condition* of the *ingratitude*.

**"That They Might Lead Astray from His Way": The Result, Not the Purpose**

The *clause* *li-yuḍillū ʿan sabīli llāh* — "that they might lead astray from His Way" — is the *statement* of the *consequence* of the *shirk*. The *commentators* (al-Bayḵāwī) explain that the *phrase* does *not* mean that the *reason* people *set up* equals unto *God* is *so that they can misguide or lead others astray*. Rather, it means that *misguidance and leading astray are the result of their setting up equals alongside God and thus are tantamount to such a purpose*. The *logic* of the *clause* is the *logic* of the *result*: the *misguiding* of *others* is the *result* of the *shirk*, and the *result* is *tantamount* to the *purpose*. The *one* who *sets up* a *rival* beside *God* *necessarily* *leads* others *away* from the *Way*, because the *rival* is the *one* who *takes* the *place* of *God* in the *heart*, and the *one* whose *heart* is *occupied* by the *rival* *cannot* *guide* others *to* the *Way*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *shirk* is thus: the *shirk* *necessarily* *leads* others *astray*, and the *leading astray* is the *result* of the *shirk*. The *parallel* at 39:8 states the *same* *principle*:

> "And as for those who reject the Hereafter and deny the Book, and those who have no authority (to decide) — they are the enemies of Allah, and those who set up rivals with Allah — they are far astray." (39:8)

**"Say, 'Enjoy yourselves!': The Irony of the Final Word**

The *command* to the *Prophet* — *qul inṭaliqū* — "Say, 'Enjoy yourselves!'" — is the *sūrah's* own *teaching* on the *nature* of the *prophetic* word in the *face* of the *closed position*. The *commentators* (al-Bayḵāwī) explain that the *phrase* is *ironic*: it *encourages* them to *persist* in their *incorrect ways*, although the *net result* will be the *opposite* of enjoyment, since their *journey* is *unto the Fire*. The *logic* of the *irony* is the *logic* of the *final detachment*: the *Prophet* has *said* everything that *needs* to be *said*; the *Prophet* has *exhausted* the *arguments*; the *Prophet* *leaves* them to *their own choice*. The *word* *"Enjoy yourselves!"* is the *word* of the *one* who *has no further* *argument*, the *word* of the *one* who *lets* the *choice* *speak* for itself. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *irony* is thus: the *irony* is the *final word* of the *one* who *has exhausted* the *arguments*, and the *exhaustion* of the *arguments* is the *condition* of the *final word*.

**"For Truly Your Journey Is unto the Fire": The Destination**

The *final* clause — *inna mawkifakum n-nār* — "For truly your journey is unto the Fire" — is the *statement* of the *destination*. The *word* *mawkif* — "journey, stopping place, destination" — is the *word* of the *destination*, the *word* of the *one* that the *one* *arrives at*. The *destination* is *the Fire*: the *one* who *sets up* a *rival* beside *God* *arrives* at *the Fire*, and the *arrival* is the *condition* of the *destination*. The *logic* of the *clause* is the *logic* of the *inevitability*: the *destination* is *not* an *alternative*; the *destination* is the *one* that the *choice* *leads to*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *destination* is thus: the *destination* is *the Fire*, and the *Fire* is the *condition* of the *inevitability*.

**The Logic: The Shirk, the Result, and the Destination**

The *logic* of the *verse*, stated in its *full* form, is a *statement* about the *three* *aspects* of the *shirk*. The *first aspect* is the *act*: the *one* *sets up* a *rival* beside *God*. The *second aspect* is the *result*: the *shirk* *necessarily* *leads* others *astray*. The *third aspect* is the *destination*: the *one* who *sets up* a *rival* *arrives* at *the Fire*. The *three* *aspects* together state the *full scope of the shirk*: the *shirk* is the *substitution* (the *first*), the *shirk* *leads astray* (the *second*), and the *shirk* *arrives at the Fire* (the *third*). The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *shirk* is thus: the *shirk* is the *substitution*, the *leading astray*, and the *arrival at the Fire* together, and the *three* are the *full scope of the shirk*.

**The Modern Application: The Psychology of the Rival**

The *verse's* teaching on the *psychology* of the *rival* is *directly* applicable to the *modern* reader. The *person* who *sets up* a *rival* beside *God* — the *person* whose *heart* is *occupied* by a *rival* — is the *person* who *leads* others *away* from the *Way*. The *rival* is *not* merely an *idol*; the *rival* is *anything* that *takes* the *place* of *God* in the *heart*: the *wealth*, the *position*, the *reputation*, the *pleasure*. The *one* whose *heart* is *occupied* by the *rival* *cannot* *guide* others *to* the *Way*, because the *one* whose *heart* is *occupied* *cannot* *see* the *Way*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *psychology* of the *rival* is thus: the *rival* is the *one* that *occupies* the *heart*, and the *occupation* of the *heart* is the *condition* of the *leading astray*.
---

## Sūrah Ibrāhīm 14:31

> **Tell My servants who believe to perform the prayer and to spend from that which We have provided them, secretly and openly, before a day comes wherein there shall be neither bargaining nor befriending.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Tell My Servants Who Believe": The Intimacy of the Address**

The verse shifts from the *description* of the *disbelievers* (vv. 28–30) to the *command* to the *believers*. The *command* is addressed with a *phrase* of *particular* weight: *qul li-ʿibādi lladhīna āmanū* — "Tell My servants who believe." The *phrase* *ʿibādī* — "My servants" — is the *divine* possessive: the *believers* are *God's* *servants*, and the *possessive* is the *word* of the *intimacy*. The *logic* of the *address* is the *logic* of the *intimacy*: the *believers* are *God's* *servants*, and the *intimacy* is the *condition* of the *command*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *address* is thus: the *address* is *intimate*, and the *intimacy* is the *condition* of the *command*.

**"Perform the Prayer": The Establishment, Not the Mere Performance**

The *first* *command* — *yuqīmū l-ṣalāh* — "perform the prayer" — is the *command* of the *establishment*. The *word* *yuqīmū* — "establish, set up" — is the *word* of the *setting up*, the *word* of the *one* who *stands* the *prayer* *firmly*, who *maintains* the *prayer*, who *does not* *neglect* the *prayer*. The *command* is *not* merely to *perform* the *prayer*; the *command* is to *establish* the *prayer*, to *set it up* in the *life* of the *believer*, to *maintain* it *through* the *trials* of the *world*. The *logic* of the *command* is the *logic* of the *establishment*: the *prayer* is *not* a *one-time* act; the *prayer* is a *standing* act, and the *standing* act is the *condition* of the *establishment*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *prayer* is thus: the *prayer* is the *establishment* of the *relationship* with *God*, and the *establishment* is the *condition* of the *relationship*.

**"Spend from That Which We Have Provided Them": The Spending as Worship**

The *second* *command* — *wa-yunfiqū mimmā razaqnāhum* — "spend from that which We have provided them" — is the *command* of the *spending*. The *word* *yunfiqū* — "spend" — is the *word* of the *expenditure*, the *word* of the *one* who *gives* from his *wealth*. The *spending* is *not* a *charity* in the *ordinary* sense; the *spending* is an *act of worship*, the *act* of the *one* who *recognizes* that the *wealth* is *God's* *provision* and that the *spending* is the *response* to the *provision*. The *logic* of the *command* is the *logic* of the *response*: the *wealth* is *God's* *provision*, and the *spending* is the *response* to the *provision*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *spending* is thus: the *spending* is the *response* to the *provision*, and the *response* is the *condition* of the *gratitude*.

**"Secretly and Openly": The Two Dimensions of the Spending**

The *command* specifies the *two* dimensions of the *spending*: *sirrā wa-ʿalāniyatā* — "secretly and openly." The *secret* spending is the *spending* that is *done* in *secret*, the *spending* that is *not* *seen* by *others*, the *spending* that is the *act* of *sincerity*. The *open* spending is the *spending* that is *done* in *open*, the *spending* that is *seen* by *others*, the *spending* that is the *act* of *social testimony*. The *two* dimensions together state the *full scope of the spending*: the *spending* is *both* *secret* (the *sincerity*) and *open* (the *testimony*). The *logic* of the *two dimensions* is the *logic* of the *completeness*: the *spending* that is *only secret* is *incomplete* (it *lacks* the *testimony*), and the *spending* that is *only open* is *incomplete* (it *lacks* the *sincerity*). The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *spending* is thus: the *spending* is *complete* only when it is *both* *secret* and *open*, and the *completeness* is the *condition* of the *acceptance*.

**"Before a Day Comes Wherein There Shall Be Neither Bargaining Nor Befriending": The Urgency**

The *final* clause — *min qabli an tatiya yawman lā baiʿa fīhi wa-lā mukālafah* — "before a day comes wherein there shall be neither bargaining nor befriending" — is the *statement* of the *urgency*. The *day* is the *Day of Judgment*, when the *ability to negotiate and appeal to friends for help will be of no avail*. The *parallel* at 2:254 states the *same* *principle* in its *full* form:

> "O you who believe! Spend from that which We have provided you before a day comes wherein there shall be neither bargaining, nor friendship, nor intercession. And the disbelievers, they are the wrongdoers." (2:254)

The *logic* of the *clause* is the *logic* of the *urgency*: the *spending* is *commanded* *before* the *day* comes, because the *day* is the *one* in which the *spending* is *no longer possible*. The *wealth* is *useful* only in *this life*; the *wealth* is *not useful* in the *next*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *urgency* is thus: the *urgency* is the *condition* of the *spending*, and the *spending* is the *condition* of the *gratitude*.

**The Logic: The Prayer and the Spending**

The *logic* of the *verse*, stated in its *full* form, is a *statement* about the *two* *commands*. The *first command* is the *prayer*: the *establishment* of the *relationship* with *God*. The *second command* is the *spending*: the *response* to the *provision* of *God*. The *two* commands together state the *full scope of the believer's life*: the *believer* *establishes* the *relationship* with *God* (the *prayer*), and the *believer* *responds* to the *provision* of *God* (the *spending*). The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *two commands* is thus: the *two commands* are the *full scope of the believer's life*, and the *full scope* is the *condition* of the *gratitude*.

**The Modern Application: The Time-Limited Nature of the Opportunity**

The *verse's* teaching on the *urgency* is *directly* applicable to the *modern* reader. The *person* who *delays* the *spending* is the *person* who *misses* the *opportunity*. The *wealth* is *useful* only in *this life*; the *wealth* is *not useful* in the *next*. The *one* who *delays* the *response* to the *provision* is the *one* who *arrives* at the *day* *without* the *response*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *psychology* of the *urgency* is thus: the *urgency* is the *condition* of the *spending*, and the *spending* is the *condition* of the *gratitude*.
---

## Sūrah Ibrāhīm 14:32

> **God it is Who created the heavens and the earth, and sent down water from the sky, then brought forth fruits thereby for your provision. He has made the ships subservient unto you, so that they sail upon the sea by His Command, and has made the rivers subservient unto you.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"God It Is Who Created the Heavens and the Earth": The Ground of the Subservience**

The verse *returns* to the *theme* of the *creation* begun in v. 19, but now in the *context* of the *subservience*. The *opening* clause — *Allāhu lladhī khalaqa s-samāwāti wa l-arḍ* — "God it is Who created the heavens and the earth" — is the *statement* of the *ground* of the *subservience*. The *subservience* (the *taskhīr*) is *possible* only because the *Creator* is the *One* who *owns* the *creation*, and the *ownership* is the *condition* of the *subservience*. The *logic* of the *clause* is the *logic* of the *ground*: the *subservience* is *grounded* in the *creation*, and the *creation* is the *condition* of the *subservience*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *ground* is thus: the *ground* is the *creation*, and the *creation* is the *condition* of the *subservience*.

**"Sent Down Water from the Sky, Then Brought Forth Fruits Thereby": The Chain of the Provision**

The *first* *link* of the *chain* of the *provision* is the *water*: *wa-anzala mina s-samāʾi māʾan* — "sent down water from the sky." The *word* *anzala* — "sent down" — is the *word* of the *sending down*, the *word* of the *one* who *sends* from *above* to *below*. The *water* is *sent down* from the *sky*, and the *water* *produces* the *fruit*: *fa-aẓra bihī mathaʾan rizqan lakum* — "then brought forth fruits thereby for your provision." The *chain* is *explicit*: the *water* *is sent down*, the *water* *produces* the *fruit*, and the *fruit* *is the provision*. The *logic* of the *chain* is the *logic* of the *dependence*: the *provision* *depends* on the *water*, and the *water* *is sent down* by *God*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *provision* is thus: the *provision* is the *fruit* of the *water*, and the *water* is the *gift* of *God*.

**"He Has Made the Ships Subservient unto You": The Taskhīr of the Sea**

The *second* *link* of the *chain* is the *ship*: *wa-sakhkhara lakumu l-jalāhīfa* — "He has made the ships subservient unto you." The *word* *sakhkhara* — "made subservient, subjected" — is the *word* of the *taskhīr*, the *word* of the *one* who *submits* a *thing* to the *service* of *another*. The *ship* is *subservient* to *you*, and the *ship* *sails* upon the *sea* *by His Command*: *tajrī fī l-baḥri bi-amrihi* — "so that they sail upon the sea by His Command." The *logic* of the *clause* is the *logic* of the *command*: the *ship* *sails* *by God's Command*, and the *Command* is the *condition* of the *sailing*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *taskhīr* is thus: the *taskhīr* is the *submission* of the *sea* to the *service* of *man*, and the *submission* is *by God's Command*.

**"And Has Made the Rivers Subservient unto You": The Taskhīr of the Land**

The *third* *link* of the *chain* is the *river*: *wa l-anhāra* — "and has made the rivers subservient unto you." The *river* is the *taskhīr* of the *land*, the *river* is the *water* of the *land*, the *river* is the *provision* of the *land*. The *two* *together* — the *ship* (the *taskhīr* of the *sea*) and the *river* (the *taskhīr* of the *land*) — state the *full scope of the taskhīr*: the *sea* and the *land* are *both* *subservient* to *man*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *taskhīr* is thus: the *taskhīr* is the *submission* of the *sea* and the *land* to the *service* of *man*, and the *submission* is the *condition* of the *provision*.

**The Notion of Taskhīr: Subservience Without Tyranny**

These verses convey the notion, found elsewhere in the Qur'an (2:29; 16:5–8, 10–16), that the beings of the natural world have been made subservient (*taskhīr*) to human beings; that is, God has put the world of nature at their service in order to help them fulfill various needs and achieve certain goals in life. The *sūrah's* own *point* on the *taskhīr* is *crucial*: *Although people have nature at their disposal, they are not without responsibility toward it. They are to be its humble custodians, not its tyrannical controllers* (cf. 30:4), *since God has appointed human beings as vicegerents upon the earth* (6:165). The *logic* of the *point* is the *logic* of the *custody*: the *taskhīr* is the *submission* of *nature* to the *service* of *man*, and the *submission* is the *condition* of the *custody*. The *one* who *submits* *nature* to his *service* is the *one* who *is the custodian* of *nature*, not the *tyrant* of *nature*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *taskhīr* is thus: the *taskhīr* is the *custody*, and the *custody* is the *condition* of the *responsibility*.

**The Inner Order: The Heart Subservient to God**

Parallel with the idea that all things in the cosmic order have been made subservient to human beings, it can be said that all elements within human beings are made subservient to God. In Islamic anthropology and psychology, the heart is identified as the seat of human beings' consciousness and knowledge; in other words, it is not only the center, but also the "totality" of his being. This is why Jaʿfar al-Ṣādiq (d. 148/765) said that God has made the heart of the believer "subservient to love for, and knowledge of, Him." So subservient to God is the heart of the sincere believer that the Prophet ﷺ is reported to have said:

> "The heart of the believer is the Throne of the All-Merciful (*ʿarsh al-Raḥmān*)."

The *logic* of the *parallel* is the *logic* of the *correspondence*: the *cosmic order* is *subservient* to *man* (the *outer*), and the *inner order* is *subservient* to *God* (the *inner*). The *two* together state the *full scope of the taskhīr*: the *outer* taskhīr is the *submission* of *nature* to *man*, and the *inner* taskhīr is the *submission* of the *heart* to *God*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *correspondence* is thus: the *correspondence* is the *condition* of the *full scope of the taskhīr*.

**The Modern Application: The Custody of Nature**

The *verse's* teaching on the *custody* is *directly* applicable to the *modern* reader. The *person* who *submits* *nature* to his *service* is the *person* who *is the custodian* of *nature*. The *one* who *treats* *nature* as a *resource* to be *exploited* is the *one* who *is the tyrant* of *nature*, and the *tyrant* of *nature* is the *one* who *breaks* the *custody*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *psychology* of the *custody* is thus: the *custody* is the *condition* of the *responsibility*, and the *responsibility* is the *condition* of the *stewardship*.
---

## Sūrah Ibrāhīm 14:33

> **And He has made the sun and the moon subservient unto you, constant, and He made the night and the day subservient unto you.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"He Has Made the Sun and the Moon Subservient unto You": The Taskhīr of the Light**

The verse *continues* the *enumeration* of the *taskhīr* begun in v. 32. The *next* *link* in the *chain* is the *sun* and the *moon*: *wa-sh-shamsa wa l-qamara muqarrarāt* — "He has made the sun and the moon subservient unto you, constant." The *word* *muqarrarāt* — "constant, made to proceed by a fixed course" — is the *word* of the *constancy*, the *word* of the *one* who *proceeds* by a *fixed course*. The *sun* and the *moon* *proceed* by a *fixed course*, and the *fixed course* is the *condition* of the *constancy*. The *logic* of the *clause* is the *logic* of the *constancy*: the *sun* and the *moon* *are constant* in their *course*, and the *constancy* is the *condition* of the *service*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *taskhīr* of the *light* is thus: the *taskhīr* of the *light* is the *constancy* of the *course*, and the *constancy* is the *condition* of the *service*.

**"He Made the Night and the Day Subservient unto You": The Taskhīr of Time**

The *final* *link* in the *chain* is the *night* and the *day*: *wa l-layla wa n-nahāra* — "He made the night and the day subservient unto you." The *night* and the *day* are the *taskhīr* of *time*, the *night* and the *day* are the *alternation* that *works* for *man*. The *logic* of the *clause* is the *logic* of the *alternation*: the *night* and the *day* *alternate*, and the *alternation* is the *condition* of the *service*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *taskhīr* of *time* is thus: the *taskhīr* of *time* is the *alternation* of the *night* and the *day*, and the *alternation* is the *condition* of the *service*.

**The Full Scope of the Taskhīr: The Four Foundations of Human Life**

The *two* *verses* (vv. 32–33) together state the *full scope of the taskhīr*. The *chain* is *complete*: the *water* (v. 32: *sent down from the sky*), the *fruit* (v. 32: *brought forth for your provision*), the *ship* (v. 32: *sails upon the sea*), the *river* (v. 32: *subservient unto you*), the *sun* and the *moon* (v. 33: *constant*), the *night* and the *day* (v. 33: *subservient unto you*). The *six* *links* together state the *four foundations of human life*: the *food* (the *water* and the *fruit*), the *transport* (the *ship* and the *river*), the *light* (the *sun* and the *moon*), and the *time* (the *night* and the *day*). The *four foundations* are the *conditions* of *human life*, and the *conditions* are *subservient* to *man*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *taskhīr* is thus: the *taskhīr* is the *submission* of the *four foundations* to the *service* of *man*, and the *submission* is the *condition* of the *life*.

**The Logic: The Constancy and the Service**

The *logic* of the *verse*, stated in its *full* form, is a *statement* about the *two* *aspects* of the *taskhīr*. The *first aspect* is the *constancy*: the *sun* and the *moon* *proceed* by a *fixed course*, and the *constancy* is the *condition* of the *service*. The *second aspect* is the *alternation*: the *night* and the *day* *alternate*, and the *alternation* is the *condition* of the *service*. The *two* *aspects* together state the *full scope of the taskhīr*: the *taskhīr* is the *constancy* (the *first*) and the *alternation* (the *second*). The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *taskhīr* is thus: the *taskhīr* is the *constancy* and the *alternation* together, and the *two* are the *condition* of the *service*.

**The Modern Application: The Infrastructure of Civilization**

The *verse's* teaching on the *taskhīr* is *directly* applicable to the *modern* reader. The *infrastructure* of *civilization* — the *light*, the *time*, the *transport*, the *food* — is the *taskhīr* of the *natural world* to the *service* of *man*. The *one* who *recognizes* the *taskhīr* is the *one* who *is grateful* for the *infrastructure*, and the *one* who *is grateful* for the *infrastructure* is the *one* who *is the custodian* of the *infrastructure*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *psychology* of the *gratitude* is thus: the *gratitude* is the *condition* of the *custody*, and the *custody* is the *condition* of the *responsibility*.

**The Frame: The Taskhīr and the Custody**

The *verse* stands in the *frame* of the *taskhīr* and the *custody*. The *taskhīr* is the *submission* of the *natural world* to the *service* of *man*, and the *custody* is the *responsibility* of *man* toward the *natural world*. The *two* together state the *full scope of the relationship*: the *natural world* is *subservient* to *man*, and *man* is *responsible* for the *natural world*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *relationship* is thus: the *relationship* is the *submission* and the *responsibility* together, and the *two* are the *condition* of the *stewardship*.
---

## Sūrah Ibrāhīm 14:34

> **And He gives you something of all that you ask of Him, and were you to count the Blessings of God, you could not number them. Truly mankind is wrongdoing, ungrateful.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"He Gives You Something of All That You Ask of Him": The Scope of the Giving**

The verse *continues* the *theme* of the *provision* begun in vv. 32–33. The *clause* — "And He gives you something of all that you ask of Him" — is the *statement* of the *scope* of the *giving*. The *commentators* (al-Bayḵāwī) explain that the *phrase* can be *read* to *mean* that *God gives people something of all that they ask for with their tongues, or of what they ask for by being in a state of need without actually articulating it verbally*. The *scope* of the *giving* is *total*: the *one* who *asks* with the *tongue* is *given*, and the *one* who *asks* in the *state of need* *without articulating* is *given*. The *logic* of the *clause* is the *logic* of the *scope*: the *giving* is *total* in its *scope*, and the *totality* is the *condition* of the *gratitude*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *giving* is thus: the *giving* is *total* in its *scope*, and the *totality* is the *condition* of the *gratitude*.

**The Alternate Reading: "That Which You Do Not Ask of Him"**

The *alternate* reading (al-Qurṭubī) is that the *article* *mā* can be *read* to *mean* *"He gives you of that which you do not ask of Him"* — the *giving* is *not limited* to the *one* who *asks*; the *giving* is *extended* to the *one* who *does not ask*. The *blessings* such as the *sun* and the *moon* are the *blessings* that the *one* *does not ask for*, and the *one* *does not ask for* because the *one* *takes them for granted*. The *logic* of the *reading* is the *logic* of the *extension*: the *giving* is *extended* beyond the *one* who *asks*, and the *extension* is the *condition* of the *totality*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *giving* is thus: the *giving* is *extended* beyond the *one* who *asks*, and the *extension* is the *condition* of the *totality*.

**"Were You to Count the Blessings of God, You Could Not Number Them": The Impossibility of the Count**

The *next* clause — *in taʿuddū niʿmata llāhi lā tuḥṣūhā* — "and were you to count the Blessings of God, you could not number them" — is the *statement* of the *impossibility* of the *count*. The *word* *tuḥṣūhā* — "you could not number them" — is the *word* of the *impossibility*, the *word* of the *one* who *cannot* *complete* the *count*. The *blessings* are *too many* to *count*, the *blessings* are *beyond* the *measure* of the *count*. The *commentators* (al-Bayḵāwī) add the *point* that *not only are people unable to count the Blessings of God, they are also unable to thank Him for all of them* (see also 16:18). The *logic* of the *clause* is the *logic* of the *impossibility*: the *count* is *impossible*, and the *impossibility* is the *condition* of the *gratitude*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *count* is thus: the *count* is *impossible*, and the *impossibility* is the *condition* of the *gratitude*.

**The Prophetic Confirmation: The Three Ways of the Granting**

The *Prophet* ﷺ is *reported* to have said of the *granting* of the *requests*:

> "Either his request will be granted to him [in this world], or God will store it away for him for the Hereafter, or He will divert from him an evil equivalent to the request." (Recorded by al-Nasāʾī 1305; Ibn Mājah 3886, from Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī and Abū Hurayrah)

The *report* *confirms* the *principle*: the *request* is *granted* in *one* of *three ways*: the *granting* in *this world*, the *storing away* for the *Hereafter*, or the *diverting* of an *evil*. The *three* *ways* together state the *full scope of the granting*: the *granting* is *not limited* to the *one* who *asks in this world*, and the *granting* is *extended* to the *Hereafter* and the *diverting* of the *evil*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *granting* is thus: the *granting* is *total* in its *scope*, and the *totality* is the *condition* of the *gratitude*.

**"Truly Mankind is Wrongdoing, Ungrateful": The Diagnosis**

The *final* clause — *innā l-insāna la-ẓālimun kaffār* — "Truly mankind is wrongdoing, ungrateful" — is the *diagnosis*. The *word* *ẓālimun* — "wrongdoing" — is the *word* of the *one* who *puts* the *thing* in the *wrong place*, the *word* of the *one* who *injures* himself. The *word* *kaffār* — "ungrateful" — is the *word* of the *one* who *covers* the *blessing*, the *word* of the *one* who *hides* the *Giver* in the *blessing* (the *kufr* of v. 7). The *two* words together state the *full scope of the diagnosis*: the *one* is *wrongdoing* (the *one* who *injures* himself) and *ungrateful* (the *one* who *covers* the *blessing*). The *parallel* at 33:72 complements the *diagnosis*:

> "Truly he has proved himself a lightbearer and ungrateful." (33:72, paraphrased)

The *logic* of the *clause* is the *logic* of the *diagnosis*: the *one* is *wrongdoing* and *ungrateful*, and the *two* together are the *condition* of the *diagnosis*. The *sūrah's* teaching on the *nature* of the *diagnosis* is thus: the *diagnosis* is the *condition* of the *gratitude*, and the *gratitude* is the *response* to the *diagnosis*.

**The Logic: The Giving, the Count, and the Diagnosis**

The *logic* of the *verse*, stated in its *full* form, is a *statement* about the *three* *aspects* of the *provision*. The *first aspect* is the *giving*: *God gives* of *all* that the *one* *asks*. The *second aspect* is the *count*: the *blessings* are *too many* to *count*. The *third aspect* is the *diagnosis*: the *one* is *wrongdoing* and *ungrateful*. The *three* *aspects* together state the *full scope of the provision*: the *giving* is *total* (the *first*), the *count* is *impossible* (the *second*), and the *diagnosis* is the *ingratitude* (the *third*).
